# Aligning Peru’s Transport Sector Development with 

# Climate Action : A b ackground note for the Peru CCDR 

Irene Portabales Gonzalez, Xavier Espinet Alegre, Dami an Pedro Saint Martin Zimmerspitz , Lorena 

Sierra Valdivieso , Jairo Quiroz Tortos, Luis Victor Gallardo, Luis Gil, Carla Pajares and Nicolas Marin 

Hurtado 5  

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

# 1. Sectoral context 

Peru’s economic growth is not followed by an increase in its infrastructure investment. Between 2008 

and 2018, the average gross domestic product ( GDP ) growth rate was 4.4 percent, while the poverty 

rate decreased substantially from 37.2 to 20.5 percent. D espite this remarkable economic growth, Peru 

still faces essential gaps in the quantity and quality of its infrastructure, which constitute a bottleneck 

to its competitiveness and shared prosperity goals . Inequality remains a significant challenge and 

thre atens the objective of social ly inclusive development. According to 2016 estimations, the national 

transportation infrastructure gap exceeded US$50 billion (AFIN 2016 ). In 2019, the Global 

Competitiveness Report ranked Peru 97 th out of 141 countries in terms of transportation infrastructure 

(WEF 2019) . These inequities also have significant implications regarding resilience to climate change 

and hamper Peru’s efforts to meet its development goals and emissions reduction commitments under 

the Paris Agreement. 

Peru’s total greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions do not have a significant global impact, but its location 

and geographic characteristics make it a vulnerable country to climate change. Peru has low per capita 

and total emissions, with a globa l share of emissions of only 0.3 percent. However, it has seven out of 

the nine 1 characteristics recognized by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change 

as those of a “particularly vulnerable” country. These features are intensified by ant hropogenic 

processes that cause the degradation of ecosystems and environmental pollution. Additionally, Peru is 

in the Pacific “Ring of Fire ,” where 90 percent of the world’s earthquakes occur. The impacts of these 

natural events are severe in Peru due to its complex geography and lack of investments in infrastructure 

resilience , and they have been exacerbated by climate change. 

Peru’s geography and spatial development patterns leave its transport infrastructure very exposed and 

vulnerable to natural hazar ds . Extreme temperatures, floods, and landslides triggered by heavy rains 

sometimes lead to closures of essential roads. Thus, investing in resilient infrastructure that is ready to 

respond to disaster risk events is essential for the country . During the 2 003 –19 period, Peru was 

affected by 61,708 emergencies caused by natural phenomena (intense rains, floods, droughts, 

earthquakes, and landslides). For example, in the El Niño Costero floods of 2017 (INDECI 2017 ), 46 

percent of the road network and 759 brid ges were damaged, and 18 percent of the road network and 

449 bridges were destroyed. Damages were even higher in the rural roads network. National annual 

losses from this natural disaster are estimated at US $4 billion. Transport infrastructure is significa ntly 

exposed to disasters, and with climate change becoming more intense, vulnerability increases. Recent 

natural phenomena —such as El Niño —have shown that insufficient preparation and response capacity 

at the subnational level are still issue s as well .

Peru has one of the most inequal distributions of infrastructure in the region, with a stark difference in 

connectivity between Lima and secondary cities or regions where the production of exported goods 

(mining and agriculture) is high (Briceño -Garmendia , Moroz, and Muente 2015). Peru ’s good 

connectivity, population, and economic activity are geographically concentrated around Lima and other 

coastal urban centers. However, secondary cities and regions that host a large share of the country’s 

production of exported good s—in the mining and agriculture sector s, for example, which represent 13 

and 6 percent of GDP, respectively —have some of the lowest connectivity in the country . This in turn 

result s in high transport cost s to reach international markets. Low rates of connectivity represent a      

> 1Characteristics of Peru recognized by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change: Low coastal zones; arid and semi -arid zones;
> areas exposed to floods, droughts, and desertification; fragile mountain ecosystems; disaster -prone areas; areas with high urban air pollution; and
> economies heavily dependent on income generated by fossil fuel production and use. 6
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

development barrier for most secondary cities , where transport costs to reach international markets 

are 50 percent higher than in Lima. In Amazonian regions, these costs could be 90 percent higher .

Figure 1: Peru ’s connectivity 

> Source: Briceño -Garmendia, Moroz, and Rozenberg 2015

The transport sector is the fastest -growing sector in terms of emissions due to the current trends of 

motorization: 63 percent growth in the last decade and 20 percent growth in emissions since 2014 .

Peru’s primary sources of emissions are land -use change and forestry (48 percent), energy (29 

percent), and agriculture (11 percent). The main contributors of the energy sector are transport (10.25 

percent of total emissions nationwide) and ele ctricity/heat production (6 percent of total emissions). 

Regarding transport subsectors, 92 percent of emissions came from road transport, 5 percent from civil 

aviation, 2 percent from sea transport, 1 percent from rail transport, and 0.1 percent from othe rs. Within 

road transport, emissions are mostly generated by light -duty trucks, heavy -duty trucks, and buses.

Emissions from this sector are caused by the mobile combustion energy (gasoline) used in various 

modes of transportation for both cargo and passengers at the national level. Thus, GHG emissions are 

intrinsically related to transportation, and to the country’s overall economic growth .7  

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Figure 2: GHG emissions by modes of transport     

> Source: INFOCARBONO -RAGEI 2016 –MINAM, 2019 estimations by the World Bank with official data of the vehicle fleet and fuel consumption in 2019

The remarkable economic growth that Peru has experienced in recent decades has notably boosted 

the demand for freight transport in the country, mostly on road s. Decarboniz ing the freight sector poses 

a major challenge among efforts to reduce GHG emiss ions by 2030. According to estimations, between 

2011 and 2018, the total tons of cargo being transported across Peru increased from 72 million to 

109 million metric tons (M t), or 50 percent over just 7 years. This growth is directly reflected in national 

exports, which grew by 50 percent in the same period. Of the total cargo transported at the national 

level, 88.6 percent was by road, 10.9 percent by rail, and the rest by sea (cabotage) and air. Of t he 

three, road transport represents 92 percent of GHG emissions (21.05 metric tons of carbon dioxide 

equivalent, MtCO 2eq) (MINAM 2016) .

Figure 3: Evolution of GDP, total exports , and M t mobilized 

> Source: MTC

The 2011 Transport Logistics Services Development Plan determined Peru ’s 57 most essential logistics 

supply chains to focus public investments and highway concessions on promoting construction and 

maintenance. The Ministry of Transport and Communication’s ( MTC ’s ) sectoral strategy has focused on 

responding to road transport needs through the modernization of its national and departmental road 

network , facilitating the transport of goods from the interior (mainly agricultural produce and mine rals) 

to the export ports located along the coast. This priority in developing road transport also responds to 

the cost of road projects compared to other means of transport. For example, while improving 1

kilometer (k m) of highway may cost US$1.5 million, a rail kilometer may cost US$12.8 million, or the 

simple construction or modernization of a port (to promote cabotage) may involve an investment of 

$155 million. But it is important to note that railway and waterway infrastructure could be 10 times 

mo re cost -effective than roads in terms of cost/ton -km, for certain distances and type of cargo. Thus ,8  

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

the MTC is aligning its portfolio of projects with the National Railway Plan and the concession of ports 

in the country. 

Freight transport is a big contributor to GHG emission s. Many of these emissions are generated in urban 

areas during last -mile transport. The 78 percent of Peru’s population lives in major urban centers and 

are exposed to this air pollution. Moreover, these areas are characterized by congestio n and 

inequalities while they continue to agglomerate population and economic activity. Thus, cities are an 

increasingly critical frontier for climate action. The rate of mass urban transport kilometers per million 

citizens is at 3.9 in Peru, three times l ower than the regional average. Cars, taxis, and low -occupancy 

transit units (including ride hailing and other newer service models) are carbon inefficient per 

passenger -km served. Managing motorization and encouraging the use of much more carbon -efficient 

modes, such as mass transport systems complemented by nonmotorized transport (NMT) modes, is 

fundamental for a low -carbon development trajectory while supporting sustainable development goals 

for livable cities, social inclusion, clean air, and road safet y. Efficient and reliable urban mobility systems 

are also critical enablers of economic activity and labor markets in cities (Bertaud 2018). Public 

transport is particularly relevant for helping lower -income people access opportunities, generate 

income, an d obtain education. Investing in this integrated infrastructure is how cities will be able to 

continue to improve accessibility, while decoupling growth in travel demand from growth in GHG 

emissions. 

Development of urban transport systems, including mass transit and NMT, has not followed Peru’s high 

urbanization and internal migration rates toward urban centers. About 78 percent of the total 

population (32 million) lives in urban areas, with nearly 30 percent located in the Lima Metropolitan 

Area (LMA) (10.28 million in 2019). Since the 1950s, LMA has attracted most of the migration. A recent 

wave of migration brought many Venezuelan immigrants to Peru, 500,000 of whom settled in LMA. 

Almost 73 percent of LMA’s population commutes by public t ransport, amounting to 12.1 million daily 

trips. The system includes 560 routes operated by some 32 ,000 buses, microbuses, and vans. In terms 

of modal sha re (2022 Lima Como Vamos ), 20.9 percent of the population travel by minibus (combis) or 

vans (coasters ), 18.4 percent walk, 14 percent use the bus, 11.7 percent use their own cars, and 6.2 

percent travels by bicycle. There is also a limited mass transit network: bus rapid transit (BRT) and 

metro line 1 in Lima (ML1) account for 3.2 and 2.3 percent, respect ively, of the modal share. The Lima 

BRT, called Metropolitano, and ML 1 carry about 1 million trips per day (9 percent of all trips). The 

Metropolitano BRT line began commercial operations in 2011 with a 26 -km north -south corridor 

crossing the historic cen ter of Lima and covering 12 municipalities. ML 1, a 34.6 -km, north -south 

elevated rail transit line (on a different alignment than the Metropolitano BRT) began commercial 

operations in 2010. ML 2 is the first underground metro line in the country, which is currently under 

construction. In Lima and Callao, nearly 91 percent of the commutes do not have access to safe, clean, 

and affordable public transport services. The public transport context is different in the country’s main 

intermediary cities —Cusco, Are quipa, Turjillo, Piura, and Ica. Mobility is similar in the five cities, where 

the use of minibuses or vans and walking is the main means of transport while cycling is almost 

nonexistent. In Cusco, for example, about 30 percent of the population walks, whi le less than 1 percent 

commutes by bicycle. Another critical feature is the accelerated and unplanned pace of urbanization of 

Peruvian cities. Almost half of these cities’ population (45.5 percent) lives in slums or inadequate 

housing and commute to urban centers for work, services, or education. Last -mile connectivity means 

longer travel times, different modes, and higher costs for the low -income inhabitants. For example, the 

average time it takes a person from the north of Lima to reach the center of the capital is 60 minutes .

Informal settlements continue to expand to peripheral regions that are prone to climate hazards, 

increasing the pressure on adequate public service provision and accessibility to goods, services, and 

jobs (Instituto Nacional de Estad ística y Censos 2017). Promoting efficient, affordable, and reliable 9  

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

public transport system, including investments in mass transit systems and NMT infrastructure will 

reduce travel times, improve air quality, road safety , and access to economic opportunit ies. 

Considering the sectoral context described above, there are three main axes to improve efficiency, 

reduce transportation costs, reduce GHG emissions in freight and urban transport, and promote 

resilient transport infrastructure. For freight transport, it is necessary to develop modes of 

transportation that increase efficiency and reduce emissions, such as rail and cabotage, as well as 

increase road transportation energy efficiency. In the urban context, it is crucial to reduce the air 

pollution and con gestion to which the population is exposed and its negative consequences on health 

and the environment. To this end, it is necessary to invest in mass transport systems complemented 

by NMT modes and clean last -mile logistics systems. Then, regarding its ex position to natural hazards, 

develop an infrastructure resilient to the natural disasters the country is exposed to. 10   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

# 2. Government p riorities for the transport sector with 

# regard to climate change 

The Government of Peru (GoP), with the approval of the Law of Climate Change (LMCC) 2 in April 2018, 

created the permanent Multisectoral Working Group (GTM -NDC) and established ambitious targets to 

fight against climate change. MTC participates in the GTM -NDC, promoting GHG emissions reduction 

from the transpo rt sector. The LMCC was followed by different regulatory and institutional efforts 

regarding the national management reduction of GHG emissions, including the Intended Nationally 

Determined Contributions (iNDCs). In 2015, the Multisectoral Commission prese nted the technical 

report, which determined that Peru ’s projected contributions would have a 30 percent reduction in 

relation to previous emissions projected for the year 2030. The government’s main objective is to not 

exceed its net GHG emissions of over 208.8 MtCO 2eq in 2030. Additionally, the GoP considers that 

GHG emissions could be reduced to 179.0 MtCO 2eq, depending on the international external financing 

and the existence of favorable conditions. 3

Figure 4: National emissions by year (thousand GgCO 2eq): business -as -usual projected emissions and 

GoP target  

> Source: INFOCARBONO –MINAM. *NDCs updated as of 2021
> Note: Business -as -usual projected emissions are in red; GoP target in green.

In the transport sector, the GoP aims to ensure the provision of transportation and communications 

infrastructure and services to the entire population in a safe, sustainable, inclusive, and competitive 

and sustainable way, oriented to national integration and development (MTC 2021). In 2019, MTC 

started a reorganization process that finished with the approval of the Integrated Text of the Regulation 

of Organization and Functions to respond more efficiently to the sector’s needs. It created the General 

Dire ctorate of Environmental Affairs, as the focal point for climate change, to monitor the 

implementation of the sector’s National Determined Contributions (NDC). To meet the commitments of 

the NDCs adjusted to 2021, the GoP has determined a total of 62 mitig ation measures, 9 of them 

under the responsibility of MTC, and 5 additional ones under the responsibility of the Ministry of Energy 

and Mines (MINEM) on electric mobility, 4 totaling 14 related to the transport sector. All these 62 

mitigation measures will entail 69.4 MtCO 2eq of reduction in GHG emissions in relation to the business -

as -usual ( BAU ) 2030 scenario (298.3 MtCO 2eq). However, according to the new NDC country target,

> Peruano 2018).

recorded reductions should reach 119 MtCO2. Thus, mitigation measures are still i nsufficient and 

currently there is an emissions gap of 49.9 MtCO 2eq for all the sectors. 

For the transport sector, MTC proposes nine measures framed by three development challenges: 

sustainable transport, improvement of railway infrastructure , and energy e fficiency in transport . These 

measures are expected to reduce emissions by 1.08 MtCO 2eq. Also, MINEM proposes five mitigation 

measures for the sector framed by the energy efficiency development challenge, which will reduce 

emissions by 5.85 MtC0 2eq by 2030. To close the existing 49.9 MtCO 2eq gap and considering that by 

2030 the transport sector will maintain its 10 percent share of total emissions at the nationa l level, it 

should reduce its emissions by 12.23 MtCO 2 in 2030. Thus, there is still a gap of 5.30 MtCO2eq to be 

identified.

# 3. Policy proposals analysis 

The NDCs identify the transport sector’s responsibility as reducing 6.93 MtCO2eq, while this should be 

closer to 12.23 MtCO2eq to meet overall 2030 targets. Considering that for the 2030 NDC targets, the 

emission s contribution of each se ctor remains constant (29 percent for energy and 10.25 percent for 

the transport sector), the energy sector should reach a maximum level of 50.1 MtCO 2eq and therefore, 

the transport sector a level not higher than 17.9 MtCO 2eq. That would imply a reduction by 2030 in the 

transport sector of 12.23 MtCO 2eq with respect to BAU. However, the contributions proposed for 2030 

include 14 mitigation measures that cumulatively reach a total of 6.93 MtCO 2eq .

Considering that 92 percent of transport emissions are produced by land transport, policy proposals 

should focus on this mode. Based on data from the Ministry of the Environment ( MINAM ), achieving the 

NDCs would mean a reduction of 1.04 MtCO 2 for motorcycles, 0.36 MtCO 2 for buses, 1.09 MtCO 2 for 

cargo tra nsport, 1.60 MtCO 2 for light vehicles, and 1.21 MtCO 2 for automobiles .

Figure 5: Share of transport emission s reduction required by vehicle type to achieve NDC s

> Source: Original elaboration

To fill the gaps and complement the mitigation measures committed to by the ministry, this report 

evaluates specific policy proposals along two axes: 

• Freight transport: Three policies will be analyzed to reduce emissions, mainly from heavy -duty 

trucks and buses, representing 27.4 percent of land transport emissions. 

• Urban mobility and logistics: Two policies will be analyzed that promote mass transport systems 

and NMT, and one policy will be analyzed related to last -mile logistics, with an impact mainly 

on light -duty truck emissions. The ben efits of traffic management will also be discussed. 

## 3.1. Freight transport 

Different combinations of public policy, infrastructure interventions, and behavioral dynamics (among 

end users and service providers in the freight transport sector ) can lead to several possible trajectories 

for Peru’s short - and medium -term transport decarbonization pathways to close the emission s

reduction gap by 2030. To analyze the potential impacts, three scenarios were conceptualized: (i) BAU, 13   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

reflecting economic growth but without additional explicit climate change policies; (ii) a scenario in 

which several key policies are implemented (e.g., an extension of the scrapping program proposed by 

MTC, the promotion of truck centers , and the promotion of intermodal exchange from land car go to 

cabotage ), framed by the three development challenges established by the MTC; (iii) the NDC scenario, 

which contemplates a reduction objective of 12.23 MtCO 2eq to 2030 for the transport sector, from 

which 6.93 MtCO 2eq come from the nine MTC and the five MINEM mitigation measures, and the 

remaining gap would be closed by the policy proposals. As a result, GHG emissions could be reduced 

by between 0.9 MtCO 2 and 2.6 MtCO 2 by 2030, in comparison to the BAU scenario; and by between 

5.92 MtCO2eq and 7.05 MtCO2eq by 2050. 

The emissions reduction estimations obtained above were made in accordance with the 

Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change guidelines, based on the amoun t of fuel burned that is 

avoided. Each type of fuel has an associated emission s factor, in accordance with its carbon content. 

So, it is necessary to know the number of kilometers avoided per year and also the associated level of 

performance (kilometers pe r gallon, associated with the type of fuel, type of vehicle , and fleet age). 

Although MTC has information on the age of cargo vehicles, o ne of the main limitations of the proposed 

methodology is that the Supervisory Agency for Investment in Energy and Mini ng (OSINERGMIN) only 

reports total fuel consumption but does not relate it to the type of vehicle or whether it is associated 

with cargo or passenger transport. 

Figure 6: Methodological process for estimating the GHG emissions reduction s expected under th e

proposed freight transport policies 

> Source: Original elaboration

Promoting the development of 10 cargo service centers or “truck centers ” in key strategic points of the 

logistics network could avoid extra tours of trucks and save between 0.11 MtCO 2eq and 0.57 MtCO 2eq 

of emissions by 2030, and between 0.15 MtCO 2eq and 0.75 MtCO2eq by 2050. In the last decade, 

MTC has prioritized the developme nt of road infrastructure for the national and subnational network, 

and to date progress has been important: 83 percent of the national road network is paved. The first 

proposed policy aims to increase efficiency in the distribution of goods by road, consi dering that in the 

past years this option has been a priority for the government and a focal intervention point. The 

feasibility study of a National Network of Carrier Service Centers —Truck Centers ( MTC 2013) indicates 

that the supply of cargo services is developed in a scattered fashion along the road network, without 

prior planning, and meets sector demand on an ad hoc basis. 

The high logistics costs of cargo transport include not only how to guarantee the good condition of 

roads, but also cargo’s efficie nt distribution across strategic points along the route. Through the 

services offered by t ruck centers , transport companies could reduce their kilometers traveled per 

vehicle unit , better distribut e goods, and avoid 5 extra tours, with an impact on GHG emissions. For this 

analysis, t he following 10 zones were considered for the implementation of truck centers: North Lima, 

South Lima, Arequipa, Truillo, Chiclayo, Huarmey, Chala, Piura, Ica , and Desaguadero. Increasing    

> 5Framed in the avoid -shift -improve approach to sustainability. 14
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

logistics efficiency will affect the number of kilometers annually traveled per truck. To analyze this 

policy, three scenarios were considered . As explained in paragraph 14 , the GHG emissions reduction 

of this policy is analyzed throw the fuel saves related to the extra -detours avoided. According to the 

prefeasibility stud y and interviews with specialist s from the trade union, a truck reduces its distance 

traveled by 85 km for each trip made, 60 km in a conservative scenario , and 20 km in a pessimistic 

scena rio with the implementation of this policy. Annually, it means that this policy reduces the average 

distance travelled per vehicle to 34 ,000, 32 ,000 , and 30 ,000 km , respectively. Therefore, the 

implementation of the cargo service centers by 2030 could redu ce GHG emissions in relation to B AU

between 0.11 MtCO 2eq and 0.57 MtCO 2eq, and by 2050, this could be between 0.15 MtCO 2eq and 

0.75 MtCO 2eq.

Expanding the scrapping program proposed by MTC to reduce vehicles more than 15 years old will 

implicate extra savings of 0.18 MtCO2eq by 2030 and 0.50 by 2050. Several analytical studies 

demonstrate a relationship between the age of vehicles a nd fuel consumption performance (AISBL 

2018). Older vehicles ’ fuel consumption increases for each kilometer traveled. According to MTC, in 

2015, of the entire cargo transport fleet, 39 percent was more than 10 years old, and 25 percent more 

than 15 years o ld. In many cases, these vehicles do not receive adequate maintenance 6 (which affects 

fuel performance) and do not have the mandatory technical inspection s. 7 Additionally, according to the 

RAGEI (2016 ) of mobile combustion , 54 percent of total fuel consumption at the national level (48,034 

thousand barrels) is of diesel, which is used almost exclusively by cargo transport vehicles .

One of the main problems of cargo transport is the high level of atomization. Ninety -two percent of 

the total fleet belong s to companies with a maximum of 4 trucks, while only 2 percent of companies 

operate a fleet with more than 10 vehicles. Likewise, the number of companies with more than 50 

trucks represent barely 0.3 percent of the sector, and in relation to the number of vehicles, 50 

percent of the sector is made up of individual carriers. This atomization represents a barrier for

better corporate practices for fleet renewal , economies of scale , and operation and maintenance

Encouraging the renewal of the cargo transport fleet through economic incentives , scrapping 

program s for older vehicles , and financing for the purchase of vehicles by medium and small 

carriers would reduce diesel fuel consumption and, consequently, GHG emission s. The MTC 

proposes , in the NDC mitigation measures , a scrapping program for vehicles that by law can no 

longer circulate, for those that are obsolete, and for public transportation units ; by 2030, the se 

could be reduced by 50, 70, and 100 percent , respectively. As a result of fleet renewal and lower 

fuel consumption, by 2030 , emissions would be reduced by 0.18 MtCO 2eq.

Peruvian geographical conditions (including more than 2,400 km of coastline between Tacna and 

Tumbes) open the door to the intermodal exchange of land cargo to cabotage. This could result in 

sav ing s of 0.05 MtCO 2eq by 2030 if 60 percent of the total tons c urrently transported by the Pan -

American Highway are transported by cabotage, and of 0.13 MtC0 2eq by 2050 . Several studies indicate 

that cabotage is more cost -efficient than ground transportation ;8 perhaps one of the greatest benefits 

of maritime cargo transportation is that it is a viable alternative to quickly connect the different regions

of the country when road infrastructure services (national or departmental) are interrupted due to 

events suc h as natural disasters and blockades, among others. For this reason, the promotion of 

maritime cabotage is of vital importance for the economic development and commercial security of the 

country. 

Since 2018, the government has been making efforts to establ ish a regulatory framework to facilitate 

this means of transportation. Today’s regulation allows national and foreign flag shipping companies 

to transport cargo. Despite these recent efforts toward a more flexible regulatory framework, cabotage 

cargo conti nues to register small volumes compared to the potential of road cargo that can be captured 

along the Peruvian coastal ports. To estimate its potential, logistic corridors between main regional 

ports that are at least 500 km from the Callao port have been considered. Taking this into 

consideration, four ports are appropriate places to focus on promot ing cabotage: the Port of Paita, Port 

of Salaverry, Port of Matarani and Por of Ilo. By 2030, the potential demand that can be promoted by 

cabotage through thes e ports could reach a maximum of 21.9 million M t, representing 14 percent of 

Peru’ nonmineral national cargo. 

To estimate GHG emissions reduction considering this policy, three scenarios were considered, which 

represent a capture of 20, 40 , and 60 percent , respectively , of the total tons that are currently 

transported by the Pan -American Highway and have been defined as the potential demand to be 

replaced.

Due to its greater capacity, the railway is an efficient mode for the transport of goods over long 

distances, although given its high cost of implementation it may not be the most cost -efficient solution 

in the medium term from the GHG reduction perspective. Currently, the railway network in Peru has a 

length of 1,940 km, of which 77 percent is under concession, 13 percent is private (mineral transfer) ,

and t he remaining 10 percent is under the responsibility of MTC. The railway infrastructure in Peru is 

underdeveloped compared to peer countries in the region such as Chile, Bolivia, or Colombia. Since 

2016, there has been no growth in the tonnage transported by rail, despite the growth in the amount 

of cargo transporte d in the country. Large investments in rail infrastructure are being prioritized (Axe 

2), despite high costs and a low cost -benefit ratio in terms of GHG emissions reduction. Because of 

rail’s tremendous impact on a country ’s economy and other benefits and externalities in the long term ,

railway infrastructure should be developed, but it is probably not the most cost -effective solution for 

GHG emissions reduction in the medium term (2030 –50). In addition to the rail infrastructure 

prioritized in the NDCs, t here are other sections considered in the National Railway Plan (see box 1). 18   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

## 3.2. Urban mobility and logistics 

Developing efficient and reliable urban mobility systems is necessary to guarantee sustainable cities 

and emphasize their role as the primary access to job and educational opportunities. From the nine 

mitigation measures proposed by MTC, four are related t o urban areas, framed in the first development 

objective, sustainable transport. The current World Bank operation (under implementation) of the 

Metropolitano BRT North Extension, the implementation of LMA ML 1 extension and future line ML 2, 

Box 1: Marcona –Andahuaylas train 

The National Railway Plan approved in 2016 includes the promotion of three new railway lines : The 

Huancayo -Huancavelica train, the Lima -Ica commuter train system, and t he San Juan de Marcona –

Andahuaylas train . Due to the efficiency of the rail mode, it is important to analyze the impact of its 

implementation. Thus, and considering that pre -feasibility studies has already be done, the San Juan de 

Marcona – Andahuaylas trains has been analyzed. This route runs through a mining and agricultural area ,

with potential cargo of around 180,000 tons per day —the equivalent of 5,000 truck trips . The project 

includes a 920 kilometer (km) long rail road, and its investment is estimated at US$9.2 billion. The 

passenger and cargo demand analysis for the project indicates that 900 passengers can be transported 

per day, mainly on the Pisco -Ayacucho route. Likewise, it is estimated that cargo transport co uld be around 

66 million M t per year, of which the majority would be mining products since the route is near various 

mining projects in the department of Apurimac .

According to the preinvestment study, an average truck has the capacity to transport 36 Mt of cargo, and 

based on the calculated distances that trucks travel by road according to the “origin -destination” points of 

the train stations, it is projected that the total number of kilometers to be stopped running by the replaced 

fleet would reach 976.1 thousand km per year. As a result of this modal exchange (scenarios for 100 

percent , 80 percent, and 60 percent modal shift) , considering the fuel saved, the reduction of GHG 

emissions in relation to the business -as -usual scenario 1 ranges between 0. 22 metric tons of carbon dioxide 

(MtCO 2) and 0.37 MtCO 2 for 2030 .

Figure 12 : Reduction of GHG s due to operation of the San Juan de Marcona –Andahuaylas railway    

> Source: O riginal elaboration 19
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

and the implem entation of different complementary corridors in the main Peruvian urban centers 

through the National Program for Sustainable Urban Transportation are some of the proposed NDC 

measures under the scope of MTC. The provision of mass urban transport infrastru cture has 

demonstrated to be of high impact; for example, the 10.2 km extension to the north of the Metropolitano 

BRT in Lima is expected to save 0.33 MtCO 2eq (net emissions) between 2024 9 and 2040, although the 

total benefits of the project come mainly f rom time savings . NMT and mass transport systems have a 

great potential to complement each other , creating a more inclusive public network, increasing job 

accessibility and road safety, and reducing GHG emissions, noise pollution, and travel times. In this 

sense, four main lines of action are analyzed: (i) expand kilometers of BRT operation in Lima and five 

intermediate cities: Arequipa, Trujillo, Ica, Piura, and Cusco; (ii) promot e NMT in Lima and the main 

intermediate cities (Cusco, Ica, Arequipa, Trujill o, and Puno); (iii) implement traffic management 

solutions; and (iv) promot e cyclelogistics for urban freight and parcel delivery. 

Expanding the kilometers of BRT in operation in Peru ’s major cities could help to significantly reduce 

emissions, improve accessibility, and reduce fuel consumption. A 400 km BRT construction program 

could implicate savings of 0.22 MtCO 2eq by the year 2030 and savings of 0.69 MtCO 2eq by 2050. BRT 

systems hav e demonstrated themselves to be transport solution s that reduce vehicle kilometers 

traveled in a city by shifting commuters to high -capacity vehicles and reducing emissions from other 

more polluting transport modes (Cervero and Muramaki 2010) . In addition, BRT vehicles also 

incorporate modern fuel efficiency technologies, and natural gas ( liquefied natural gas ) in Peru, which 

contribute to reducing air pollution in the urban areas. Some scientific studies demonstrated that a 

combination of cycling and walki ng with an emphasis on a modal shift to BRT can achieve a reduction 

in GHG emissions of approximately 25 percent in cities (Wright and Fulton 2005) . Furthermore, BRTs 

have proven to be lower -cost systems to build than mass transit systems , such as metros and light rail 

transit , and therefore can be deployed more cost -effectively within a given budget (Vincent and Jerram 

2006) .

The transportation plans of six Peruvian cities, including Lima and Callao, aim to achieve a total of 130 

km of BRT operating by 20 30, which would mean annual savings of 0.22 MtCO 2eq of emissions. 

However, if 400 km of BRT were to operate, the emissions savings for Peru would be three times higher 

than estimated for 2030. The model developed for this study takes as a reference the emi ssion s

reduction achieved for the 10.2 km extension of the Lima BRT, which estimates 0.18 MtCO 2eq on 

average per year ( World Bank 2019 ). An estimated 400 BRT km , to operate in Peruvian cities by 2050 ,

is based on a case study of BRT systems in 13 different cities.

An efficient public transport network must include the development of NMT infrastructure for short 

distances and to increase access to mass public transport systems. The expansion of the NMT network 

in Lima, and its implementation in the main intermediate cities, could mean 0.27 MtCO2eq of 

emissions savings by 2030 and savings of 1.08 MtCO2eq by 2050. Cities that promote and enhance 

active mobility contribute to economic and social justice in their territories. Investing in NMT is a policy 

that responds to the needs and journeys of the most vulnerable. In addition to improving the urban 

experience of people that generally cannot afford motorized private transportation, active mobility 

projects can unlock the health, environmental, and economic benefits of lo w-carbon lifestyles among 

residents. Thus, NMT options (walking or cycling), as a complement or alternative to transit services, 

will be essential in designing a multimodal, environmentally sustainable, and inclusive urban transport 

system. Specific calcul ations for Lima show that 1 km traveled by private vehicle costs 1.37 Peruvian 

new soles (S/. ) to society, travel by public transport costs S/. 1.07, but travel by bike represents benefits 

to society of S/. 2.52 .10 

The World Bank, with Metropolitan Munici pality of Lima support, has elaborated a plan for the NMT 

infrastructure development in Lima and Callao and a strategy to promote its use (World Bank 2020 a, 

2020 b) . The NMT infrastructure plan considers a 1 ,383 km bike network for 2041, related to a fully 

reachable city with safe infrastructure. This allows going from 0.39 percent of the modal share for bikes 

in 2020 to 11.58 percent by 2050. A model developed to study this infrastructure plan estimates that 

in LMA, this project could reduce emissions by 0.64 MtCO 2eq for 2030 and by 1.03 MtCO 2eq for 2050. 

This measure could be extrapolated to the main intermediate cities of the country: Arequipa, Cusco, 

Ica, Puno, and Trujillo. Several studies have demonstrated that the percentage of bicycle trips betw een 

a 1- and 5 -minute interval is 15 percent, and for the 6 –10 minute interval it is 30 percent. This means 

that bikes are chosen mainly for short trips. 

Considering that the main intermediate cities are significantly smaller than Lima (see table 5), the 

potential modal share for a city fully covered by a bike network is higher.

The GHG emissions saved derive from the fuel savings related to the increase of the bike modal share 

to its potential maximum . For the motorized trips, the modal share remains constant compared to 

current modal shares. Thus, for a 2030 scenario, NMT policy would allow a 0. 27 MtCO 2eq reduction, 

and for 2050 a 1. 08 MtCO 2eq reduction. 

Traffic management policy to discourage the use of private vehicl es has proven effective in reduc ing 

emissions in major urban centers. In São Paulo, a city with similar characteristics to Lima, the 

implementation of these policies would imply a reduction of up to 5.97 MtCO 2eq by 2030. The 

International Energy Agency pro poses 10 actions that can be taken to reduce oil demand with 

immediate impact —and provides recommendations for how those actions can help pave the way to 

putting oil demand on to a more sustainable path in the longer term (IEA 2022 ). These measures 

implica te some change of habits in the population, as for example: working from home up to three days 

a week, using car -sharing programs, avoiding business travel by meeting virtually, and using high -speed 

and night trains instead of planes. Also, it requires a s et of polic ies , such as reduc ed speed limits on 

highways, car -free Sundays in large cities, micromobility and public transport, the efficient use of freight 

trucks , and electrification program s for vehicles, in addition to promot ing a change of habit. Immediate 

actions can cut oil demand by 2.7 million barrels a day in four months in advanced economies. 

In this vein, the world’s biggest cities have already started to adopt travel demand management (TDM), 

a set of measures that aims to reduce travel car dependence. The TDM measures are subdivided into 

soft and hard. Some examples of soft TDM measures are: personalized travel planning, promoting 

carpooling at workplace s and schools, and creating advertising campaigns for alternative tran sport 

modes and to increase knowledge about the impact of using private cars. The main hard TDM measures 

are: 

• Vehicle restrictions: These aim to improve air quality in main cities. They have been 

implemented in São Paulo, where circulation is forbidden for certain vehicle plate numbers 

during peak hours, depending on the day of the week. In Bogotá, a similar program has been 

implemented. But no reliable evidence on the effectiveness of the restriction has been found. 

In fact, in the Brazilian case, resident s have acquired a second vehicle with a different number 

plate. 

• Congestion charge: This measure involves a cordon pricing system whereby vehicles are 

charged on entering into a restricted zone. Singapore is a pioneer of this policy, while London 

is a succe ssful example: the city achieved a 20 percent reduction in traffic in the central zone, 

reduced emissions, and earned a net revenue of £173.5 million in 2011 (New York City Global 

Partners 2012), which must be invested in public transport by law. 22   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

• Low -emiss ion zones (LEZs): These are areas restricted to pollutant vehicles, and aim to improve 

air quality. London implemented this measure in 2006 to encourage the most polluting heavy 

diesel vehicles to become cleaner. Paris’s 2030 target is to allow only batter y-electric and 

hydrogen fuel cell vehicles in the central zone. In Brazil, the implementation of a LEZ has been 

analyzed. In São Paulo, it is estimated that this measure could have an impact of 5.97 MtCO 2eq 

annually. 

In Brazil, the implementation of LEZs i n São Paulo, Belo Horizonte, and Porto Alegre has been 

evaluated. Considering resident s’ willingness to accept a congestion charge in the central area s of 

these cities, surveys showed that 19.6 percent of the people would use a car with this measure 

implem ented. Banning cars and motorcycles manufactured before 2006 could reduce the fleet in 

circulation by 45 percent in Belo Horizonte, 36 percent in Porto Alegre, and 51 percent in São Paulo. 

In São Paulo, banning vehicles manufactured before 2006 from entering the proposed LEZ (city center) 

would avoid 5.97 MtCO 2eq of emissions annually. Also, supposing that 19.6 percent of car users pay a 

charge for using LEZ s, this policy implies benefits of US$120 million. These results are comparable to 

the potentia l measures ’ impact in Lima, given the similarities between the two cities, as seen in table 

6.

Currently, the Bank is coordinating with the GoP in the preparation of a new project with the objective 

of improving transit management and making urban transport efficient, safe, and clean in Lima, 

focusing on public transport and NMT . This project will focus on driving efficiency, road safety , and 

emissions reduction. 

Around 25 percent of urban transport GHG emissions are related to freight movement. With the 

emergence of new business models and the rise of e -commerce, c yclelogistics is an effective solution 

for achieving carbon neutrality in urban freight and parcel delivery. Urban logistics is a critical aspect of 

supply chains and urban mobility. While goods delivery is inherent to urban life, the associated negative 

externalities are partially responsible for the problems most often linked to cities, such as noise, 

congestion, pollution, and poor road safety. According to research in Europe, urban logistics represents 

between 8 to 18 percent of traffic flows (MDS Transmodal Limited 2012), using about 30 percen t of 

road capacity for pickup and delivery operations (Nocerino et al. 2016). In the case of Lima, congestion 

causes 76 percent of trip lengths to range from 30 minutes to more than an hour. Even in intermediate 

cities like Piura, 35 percent of trip length s range from 30 minutes to more than two hours (MTC 2019). 

Furthermore, trips related to urban logistics are responsible for at least 30 –50 percent of nitrogen 

oxides and particulate matter emissions (Cairns and Sloman 2019; Bundesregierung 2008), and 

tran sport of goods corresponds to a quarter of CO 2 emissions in urban areas (Dablanc and Rodriguez 

2017). These numbers could be even higher in developing countries due to the advanced age of many 23   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

vehicles; in the case of Peru , light commercial vehicles ( LCVs ) (usually related to urban freight) emit 

27.58 percent of total road transport emissions. 

The COVID -19 pandemic has increased the demand for urban freight and parcel delivery, new business 

models such as e -delivery together with technological transformatio ns in businesses and a rising 

middle class are expected to increase usage of delivery vehicles by 36 percent by 2030, resulting in a 

rise in GHG emissions (by 32 percent) and congestion levels (by 21 percent ) ( WEF 2020). E -delivery 

services in Peru amounte d to S/. 2.6 million in April 2021, doubling prepandemic levels. Although these 

services are concentrated in Lima (87.5 percent), other cities such as Arequipa (4.5 percent), Trujillo 

(3.4 percent), Chiclayo (1.4 percent), and Piura (1.3 percent) are also experiencing an emergence in e -

delivery (Diario Gestión 2021). Unsurprisingly, the negative externalities of urban logistics are expected 

to increase dramatically if there are no interventions by local, regional, and national governments. 

Cyclelogistics i s considered a tool for addressing the last -mile problem and the most negative 

externalities of urban logistics for several reasons. First, it does not directly emit pollutant gases and 

particulates into the atmosphere ;11 a study concluded that cars emit 1 1 times more pollutants than 

bikes to deliver the same food parcels (Allen et al. 2021). Second, cargo cycles make more efficient 

use of parking spaces (curbside or parking lots) compared to motorized vehicles. Third, according to 

the European Cyclist Fede ration, cargo bikes move at a speed comparable to that of motorized vehicles 

in city centers (14.4 kilometer s per hour [km/h] compared to 18 km/h, respectively). Moreover, they 

can be driven in pedestrian -friendly, low -emission areas and can deliver outsid e the time frames of 

other freight vehicles, as they usually escape the restrictions imposed on motorized vehicles within city 

centers. Fourth, cargo vehicles are more affordable than fossil fuel vehicles used for goods delivery, 

both in acquisition and ma intenance costs. 

It should be noted that cyclelogistics cannot replace all vehicles in urban logistics. The potential of 

cyclelogistics for improving urban logistics is related to the replacement of LCV s and motorcycles. Cargo 

bikes can carry a maximum of 200 –350 k ilo grams and 1.5 cubic meters of load, and trips do not 

normally exceed 7 –12 k m. They are particularly suitable for small, light consignments, which are 

currently increasing, especially for deliveries to private customers ( business to customer ). Up to 51 

percent of motorized trips for transporting goods in urban areas could be replaced by cyclelogistics 

according to data from the E uropean Union. 12 Moreover, according to research in the United Kingdom, 

cyclelogistics has the potential to replace perhaps 1.5 –7.5 percent of all urban vehicle mileage (Cairns 

and Sloman 2019). However, to achieve this, there are major challenges regarding legal and regulatory 

issues, infrastructure, stakeholders’ engagement, urban planning, data, safety, secu rity, gender, and 

labor conditions. 

An optimistic scenario, which includes policies related to incentives for the cargo bikes’ acquisition, 

and investments in needed infrastructure, considers that 25 percent of cargo motorcycle trips and 50 

percent of lig ht commercial truck trips could shift to be done by cargo bikes in LMA .13 Considering this, 

up to 3.6 MtCO 2eq could be saved due to urban freight transport in 2050 (0.8 MtCO 2eq in 2030).

## 3.3. Carbon -neutral scenario 

In 2021, the GoP started updating its 2015 National Strategy against climate change to include the net 

zero goal by 2050. A carbon -neutral scenario (deep decarbonization pathway, DDP) considers that by 

2050, emissions from the transport sector cou ld be reduced by 76 MtCO 2eq with respect to the BAU 

value by 2050, through aggressive vehicle electrification and new habits, such as densification of cities 

(transit -oriented development, TOD), 14 the digitalization of procedures, and the promotion of remo te 

work (Quirós -Tortós et al. 2021). The decarbonization scenario considers a modal shift from private 

transport to nonmotorized transport (pedestrian and bicycle use). It also considers the growing 

digitization of services and teleworking, which help redu ce passenger demand by a total of 30 percent 

by mid -century. Investments in infrastructure and the densification of cities enable a reduction in 

passenger demand by another 15 percent by 2050. Similarly, the scenario considers infrastructure 

investments an d logistics improvements that reduce freight demand by 20 percent by 2050. 

Considering an accelerated penetration of electrification in the medium term due to competitive prices 

of low -emission s vehicles, both in the private and public sector and in passen ger and freight transport, 

zero emissions would be achieved by 2050. However, this process would require strong coordination 

among public authorities and private actors due to the investment required, estimated at US$47 billion. 

These investments are relat ed to modernization of the transportation fleet, investment in infrastructure 

for both freight and passenger transport, and investment in electric technologies. This transition would 

bring long -term economic benefits in terms of O&M costs, greater producti vity due to decrease d

congestion, reduction in the number of accidents, and health benefits for the population.    

> 14 Transit -oriented development (TOD) is an urban m odel that seeks to build cities around public transport, in a shift away from the unsustainable growth
> of cities dependent on private cars. A menities are within walking distance of the primary means of public transport, promoting high density. 25
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Figure 1 3: Energy consumption in the transport sector, comparing B AU and DDP scenario to 2050 

> Source: Quirós -Tortós et al. 20 21
> .

Despite the great benefits that electromobility presents , including the reduction of GHG emissions, the 

promotion of initiatives and policies for the electrification of Peru’s automotive fleet has been very 

limited compared to other countries in the region. While countries such as Chile, Colombia, and Mexico, 

among others , began to adapt their regulatory framework to implement incentives for the acquisition, 

circulation, and operation of electric and hybrid vehicles more than a decade ago, in Peru in 2018, tax 

incentives were approved to increase the purchase and sale of c leaner energy vehicles. There were 

only 39 battery -powered electric vehicles in Peru as of 2020. However, according to the MTC ’s

commitment, it is expected that 6 ,700 buses and 171 ,300 light electric vehicles will start circulating in 

2030, which would rep resent 5 percent of the vehicle fleet at the national level. Thus, it is essential 

that in the coming years the MTC is able to promote a more competitive regulatory framework with clear 

incentives for the acquisition and operation of electric vehicles in t he country (AAP 2021) . The DDP 

reaches net zero emissions in 2050 thanks to t he electrification of the entire private, public, and cargo 

fleets. By 2030, 30 percent of the private and public fleet will be electrified with battery or hydrogen -

based vehicles (hydrogen is considered for buses). The electrification of freight transport with battery 

or hydrogen -based vehicles begins in 2030, as shown in figure 1 3.

Decarbonization of the transport sector benefits the GoP by reducing operating costs ( thanks to re duce 

fossil fuel purchases) and maintenance costs, health improvements due to lower combustion, 

increased productivity, and fewer accidents, all of which represent a total economic benefit of US$ 160 

billion by 2050. The transport sector model analyzed in the Inter -American Development Bank ’s

decarbonization report includes several benefits of decarbonization. The reduction in the cost of O&M 

of the vehicle fleet (mainly due to its electrification and energy efficiency that reduces consumption), 

the reducti on in health costs associated with respiratory issues generated by combustion, the increase 

in productivity due to less congestion (and therefore an increase in productive time), and a reduction 

in the cost related to road accidents due to a decrease in th e use of private transportation are the main 

factors associated with the defined benefits. The report also mentions certain benefits not included in 

the amount specified due to limitations with the available information. Supplementary benefits could 

exist due to an injection of energy into the grid from electric vehicles, reduced stress levels from quieter 

and less vibrating transport, and the health benefits of increased modal share from active mobility. 26   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

3.3.1. Fiscal impact of transport decarbonization 

The DDP’s costs and benefits evaluation shows that it requires several economic transformations to 

reduce emissions while maintaining economic growth. Examples of such transformations are deploying 

renewables in the electricity system, elec trifying end uses, promoting public transport, reverting 

deforestation, increasing reforestation, reducing waste, increasing efficiency, and changing 

consumption habits. The DDP envisions a more sustainable transport system by 2050. The passenger 

transport demand will be about 25 percent higher in 2050 than in 2022. In contrast, the BAU will have 

a demand 221 percent higher in 2050 than in 2022. Greater efficiency causes the demand difference 

between scenarios: higher levels of public transit will translate into comparatively fewer vehicles, and 

kilometers traveled in the DDP than in the BAU. Also, in the DDP, 31 percent of vehicles would be zero 

emission vehicles (ZEV s) by 2030, rising to 100 percent by 2050. The ZEVs are more energy efficient 

than internal combustion engine vehicles, reducing energy needs. In freight transport, the effect occurs 

after 2030, and the DDP has a demand closer to the BAU in 2050. The reduction is primarily related to 

improvement in logistics: the DDP considers full freight elect rification by 2050. The current fossil -fuel -

based system, reliant on diesel, gasohol, gasoline, natural gas, and liquified petroleum gas, will switch 

to electricity by 2050. 

From a fiscal perspective, the deployment of the DDP translates into reduced gover nment revenue from 

fossil fuels and internal combustion engine vehicles. Meanwhile, transport electrification may increase 

government revenue from the sales tax on electricity consumed for transportation and taxes on ZEVs, 

partially compensating for revenue lost. In 2019, total government revenue represented 16.6 percent 

of GD P. Transport -related revenue in the same year was estimated at 1.8 percent of GDP, revenues 

from fuel excises and gasoline sales tax represented about 0.5 percent of G DP, from import, excise, 

and property tax on vehicles was estimated at nearly 0.2 percent of G DP, and sales tax on vehicles 

almost 0.6 percent (SUNAT 2022) . The analyzed fiscal impact is related to the unrealized tax revenue 

resulting from the deployment of the DDP relative to the BAU scenario. However, the DDP involves a 

series of benefits th at will bring a positive financial impact. 

The fiscal impact would primarily occur in the long term when the electrification becomes predominant. 

Electrifying the fleet would lower the consumption of fossil fuels, thus, the revenue from fuel excise 

(Impues to Selectivo al Consumo, ISC ), gasoline sales tax ( Al rodaje ), and general sales tax ( Impuesto 

General a las Ventas ) on fuels. The fiscal impact of the fuel excise tax is limited in the mid term (less 

than -0.1 percent of GDP). In the long term, it increase s to -0.3 percent of GDP (yearly average) as fewer 

fossil fuels are consumed. Revenue from gasoline sales is currently low. Its fiscal impact is practically 

null in the mid term, and its yearly average in the long term accounts for -0.1 percent of GDP. The fiscal 

impact of general sales tax is the largest among fuel -related taxes. Its yearly average is -0.1 percent of 

GDP in the mid term and then increases to -0.4 percent of GDP in the long term. Vehicle -related fiscal 

impact (import, excise, general sales t ax, and property) would represent -0.2 percent of GDP and -0.5 

percent of GDP in the mid and long term, respectively . Import and property taxes are the lowest and 

together represent less than -0.1 percent in the mid and long term (yearly average). The fisc al impact 

of excise tax in the mid term accounts for nearly -0.1 percent of GDP, increasing to more than -0.1 

percent of GDP (yearly average). The fiscal impact of general sales tax is the largest among vehicle -

related taxes. Its yearly average in the mid term represents -0.2 percent of GDP, which then doubles in 

the long term (see figure 1 4a). 

Revenue loss may be partially compensated with increased revenue from electricity for transport. The 

deployment of ZEVs would raise electricity demand; thus, governmen t revenue from electricity general 

sales tax. While its fiscal impact is practically null in the midterm, as electrification is limited in this 

period, in the long term, the yearly average fiscal impact accounts for +0.3 percent of GDP. The 

decarbonization of the transport sector would bring a positive financial impact (a net benefit) that is 27   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

larger than the fiscal impact. Transforming the transport sector from a fossil -based to a ZEV one 

requires higher capital investments (electric buses an d hydrogen trucks). However, using ZEVs reduces 

operational costs as they are cheaper to maintain and operate over time. In the mid term, additional 

yearly average investments are estimated at -0.5 percent of GDP. These are compensated with reduced 

O&M cost s from ZEVs (+1.1 percent of GDP), which results in a yearly average net benefit in the mid term 

of +0.6 percent of GDP. In the long term, yearly average investments required under the DDP relative 

to the BAU scenario increase to -4.0 percent of GDP. These investments are overcompensated by 

significantly reduced O&M costs (+8.5 percent of GDP), resulting in a yearly average net financial 

benefit of +4.5 percent of GDP. Over the entire analysis period (2022 –50), the yearly average net 

financial benefit repres ents +3.3 percent of GDP ( see figure 1 4c ). 

Figure 1 4: a) Tax revenue per tax and period as a percentage of GDP , b) fiscal impact per tax and period 

as a percentage of GDP , and c) financial impact per period as a percentage of G DP

> Source: Original elabor ation
> Note: The horizontal red line represents net values .

## 3.4. Climate adaptation 

Transport infrastructure in Peru is highly exposed to natural hazard s with 13 percent of the population 

at risk of becoming isolated due to flooding or landslide impacts. Approxi mately US$95 billion dollars 

of transport asset value are exposed to natural hazards, including roads and bridges. Transport 

infrastructure is critical for climate resilience as it allows access to critical services, the movement of 

aid , and emergency response. Flooding and landslide events can impact transport infrastructure ,

leaving thousands of rural households isolated and without access to the primary transport network. In 

fact, it is estimated that approximately 13 percent of the po pulation in Peru is at risk of becoming 

isolated due to landslide disruption (World Bank 2021) . These risks are concentrated in the districts of 

Junín, Cusco, Cajamarca, and Huancavelica. 

Expected annual costs due to climate impact in the transport sector are estimated nationally to be 

between S/ . .35 and 1 .4 billion. Flooding is the largest risk for the transport sector in Peru. Historically, 

this climate event has been responsible for most of the damages. During the El Niño event of 19 97 –

98 , damages in t he road s ector accounted for 35 percent of total damages, estimated at S/ . 1.9 billion. 28   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Flooding affects road infrastructure in all provinces in Peru , but most damages are concentrated in the 

department in the north , in the selva (Loreta, Madre de Dios , an d Ucayali) , Junín , and Cusco. 

Figure 1 5: Candidate critical links are in red, mostly related to flooding near the coast 

> Source: World Bank

Climate adaptation of the transport sector in Peru is critical for certain corridors to avoid large economic 

impacts . The Peruvian road network has critical points (Rozenberg et al. 2017) for climate adaptation 

action, determined by its traffic, agriculture exports, and high exposure to climate impacts, distributed 

around three clusters: Carretera Central near Lima, Piura in the north , and a southern part of the Pan -

Americana Road. Disruption of these critical segments would substantially increase travel times and 

costs and exacerbate existing inclusion gaps. Disruption of some of these links could result in more 

than 300 km of detour s, an additional US$400 daily road user cost, and up to US$4 million of economic 

cost s a day . At least four of these high ly critical points are exposed to severe floods ( of 1 in 10 -year 

return periods) with projected wat er depths of more than 50 centimeters on road segments. 

Investing in the climate adaptation of the road sector is critical to support the GoP’s priority of improving 

rural connectivity . Climate adaptation is a critical component to ensure the development of the rural 

road network 15 and to guarantee access to economic opportunities for people from poor rural areas, to 

enhance local markets and increase trade. The type of climate adaptation intervention needed varies 

by region and road segment, but recent studies find that the most ro bust option along the Piura and 

Pan -American road clusters is to flood -proof critical roads by improving drainage capacity and 

performing maintenance work frequently to reduce disruption time related to disastrous events. 

Alternatively, upgrading an existi ng alternative road along Carretera Central region is a robust climate 

adaptation strategy. Regarding natural disaster risk, it is important to continue developing a Sectoral 

Plan for Disaster Risk Management (PSGRD).    

> 15 Aglined wi th GoP priorities, f or more than 20 years, The World Bank has been involved in financing the improvement and rehabilitation of rural roads
> in Peru across five programs. 29
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

# 4. Recommendations 

In this context, Peru ’s transport sector requires efforts toward mitigation and adaptation such as to : (i) 

Increase multimodality and improve freight transport efficiency to reduce GHG emissions; (ii) develop 

mass transit systems and promote NMT modes and electric mobility whi le better managing traffic to 

reduce pollution, improve road safety, and provide access to educational and employment 

opportunities in urban contexts; and (iii) build a resilient and redundant infrastructure network, 

prepared to respond to natural disaster s. 

Figure 1 6: Policy proposals and recommendations 

> Source: Original elaboration

Considering the proposed policies for 2030 and the 13 NDC mitigation measures, 16 GHG emissions 

could be reduced by 8.76 MtCO 2eq. With the reduction objective of 12.23 MtCO 2eq, there is still a gap 

of 3.47 MtCO 2eq to achieve the objective proposed in the document for 2030. There are several 

opportunities for policies to fill the existing gap s. For example, measures to prevent the use of private 

vehicles in S ão Paulo —a city wi th similar characteristics to Lima, although with longer average trips (see 

table 6) —could reduce emissions by up to 6 MtCO 2eq. In addition, the transition to a zero -carbon 

scenario by 2050 involves measures such as electrification and habit changes (which would reduce 

the number of daily trips by the population), which would have a partial impact by 2030. Thus, it remains 

crucial for the GoP to act and expand its transport agenda to achieve fixed emissions objectives. Just 

eight years from 2030, there are no s ufficient investment projects that are advancing progress in this 

direction.

The GoP proposes 14 mitigation measures under the NDC to achieve GHG emissions goals fo r 2030. 

A cost -benefit analysis shows that the measures proposed by MINEM have greater impact than those 

offered by the MTC. It is important to focus on the ones framed in the energy efficiency objective to 

achieve more significant results in a short period. 31
