The mitigation measures M14 (Energy efficiency labeling for light vehicles) and M13 (Promotion of LNG 

for cargo transportation of the natural gas massification project) show largely the best results in terms 

of CO 2 saved per US$1 million invested . Likewise, policies that help to reduce and optimize the route 

of freight vehicles (P1 —truck centers, and P6 —last -mile cyclelogistics) have an important effect on CO 2

emissions, in relation to their implementation cost. Thus, it is important for the GoP to prioritize in the 

short term the policies that have a higher benefit -cost ratio, in order to achieve 2030 NDC objectives .

Nevertheless, the rest of the policies have important benefits that justify implementation . In the urban 

context, investing in mass transit systems and NMT (policies P4, M1, M2, M3, and P5) foster the 

creation of a better living context for the urban population, reducing congestion , travel times, noise ,

and air pollution. On the other hand, the geographic conditions and the country ’s context pave the way 

to facilitate the implementation of specific proposals, such as strengthening cabotage transportation 

(policy P3). 

Among mitigation measures, it is reco mmended that the sustainable mobility plans of Peruvian cities 

increase investment in BRT corridors, as well as to develop the projects in the period until 2050. 

Increasing investment objectives in BRT corridors, not only in Lima -Callao, but also in interm ediate 

Peruvian cities, is an objective that should be contemplated in mobility plans. In the short term, planners 

and policy makers should support increasing the number of BRT kilometers (to 400 km in 2050) in 

cities and estimate the cost s and benefit s th at this may entail. BRT helps reduce transport -related GHGs 

while boosting transit ridership, reducing traffic congestion, and promoting economic development and 

social equity. The climate and economic benefits of investing in this type of infrastructure, which costs 

less than light rail systems, are substantial . In addition, BRT systems favor the reorganization of feeder 

routes and conventional bus systems. Likewise, new corridors would promote the development of clean 

technologies, TOD initiatives in stat ions , and intermodality with nonmotorized means. 

In intermediate cities 17 , transport systems cannot compete against the taxi and private vehicles since 

these are relatively cheap and highly available, which generates the potential risk of increasing the us e

of the private car . Urban infrastructure development has failed to keep pace with the rapid economic 

growth of these cities during the last decades, and now they face significant congestion problems and 

a continuous increase in their vehicle fleet. TMS i s the key to coping with all these challenges. The 

problem is compounded because transport capacities and traffic control mechanisms are typically sub -   

> 17 As defined along this document, these cities are: Ica, Arequipa, Trujillo, Cusco and Piura. 32
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

optimal, employing either fixed -time signal or manual control, and a lack of a centralized control center 

to effectively manage traffic congestion: The current traffic management system of the cities is 

inefficient. In particular, local public transport (with competition from numerous private companies) 

faces several challenges, including slow travel flow, po or safety standards, and high emissions. 

Additionally, driving is becoming increasingly popular because these local public transport systems are 

often poorly organized. Thus, it is crucial to invest in these TMS systems and urban infrastructure in the 

main intermediate cities to lead to the cultural change needed to intend motorization and mobility 

trends, promote sustainable mobility, and reduce GHG emissions. 

Recommendations to promote and increase NMT, including the use of cargo bikes for urban logistics ,

could be implemented immediately and at a low cost. Although the need for nonmotorized mobility is 

designated as an objective in national and local instruments for sustainability, NMT infrastructure has 

not been broadly deployed in order to enhance a mod al shift to cleaner modes , and cyclelogistics has 

not been acknowledged as a means to decarbonize the movement of goods. 

Some legal and regulatory measures that create competitive advantages for pedestrians, bikes, and 

cyclelogistics relative to other mea ns of transport include : (i) regulatory measures like zero -emission 

zones (geographically determined areas where only vehicles with zero -emission standards are allowed), 

pedestrianized streets (like historic city centers), parking regulations; and (ii) mar ket -based measures 

like congestion charges (Central London), taxation, tax allowances, and subsidies. 

At the national level, MTC could update the PNTU (National Policies for Urban Transport) to include 

detailed actions to decarbonize goods movement that go beyond emission and congestion aspects, 

including road safety . At the local level, policy instruments like urban mobility plans, urban development 

plans, and sustainable individual mobility plans could specif y guidelines for cyclelogistics. More 

important ly, specific binding instruments to decarbonize goods transport at the national and local level 

should be enacted (urban logistics plans). 

The network of cycling infrastructure should be extended in Lima and Callao and intermediate cities, 

generating a coh erent (complete, well connected, and signalized with clear messages), direct 

(minimizing detours and delays), safe, and comfortable network for users (emitting the least noise and 

air pollution possible). It m ust be attractive and integrated with urban lan dmarks , green areas, and 

commercial zones in order to generate socioeconomic value. Cargo bikes should be taken into account 

in the design : bike infrastructure should be at least 2 meters (m) in width and ideally 2.25 m for cycle 

lanes and 2.4 m for cycle paths to provide access to all models of cargo bikes. Also, bike lanes should 

be adapted with frequent exits for cargo bikes to do deliveries. It is also important to provide safe space 

for parking in public areas. 

Designating loading zones in dense urban areas will encourage cargo bike use and improve the overall 

efficiency of urban logistics. Charging points that are geographically distributed and adequa tely 

connected with bike infrastructure can maximize the operational advantages of cargo bikes and the 

potential modal shift to e -bikes. Microdeposits or transshipment hubs have as proven essential for 

unleashing cargo bike potential for first - and last -mi le deliveries. 

All stakeholders participating in urban logistics (shippers, transport operators, relevant associations, 

civil society, receivers, etc.) as well as other NMT users should be identified for further integration into 

the planning process. Spac es for stakeholder participation should be kept permanently for consultation 

and evaluation of regulatory measures, infrastructure provision , and data management. Georeferenced 

information about bike infrastructure availability and characteristics should b e made available to 

improve coverage, accessibility, and efficiency. Law enforcement officers (national police) should be 33   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

brought into the planning process as well as during stakeholder engagement phases to ensure security 

and safety. 

Specialized regulations for deliverers from e -delivery companies must be enacted to guarantee 

minimum working conditions according to Peruvian law and to make insurance available to 

cyclelogistics operators. 

The political context of Per u shows a fragmented governance and weak institutional capacity to plan 

and manage efficient, green, and integrated transit systems in key areas, including electromobility, 

promoting nonmotorized or active transport, and better coordination with land use and urban 

development policies. In this sense, it is crucial that governmental and administrative capacity be 

adequate to support measures of such magnitude, allowing good planning and coordination among the 

different actors. In 2018, the Lima and Callao Urban Transport Author ity (ATU) was created. The ATU’s 

legal and institutional framework was approved to address urban transport planning, transport 

integration, infrastructure provision, service concession and permitting, and fare policy, including fare 

setting and adjustments . If current trends are not stopped or reversed, Peruvian cities will face even 

more severe congestion, air pollution, noise, traffic accidents, and economic losses. Thus, it remains 

relevant to strengthen the ATU’s role to coordinate e -mobility, cyclelogi stics , and public transportation 

systems. 

The promotion of initiatives and policies for the electrification of the automotive fleet has been poor 

compared to what is being advanced by other countries in the region. Likewise, it is important to 

guarantee op eration of the electric fleet expected in Lima, supported by coordination between MINEM 

and MINAM. To make 5 percent of the vehicle fleet electric by 2030, it is necessary to promote a more 

competitive regulatory framework with clear incentives for the acq uisition and operation of electric 

vehicles in the country. 

Without changes in the tax structure, decarbonizing road transport in Peru would lead to a moderate 

fiscal impact of -0.3 percent of GDP in the mi dterm (2022 –30) and -1.0 percent of GDP in the lon g

term (2031 –50), while the financial benefits of decarbonizing the transport sector outweigh the fiscal 

impact s, estimated at +0.6 percent of GDP in the mid term and +4.5 percent in the long term. However, 

the GoP can mitigate the fiscal impact with tax re forms. A tax structure with reduced distributional 

impacts requires monitoring the evolution of costs and fuels over time. Excessive zero -emissions 

vehicle costs may already represent a burden for decarbonization; thus, increasing its taxes may limit 

the e ffectiveness of decarbonization policies. The GoP may also explore the incorporation of other new 

taxes. Hydrogen will play an important role in decarbonizing long -distance transport (e.g., buses and 

freight). The country can explore potential revenues fro m its production (electricity -to -hydrogen) and its 

use in the future. Another option is a vehicle -kilometer traveled tax, which can price the externalities of 

driving and replace existing energy -based taxes. While there are policy options to mitigate the f iscal 

impact of decarbonizing the transport sector, including taxes to mitigate the fiscal impact will lower 

financial benefits. Hence, the government must consider taxes ’ socioeconomic impacts before pursuing 

a fiscal reform. Moreover, the fiscal options should be designed to eliminate the fiscal impact, 

considering the additional indirect revenue resulting from the financial benefits. An eventual new tax 

structure for the sector must be designed to avoid disincentivizing the adoption of zero -emission 

tech nologies. 

Adapting roads to climate change calls for a life -cycle approach with a specific focus on asset 

maintenance and preservation. The life -cycle approach to climate adaptation of infrastructure includes 

(i) systems planning , considering integration and redundancy of critical infrastructure to offer 

alternatives; (ii) engineering and design —the use of innovative materials and design specifications that 34   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

enhance the robustness and flexibility of infrastructure; (iii) asset management —improving institutional 

and financial arrangements for infrastructure maintenance, as well as integration of climate and 

disaster risk considerations in th e prioritization of investments in new infrastructure, rehabilitation, and 

restoration; and (iv) contingency programming —policy and institutional frameworks, communication 

protocols, investments in emergency preparedness and response, and alignment of tran sport systems 

and flows with local and regional evacuation, response, and recovery needs. 

Maintaining road infrastructure in good condition is fundamental to reduce climate impacts. Poor and 

delayed maintenance such as the cleaning of road culverts or sid e drains can lead to premature road 

failures, with significant damages and repair cost s. Investing in maintenance offers the highest 

economic returns in the road sector, with US$1 investment in maintenance as effective as US$1.5 0 in 

new investments (Kornej ew, Rentschler, and Hallegatte 2019) . Improving road maintenance could 

reduce asset losses due to climate impacts by between 6 and 18 percent for several small island 

development states (World Bank 2017) . Additionally, better maintenance and investment in 

maintenance must accompany better asset management that would allow move the roads sector to 

shift away from a reactive to a proactive approach. Innovative financing mechanism s can help fill 

investment gaps in maintenance, for example, performance -based contracts (most commonly known 

in Latin America as contracts for restoration and maintenance , or CREMA] ) have been proven to 

improve efficiency of asset preservation works. The CREMA represented a change in paradigm, and 

offered operational efficiency of a bout 19 percent over the total contract amount (Lancelot 2010). 35   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

# Appe ndix C . Key characteristics of W orld Bank 

# engagement in th e transport sector related to climate 

# change 

Lima Metropolitan bus rapid transit (BRT) North Extension (P170595, in execution). The World Bank is 

financing the construction of this 10.2 kilometer (km) BRT, also framed in the first axe of the transport 

sector NDC defined by the Government of Peru. The project will benefit 1.46 million people in four low -

income districts in North Lima, increasing their access to job and education opportunities and t o public 

services. 

Support to the Subnational Transport Program Project —PATS (P172515, in execution). This program 

aims to improve and rehabilitate rural roads. Because of Peruvian geography, population dispersion 

has been a significant cause for rural pov erty, hampering access to markets and public services. For 

more than 20 years, the World Bank has supported the national PATS program to increase accessibility 

to health services, education, and opportunities. 

Cusco Transport Improvement Project —METRA (P1 32505, in execution). The project involves the 

improvement of 6.4 km of Via Expresa Avenue to improve livelihoods and competitiveness in Cusco, by 

bringing better connectivity to more than 150,000 inhabitants. 

Installation of cable accessibility service co nnecting the districts of Independencia and San Juan de 

Lurigancho, Province of Lima (P170609, in preparation). The World Bank aims to support the 

construction of this 6 km length cable car to give better accessibility conditions to the northern Lima 

popul ation, where the poorest neighborhoods are. The cable car will be connected to the Metropolitano. 

The World Bank is executing a GFFDR TF (TF0B2112) to improve resilience to climate and geophysical 

hazards in Northern Lima’s Transport Network. 

Lima and Cal lao Urban Transport Authority —ATU (P173856, in preparation). The World Bank is 

supporting the enhancement of the ATU, an institution recently created to address urban transport 

planning, transport integration, infrastructure provision, and service concessi on. 

Lima Traffic Management to Support nonmotorized transport expansion (P178842, in preparation). 

The World Bank will support Lima’s Metropolitan Municipality to improve traffic management and make 

Lima’s urban transport sector more efficient, safer, and greener, especially for transit corridors and 

nonmotorized transport. 

Support the transaction model analysis for the provision, operation, and maintenance of e -buses in the 

conventional transport system in Peru (TF0B6283, in execution). This consultancy a ims to inform the 

strategy to introduce electric buses in the conventional transport system, and long -term strategies to 

support electromobility in the Lima Metropolitan Area public transport system. It corresponds to the 

third axe of the Government of Per u’s NDC objectives. 

Peru: Enabling a Green and Resilient Development DPF (P177765, in preparation). The Program 

Development Objective is to support Government policies to: (i) strengthen the foundations for a green 

economic recovery, (ii) build resilience and enhance climate change adaptation, and (iii) support the 

transition toward a greener economy in selected sectors. It supports among others: (i) the disaster risk 

management national policy, and (ii) measures to support massive transit systems and a move toward 

vehicles with lower emissions. 42   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

# Appendi x D. Fiscal Impact of Transport Decarbonization 

# in Peru 

Abstract 

Peru aims to become a net -zero economy by 2050, with total net benefits equivalent to 60 percent of 

its 2019 gross domestic product (GDP) (MINAM 2021) . Two -thirds of these benefits would be linked to 

road transport as its decarbonization red uces operational and maintenance costs and boosts health 

and productivity while lowering accidents (Quirós -Tortós et al. 2021) . These transformations would also 

cut down on fuel consumption, thus reducing the associated tax income —almost 11 percent of total 

government revenue in 2019. Anticipating the possible tax revenue loss from transport decarbonization 

can prepare policy makers for possible fiscal reforms (OECD 2019; Rodriguez et al. 2021; van Dender 

2019) .

This study investigates the fiscal i mpact of decarbonizing Peru’s road transport over the years 2022 –

50. The fiscal impact is the amount of tax revenue not realized due to decarboniz ation ( relative to the 

status quo) . We contrast this fiscal impact with the financial impacts of decarbonizing , i.e., the avoided 

expenses relative to the status quo. The study builds upon a cost -benefit analysis of a net -zero transport 

sector (Quirós -Tortós et al. 2021) . Without changes in the tax structure, we find that decarbonizing road 

transport would have a moderate fiscal impact ( -0.8 percent of yearly average GDP) that occurs mainly 

in the 2031 -2050 period and is lower than the financial benefits of decarbonization (+3.3 percent of 

yearly average GDP). We argue that tax reforms that consider distributive im pacts can mitigate this 

fiscal impact without affecting businesses and households; possible options are eliminating 

exonerations of zero emissions vehicles and introducing a distance traveled tax. 

Keywords 

Decarbonization; financial impacts; fiscal policy; Peru; road transport; bottom -up modeling 

## D.1. Introduction 

Peru has made efforts to reduce its greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. In 2015, it presented its first 

Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) with a conditional target of 238.6 megatons of carbo n

dioxide equivalent (MtCO 2e ) by 2030. In 2020 , in the process of updating its NDCs, the country 

increased its level of ambition and committed to 208,8 MtCO 2e by the same year. In 2021, the country 

updat ed its 2015 National Strategy against Climate Change to include the net -zero goal of 2050. 

The costs and benefits of a deep -decarbonization pathway (DDP), that has been recently developed, 

have been evaluated against a business -as -usual (BAU) scenario (Quirós -Tortós et al. 2021). The DDP 

requires transformat ions in the economy to reduce emissions while maintaining economic growth. 

Examples of such transformations are deploying renewables in the electricity system, electrifying end 

uses, promoting public transport, rever sing deforestation and increasing refore station, reducing waste 

and increasing efficiency, and changing consumption habits. On the other hand, under a BAU scenario ,

based on trends, a greater amount of fossil fuel energy is consumed , deforestation continues to grow, 

and waste increases while mee ting the needs of a growing and wealthier population. The study found 

that following a DDP allows GHG emissions to be reduced to net zero by midcentury , with a net 

socioeconomic benefit of US$140 billion, about 60 percent of 2019 gross domestic product ( GDP). 

The DDP would enable a more sustainable transport system by 2050. Passenger transport demand 

would be about 25 percent higher in 2050 than in 2022 ( figur e AD1 .1). Under BAU , by contrast, demand 43   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

would rise by 221 percent (figure AD1 .1). Greater efficiency is behind the demand difference between 

the scenarios: more public transit translate s into fewer vehicles and kilometers traveled in the DDP 

than in the B AU. Another difference is vehicle type: in the DDP, 31 percent of vehicles would be zero 

emission vehicles (ZEV s) by 2030, rising to 100 percent by 2050. ZEVs are more energy efficient than 

internal combustion engine vehicles (ICEVs), reducing energy needs . In freight transport, the effect is 

felt after 2030, and the difference in demand in both the DDP and the BAU are small in 2050 (see 

figure AD1 .1). The reduction is primarily related to improvement s in logistics. The DDP also involves full 

freight electr ification by 2050. Energy consumption in the transport sector would shift (see figure 

AD1 .2) from a reliance on diesel, gasohol, gasoline, natural gas, and liqu efied petroleum gas (LPG) to 

electricity by 2050. Appendix A presents these transformations in more detail .

From a fiscal perspective, the deployment of the DDP translates into reduced government revenue from 

fossil fuels and ICEVs. But the sales tax on ele ctricity consumed for transportation and taxes on ZEVs 

would partially compensat e for revenue lost. In 2019, total government revenue represented 16.6 

percent of GDP (OECD 2021). Transport -related revenue in the same year was estimated at 1.8 percent 

of GD P, and about 11 percent of total government revenue (SUNAT 2022) : fuel excises and gasoline 

sales tax represented about 0.5 percent of GDP (SUNAT 2022) ; sales tax on fuels , another 0.5 percent 

(SUNAT 2022) ; import, excise, and property tax es on vehicles, 0.2 percent (Rivera 2021; SUNAT 2022) ,

and sales tax on vehicles , 0.6 percent (SUNAT 2022) .

This study estimates the fiscal impact of decarbonizing the transport sector in Peru. We define the fiscal 

impact as the unrealized tax revenue resulting from the de ployment of the DDP relative to the BAU 

scenario —that is, the difference between the DDP and BAU scenarios . The study builds upon a cost -

benefit analysis of decarbonizing the economy in Peru (Quirós -Tortós et al. 2021). A revenue estimation 

model is used to evaluate government income per scenario from fossil fuel consumption, electricity 

consumption for transport, and vehicle type. We use n ational statistics (OECD 2021; Rivera 2021; 

SUNAT 2022) to calibrate the revenue estimation model for the 2018 –21 period. We estimate revenue 

related to fossil fuels and vehicle excise tax ; fossil fuel, electricity, and vehicle sales tax ; import tax on 

vehicles ; and property tax on vehicles. The fiscal impact is estimated from 2022 to 2050. We contrast 

the fiscal impact with the financial impact of decarbonizing the transport sector. The financial impact 

is defined as the total costs under a BAU scenario minus the total cost under the DDP. A positive value 

means that decarb onizing is less expensive than the status quo ( i.e., implying a net benefit ). The 

opposite means a net cost. While the analysis is annual , we present yearly average results for two 

periods: 2022 –30 and 2031 –50. 

We first evaluate the fiscal and financial im pacts of decarbonizing Peru’s transport sector considering 

base values of costs trajectories, adoption of technologies, and demands. These base values are 

standard assumptions that can change over the planning horizon. We then assess the effects of these 

uncertainties on the fiscal and financial impacts. To do this, we use the 1 ,001 plausible futures of a 

decarbonize d Peruvian transport sector produced in Quirós -Tortós et al. (2021). 

The remainder of this paper is structured as follows: section D. 2 presents the methodology, section D. 3

the results, and section D. 4 the conclusions and policy implications. 

## D.2. Methodology 

We build upon the methodology suggested by Conte, Rasteletti, and Muñoz (2022) and its practical 

implementation in a study of Costa Rica ( Rodriguez et al. 2021). Government revenues from the 

transport sector are linked to fossil fuel and electricity consumption, vehicle costs, and tax rates 44   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

associated with each technology and energy vector. In Peru the road transport tax structure is as 

foll ows: 

• Fossil fuels involve an excise tax ( impuesto selectivo al consumo , ISC), a gasoline sales tax ( al 

rodaje ), and a general sales tax ( impuesto general sobre las ventas ). 18  The excise tax varies by 

fuel, and the gasoline sales tax applies only to gasoline. A general sales tax is applied to all .

• Electricity involves only the general sales tax. 

• Fossil fuel vehicles pay an import tax, an excise tax, a general sales tax, and a property tax. 

The se differ by vehicle type .

• ZEVs are currently exempt from the excise and sales taxes , and pay only import and property 

taxes. 

Annex D 2 shows the tax rates per type of fuel and vehicle. The revenue associated with each tax is 

calculated using the energy consu mption (fossil or electricity) or vehicle cost multiplied by the 

corresponding tax rate. The steps to estimate the yearly fiscal impact are to :

1. Estimate fossil energy consumption using OSeMOSYS -Peru and national statistics. 

2. Multiply the energy consumption per fuel (i.e., diesel, gasoline, LPG, natural gas) by the 

respective fuel unit taxes , to obtain the government revenue from fossil energy taxes ( 𝐺 𝑅 𝑓 ). 

3. Extract electricity consumption from OSeMOSYS -Peru. 

4. Combine the electricity consumption, the electric ity price ,19 and an electricity tax rate to 

estimate the government revenue from electricity ( 𝐺 𝑅 𝑒 ). 

5. Extract vehicle costs from OSeMOSYS -Peru. 

6. Multiply the vehicle cost per type of vehicle (e.g., sedan, taxi, bus, freight) and fuel (diesel, 

gasoline, ZEV ) by the respective vehicle unit taxes to obtain government revenue from vehicles 

(𝐺 𝑅 𝑣 ). 

The fiscal impact, 𝑭𝑰 , is then defined as the government revenue under a DDP scenario relative to the 

BAU scenario, both as proportions of the GDP, which can be w ritten as follows: 

𝐹𝐼 = 𝐺 𝑅 𝑓 ,𝐷𝐷𝑃 + 𝐺 𝑅 𝑒 ,𝐷𝐷𝑃 + 𝐺 𝑅 𝑣 ,𝐷𝐷𝑃 − 𝐺 𝑅 𝑓 ,𝐵𝐴𝑈 − 𝐺 𝑅 𝑒 ,𝐵𝐴𝑈 − 𝐺 𝑅 𝑣 ,𝐵𝐴𝑈  1

A negative value means reduced income from a DDP scenario, whereas a positive value means that 

the DDP scenario brings greater revenue than the BAU scenario . Since this work does not aim to study 

changes in the tax structure, we keep it constant over the 2022 –50 period. 

To understand the national financial impact of decarbonizing the transport sector, the fiscal impact on 

the gove rnment is contrasted against the financial impact on transport users, defined as the BAU 

scenario’s capital and operational costs minus the DDP’s costs . A positive financial impact (i.e., a net 

benefit for users) means that implementing the DDP scenario wo uld reduce expenses ; a negative 

financial impact (i.e., a net cost) means the opposite. Both metrics are calculated as a proportion of the 

GDP. 

The fiscal and financial impacts are initially estimated considering a base case that assumes trajectory 

costs of technologies current ly available in the literature, as well as their adoption, and transport 

demands, as adopted in Quirós -Tortós et al. (2021) . However, there are uncertainties associated with     

> 18 It includes the promotional tax for municip alities.
> 19 Since the study by Quirós -Tortós et al. (2021) does not provide electricity prices, it is assumed that the current price (16 cents/kilowatt -hour) remains
> constant over time. 45
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

their evolution over time. Thus, we estimate the fiscal and financial impacts of t he 1 ,001 plausible 

decarbonization trajectories produced by Quirós -Tortós et al. (2021). 

## D.3. The fiscal impact of decarbonizing Peru’s transport sector 

The yearly average fiscal impact of decarbonizing Peru ’s transport sector during the 2022 –50 period 

is estimated at -0.8 percent of GDP. Under a BAU scenario (figure D. 1a), yearly average tax revenue 

would remain around 1.8 percent of GDP over the entire period ; in the DDP, this would lower to 1.4 

percent of GDP in the midterm (2022 –30) and to 0.8 percent of GDP in the long term (2031 –50). Thus, 

the yearly average fiscal impact in the midterm is estimated at -0.3 percent of GDP (figure D.1b). It 

would grow to -1.0 percent of GDP in the long term. 

The fiscal impact would primarily occur in the long term when electrification becomes predominant. 

Electrifying the fleet would lower the consumption of fossil fuels, reducing the revenue from the fuel 

excise tax (impuesto selectivo al consumo ), gasoline sales tax ( al rodaje ), and general sales tax 

(impuesto gen eral a las ventas ) on fuels (figure D. 1b). The fiscal impact of the fuel excise tax is limited 

in the midterm —less than -0.1 percent of GDP. In the long term, it increases to -0.3 percent of GDP 

(yearly average) as fewer fossil fuels are consumed. Revenue from gasoline sales is currently low. Its 

fiscal impact is practically null in the midterm, and its yearly average in the long term accounts for -0.1 

percent of GDP. The fiscal impact of the general sales tax is the largest among fuel -related taxes. Its 

ye arly average is -0.1 percent of GDP in the midterm and then increases to -0.4 percent of GDP in the 

long term. 

Vehicle -related fiscal impacts (import, excise, general sales, and property taxes) would represent -0.2 

percent and -0.5 percent of GDP in the mi d and long term, respectively. Import and property taxes are 

the lowest and together represent less than -0.1 percent in the mid and long term (yearly average). The 

fiscal impact of the excise tax in the midterm accounts for nearly -0.1 percent of GDP, inc reasing to 

more than -0.1 percent of GDP (yearly average). The fiscal impact of the general sales tax is the largest 

among vehicle -related taxes. Its yearly average in the midterm represents -0.2 percent of GDP, which 

then doubles in the long term. 46   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Fig ure D. 1. a) Tax revenue per tax and period , b) fis cal impact per tax and period , and c) financial impact 

per period (as a percentage of GDP )

> Note: The horizontal red line represents net values.

Revenue loss may be partially compensated by increased revenue from electricity for transport. The 

deployment of ZEVs would raise electricity demand , and thus government revenue from the electricity 

general sales tax. While its fiscal impact is practically null in the midterm, as electrification is limited in 

this period, in the long term, the yearly average fiscal impact accounts for +0.3 percent of GDP. 

The decarbonization of the transport sector would bring a positive financial impact (a net benefit) that 

is larger than the fiscal impact (figure D. 1c). Transforming the transport sector from a fossil -fuel -based 

to a zero -emission one requires higher capit al investments (e.g., in electric buses and hydrogen trucks). 

However, using ZEVs reduces operational costs as they are cheaper to maintain and operate over time. 

In the midterm, additional yearly average investments are estimated at -0.5 percent of GDP. T hese are 

compensated by reduced operation and maintenance costs from ZEVs (+1.1 percent of GDP), which 

result in a yearly average net benefit in the midterm of +0.6 percent of GDP. In the long term, yearly 

average investments required under the DDP relativ e to the BAU scenario increase to -4.0 percent of 

GDP. These investments are overcompensated by significantly reduced operation and maintenance 

costs (+8.5 percent of GDP), resulting in a yearly average net financial benefit of +4.5 percent of GDP. 

Over th e entire analysis period (2022 –50), the yearly average net financial benefit represents +3.3 

percent of GDP. 

While these are the direct financial impacts of decarbonizing the transport sector , they are far from all 

the benefits to be expected . Added up, the economic benefits of decarbonizing transport —such as fewer 

accidents, better air quality, and higher productivity caused by lower congestion —amount to a net 

economic benefit of US$92 billion by 2050 (Quirós -Tortós et al. 2021 ). 

The above estimates do not consider the indirect tax revenue resulting from the financial benefits of 

transport decarbonization in the country. In practice, the financial benefits of transport decarbonization 

will be used in activities not necessarily r elated to transport, generating additional revenue for the 

government. Estimating this indirect collection at 16.5 percent of the financial benefit of the actors, 47   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

based on the average revenue in Peru between 1990 and 2019 (OECD 2021) , the yearly average fi scal 

impact of the decarbonization of transport in the period 2022 –50 would be -0.3 percent of GDP. 

Even under uncertainty, the fiscal impact of decarbonizing the transport sector is likely to be smaller 

than its financial impact. Figure D. 2 shows the 2022 –50 yearly average fiscal ( y axis) and financial ( x

axis) impacts of the 1 ,001 plausible futures produced in Quirós -Tortós et al. (2021), which considers 

uncertainties around the costs and adoption of technologies and transport demand. The analysis 

highlig hts that the larger the financial benefit for Peruvians, the larger the direct fiscal impact on the 

government .20 We define a future as success ful when its fiscal impact is smaller than its financial 

benefit. The opposite is defined as a risk. About 95 percent of the plausible futures are successful (blue 

circles). On average, for all the plausible futures explored, the yearly average fiscal impact is estimated 

at -0.7 percent of GDP, and the yearly average financial impact is estimated at +3.1 percent of GDP. 

There are common causes of risk (crosses in figure D. 3). Three -quarters of the cases with risk are 

explained by ZEV costs higher than the valu es considered for the base case. High ZEV costs result in 

low financial benefits as users need to spend more to electrify transport. Moreover, since ZEV s are 

currently exempt from excise and general sales taxes, high ZEV costs do not increase revenue in th e

future. 

Figure D. 2. The fiscal and financial impacts of decarbonizing the transport sector under uncertainty      

> Source: The figure shows average values of the 1,001 plausible futures created in Quirós -Tortós et al. 2021
> Note: The vertical line shows the range of values with minimum and maximum values at the extremes.
> 20 In practice, there is an indirect revenue resulting from larger financial benefits that can partially compensate this revenue loss, thus leading to a lower
> fiscal impact for the government. 48
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Figure D. 3. Common causes of fiscal impacts being larger than financial benefits

## D.4. Key takeaways and policy implications 

The findings of this study suggest the following key takeaways: 

➢ Without changes in the tax structure and under base assumptions, decarbonizing road 

transport in Peru would lead to a moderate fiscal impact of -0.3 percent of GDP in the m idterm 

(2022 –30) and -1.0 percent of GDP in the long term (2031 –50). The yearly average fiscal 

impact over the 2022 –50 period is -0.8 percent of GDP. 

➢ The financial benefits of decarbonizing the transport sector outweigh the fiscal impact. These 

benefits ar e estimated at +0.6 percent of GDP in the midterm and +4.5 percent of GDP in the 

long term. Over the 2022 –50 period, the yearly average financial benefits are +3.6 percent of 

GDP. 

➢ The benefits of decarbonizing are larger if we add the economic benefits fro m a reduced 

number of accidents, better air quality, and greater productivity. 

➢ Indirect tax revenue resulting from the country ’s financial benefits of transport decarbonization 

may lead to a yearly average fiscal impact of transport decarbonization in 2022 –50 of only -

0.3 percent of GDP. 

➢ Decarbonizing the transport sector would lead to greater financial benefits than its fiscal 

impact, even under uncertainty. Over 95 percent of the plausible pathways to net -zero transport 

would result in larger financial benefits than fiscal impacts. 

The findings of this study and international experience suggest the following policy implications: 

➢ The Government of Peru can mitigate the fiscal impact of decarbonization with tax reforms. 

➢ Adapting Peru’s tax structure requires assessing the corresponding distributional impacts. 

While taxes on diesel may seem beneficial for decarbonization, they may increase the costs of 

goods and services and bus trips —leading to greater impacts on the first two income quintiles. 49   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

➢ A tax structure with reduced distributional impacts requires monitoring the evolution of costs 

and fuels over time. Excessive ZEV costs may already represent a burden for decarbonization; 

thus, increasing the tax es levied on ZEVs may limit the effectiveness of decarbonization policies. 

➢ The Government of Peru may also explore the incorporation of other new taxes. Hydrogen will 

play an important role in decarbonizing long -distance transport (e.g., buses and freight). The 

country can explore potential revenues from its production (electricity -to -hydrogen) and its use 

in the future. Another option is a vehicle -kilometer traveled (VKT) tax, which can price the 

externalities of driving and substitute existing energy -based taxes (van Dender 2019) .

➢ ZEVs are currently exempt from the excise tax ( impuesto selectivo al consumo ) and the general 

sales tax ( impuesto general sobre las ventas ). However, when ICEVs and ZEVs reach cost parity, 

the government could tax these technologi es to compensate for revenue loss. For instance: (1 )

if an excise tax of 10 percent is introduced to ZEVs in 2035, the yearly average fiscal impact 

over the 2022 –50 period would be -0.6 percent of GDP ; (2 ) if the general sales tax of 18 

percent is applied to ZEVs starting in 2035, the yearly average fiscal impact over the 2022 –50 

period would be -0.5 percent of GDP ; and (3 ) if both taxes are applied to ZEVs from 2035 

onwards, the yearly average fiscal impact over the 2022 –50 period would be -0.3 percent of 

GDP. 

➢ While there are policy options to mitigate the fiscal impact of decarbonizing the transport 

sector, including taxes to mitigate the fiscal impact will lower financial benefits. Hence, the 

government must consider the taxes ’ socioeconomic impacts befor e pursuing a fiscal reform. 

Moreover, the fiscal options should be designed to eliminate the fiscal impact, considering the 

additional indirect revenue resulting from the financial benefits. 

## ➢ Eventual ly, a new tax structure for the sector must be designed t o avoid disincentivizing the 

adoption of zero -emission technologies.

## Annex D1. Summary of transformations in the transport sector 

This work is based upon the results presented in Quirós -Tortós et al. (2021) , thus complementing the 

assessment of costs and benefits by analyzing the fiscal implications of moving forward with the 

decarbonization of the transport sector. Here, we summariz e the key transformations of this pathway. 

Passenger and freight demand grows with the gross domestic product of the country. Under a 

business -as -usual (BAU) scenario, it doubles from 2022 values (figure AD1 .1a). While it still grows in 

the deep decarboniz ation pathway (DDP), efficiency measures lower their magnitude by 2050. The 

scenario considers a modal shift from private transport to nonmotorized transport (pedestrian and 

bicycle use). It also takes into account the growing digitization of services and teleworking, which help 

reduce passenger demand by a total of 30 percent by midcentury. Investments in infrastructure and 

the densification of cities enable a reduction in passenger demand by another 15 percent by 2050. 

Similarly, the scenario considers in frastructure investments and logistics improvements that reduce 

freight demand by 20 percent by 2050. 

The deep decarbonization pathway reaches net -zero emissions in 2050 thanks to t he electrification 

of the entire private, public, and cargo fleets (figure AD1 .1b). By 2030, 30 percent of the private and 

public fleet will be electrified by battery or be hydrogen based ( especially for buses). The 

electrification of freight transport with battery or hydrogen -based vehicles begins in 2030. 

Figure A D1 .2 shows the consumption of fossil fuels per scenario. Under a BAU scenario, diesel 

consumption over time remains predominant, followed by gasohol, natural gas, and liqu efied 51   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

petroleum gas (LPG). The DDP lowers the use of fossil fuels over time and increases electrici ty use. 

The energy demand for transport also lowers over time under a DDP , thanks to the higher efficiency of 

zero -emission vehicles (ZEVs) compared to internal combustion engine vehicles (ICEVs) .

## E.1.  Introduction 

The negative externalities of urban logistics, such as pollution, congestion, and road safety, have a 

significant impact on cities. These impacts are expected to increase due to rapid urbanization in the 

Global South and the rise of e -commerce and new business models that have increased the demand 

for goods’ movement in urban environments. However, research and policy remain highly focused on 

passenger transport ra ther than freight. 

As cities and businesses search for ways to mitigate these impacts, one alternative that has gained 

momentum is cyclelogistics, which refers to transporting goods, including parcels, freight, and tools for 

service delivery, using bikes o r tricycles, either human powered or assisted by zero -emission engines, 

with or without adapted compartments or trailers. Sustainable urban mobility activists have been 

asserting for over a decade that these vehicles could replace fossil fuel vehicles (e.g ., light commercial 

vehicles [LCVs], cars, motorcycles, vans, and even small trucks). Moreover, recent technological 

improvements to e -cargo bikes that allow longer battery ranges, facilitate bigger payloads, and mitigate 

driver fatigue, have dissipated th e problems associated with traditional cargo bikes. 

This report identifies international experiences and lessons in the implementation of cyclelogistics in 

urban environments and provides recommendations for the large -scale implementation of 

cyclelogistic s in the Peruvian context, taking into consideration the country’s regulatory and policy 

frameworks as well as the barriers for implementation. Specifically, it: 

• Review s local and national programs, initiatives, laws, and regulations related to the 

sustain able movement of goods in urban areas (spatial organization, loading and unloading 

rules, security, registration, and controls). 

• Review s international experiences in the Global North and South in the implementation of 

cyclelogistics. 

• Define s potential indi cators to evaluate the implementation process of a cargo bike system at 

a local scale .

• Estimate s the potential impact that introducing cargo bikes will have on carbon dioxide 

emissions, based on two scenarios (business as usual, and optimistic) .

• Propose as pects that could be included in regulations and laws to incentivize cyclelogistics, 

Finally, the report highlights aspects than could be included in regulations and policy instruments to 

incentivize cyclelogistics at the national and local levels. These i nclude but are not limited to economic 

incentives, process support programs, tax reliefs, traffic restrictions, infrastructure, and institutional 

arrangements. 

## E.2. Problem: Urban logistics and the last mile 

Urban logistics is a critical aspect of supply chains and urban mobility. Whereas goods delivery is 

inherent to urban life, the associated negative externalities are partially responsible for the problems 

most often linked to urban life such as noise, congestion, pollution, and road safety. 

First, we define urban logistics and the last mile and briefly describe related processes, especially those 

with a large impact on urban environments. Urban logistics is “the part of supply chain management 

that plans, implements, and controls the efficient, effecti ve forward and reverse flow and storage of 

goods, services, and related information between the point of origin and point of consumption to meet 

customers’ requirements” (Rose et al. 2017). 57   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

For this report , urban logistics is classified by the type of good s carried: parcel or freight. While parcel 

delivery is concerned with parcels and small packages, freight relates to items above 32 kilograms (kg), 

such as furniture and construction materials (World Economic Forum 2020). 

The commercial uses and supply ch ains of both parcel s and freight are very wide and vary according to 

a city’s size and the economic agents’ demands (Dablanc 2009) . Further, whereas passenger transport 

can be classified by transport mode, goods transport is highly fragmented and could be organized by 

transport mode, type of operator, origin of goods (local, long distance), relationship between shipper 

and receiver, size of the parcel, and so on. 

Goods transport can also be classified based on who transports the goods: manufacturers or sellers 

(e.g., local level businesses, small and medium enterprises), or a professional carrier. 

Last -mile logistics centers on the process when a good reaches its destination and is delivered to the 

final recipient. This stage is the most expensive, time consuming, inefficient, and polluting of the entire 

supply chain (Dolan 2022; Nurnberg 2019). Last -mile trips are characterized by fossil fuel vehicles out 

for delivering goods to the recipient, constantly stopping, disrupting, and congesting road flow al ong 

already congested roads, producing pollution and noise. Another typical inefficiency in urban logistics 

has to do with the use of oversized vehicles for deliveries. One cyclelogistics operator in Lima reports 

that 33 percent of urban freight is moved b y oversized vehicles (Figueroa and Polo 2022) .

Furthermore, last -mile trips add to congestion beyond the evident occupation of road space. While 

some cities have addressed the issue by creating loading and unloading zones along curbs of 

commercial streets during specific time frames, these spaces are already occupied on many occasions, 

forcing delivery vans and trucks to stop in second lanes that should be devoted to traffic flow or even 

invad ing public space dedicated to pedestrians and cyclists (sidewalk s, crosswalks) .

E.2.1. Urban population and motorization growth 

Exacerbating the existing complex ity of urban logistics, the expected demographic growth of cities 

(which will host 60 percent of the global population by 2030 , equaling 5.1 billion people) is expected 

to increase the demand for goods and services within already congested urban areas. 

Rapid urbanization is particularly dramatic in the Global South. It is estimated that future urban growth 

will mostly happen in cities in developing countries and that by 2025, among the 25 largest cities 

globally, only four will be in developed countries (CEPAL 2015). For its part, Peru has quadrupled its 

urban population during the last 50 years, adding more than 20 million dwel lers to its cities (GRADE 

2022) . Whereas in 1950, only 41 percent of the population was urban , by 2019 a bout 78 percent of 

the total Peruvian population (32 million) live d in urban areas, with more than one -third located in the 

Lima Metropolitan Area (LMA) (10.28 million). LMA’s spectacular demographic growth is explained by 

a high degree of state centralization and economic activity that persist ed over time, with demographic 

projections forecasting the area to have 13 million inhabitants by 2035 (INEI 2015 ). 

Another critical feature of urbanization in Peru is that almost half of its urban population (45.5 percent) 

lives in slums or inadequate housing. Informal settlements continue to expand to peripheral regions 

that are prone to climate hazards, increasin g the pressure on adequate public service provision and 

accessibility to goods, services, and jobs (Instituto Nacional de Estad ística y Censos 2017). 

Also, income improvement in middle - and low -income countries is reflected by the sustained growth in 

moto rization. The growth of Peru’s vehicle fleet has been 6 percent on average since 2010 (Ciudades 58   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Como Vamos 2021). Accelerated motorization will increase congestion and energy consumption in 

urban environments, despite the newer energy -efficient vehicles. 

By 2025, it is estimated that there will be 1 billion more consumers in emerging commercial cities. In 

addition to this , a significant part of intraurban trade will be made up of a complex network of informal, 

traditional channels, which could be described as “one -person businesses.” By 2025, there will be at 

least 10 million of these nano businesses in the world’s 600 largest cities (Blanco and Fransoo 2013). 

E. 2.2 Negative impacts of urban logistics: Pollution, congestion, health, and high costs 

Pollution is one of the most severe negative externalities related to motorized transport. In Peru, 10.25 

percent of total emissions nationwide come from the transportation sector. Among the transport 

subsectors, 92 percent of emissions came from road tran sport, 5 percent from civil aviation, 2 percent 

from sea transport, 1 percent from rail transport, and 0.1 percent from others. Besides the enormous 

danger s of climate change, these emissions also present considerable risks to human health. 

CO 2 emissions is a significant concern from a climate perspective. But for local air quality, the most 

important are local pollutants, which include carbon monoxide (CO), nitrogen dioxide (NO 2), 

hydrocarbons (HC), and particulate matter (PM) among others, which can have toxic effects on human 

health. 

When nitrogen is released during fuel combustion, it combines with oxygen atoms to create nitric oxide 

(NO). It further combines with oxygen to produce nitrogen dioxide (NO 2). NO 2 and NO are referred to as 

oxides of nitrogen (NOx). NOx gases react to form smog and acid rain and are central to the formation 

of fine particles (PM) and ground -level ozone, both of which are associated with adverse health effects. 

PM is a mixture of solid particles and liquid droplets found in the air. These particles are about 1/30th 

the width of human hair and consist of dust, dirt, smoke, and heavy metals. Their tiny size makes them 

especially dangerous as they can penetrate the respiratory tract, leading to lung cancer, stroke, heart 

disease, a nd other serious illnesses (Alvarez, Wadhwa, and Urvashi 2020). 

Some PM are emitted directly from a source, such as construction sites, unpaved roads, fields, 

smokestacks, or fires. However, most particles are form ed in the atmosphere due to the complex 

reactions of chemicals such as sulfur dioxide and nitrogen oxides, which are pollutants emitted from 

power plants, industries, and automobiles. Particle pollution includes: 

• PM 10 : Inhalable particles with diameters that are generally 10 micrometers and small er. 

• PM 2.5 : Fine, inhalable particles with diameters that are generally 2.5 micrometers and smaller. 

In the case of Lima, air quality is monitored by nine meteorological stations. These stations report that 

PM 2.5 and PM 10 concentrations are up to three time s higher than the recommended level by the World 

Trade Organization and nitrogen oxides are twice the recommended concentration level (SENAMHI 

2022). The Ministry of Transport and Communications (MTC) estimates that 50 percent of the fine 

particulate matte r (PM 2.5 ) present in the city’s air can be attributed to road transport. Further, 

cardiovascular, and respiratory conditions attributed to PM w ere responsible for 1,657 deaths in Lima 

in 2014 (MINAM 2014) 

Almost all of Lima’s geographical areas report nois e pollution well above recommended levels. A study 

conducted in 2015 found that areas with critical levels of noise pollution we re associated with heavy 

vehicular traffic (MINAM 2015). According to research, LCVs cause more noise pollution than do rail ,

water, and heavy -duty vehicles (Maes 2017). 59   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

According to research in Europe, urban logistics represent s between 8 to 18 percent of traffic flows 

(MDS Transmodal Limited 2012), using about 30 percent of road capacity for pickup and delivery 

operations (Noce rino et al. 2016). In the case of Lima, congestion causes 76 percent of the population’s 

trip length s to range from 30 minutes to more than an hour. Even in intermediate cities like Piura, 35 

percent of the population’s trip lengths range from 30 minutes t o more than two hours (MTC 2019). 

As regards road safety, EU road traffic data show that even though trucks make up only 3 percent of 

road traffic, they are responsible for 14 percent of fatal collisions (European Cyclist Federation 2012 b). 

The situation in Peru, as in many developing countries, is critical. Even if there is no specific information 

on the involvement of LCVs and trucks in road safety incidents, around 100,000 accidents have been 

recorded every year and 2,696 deaths were reported in 2017 . However, estimates from the World 

Health Organization suggest that traffic fatalities in Peru are much higher —at around 4,300 annually. 

Further, the trend shows no signs of a downturn, with the total number of fatalities increasing between 

2010 and 2019 (Ci udades Como Vamos 2021), making Peru the country with the highest mortality rate 

caused by road accidents in the region (MTC 2019). Also, Peru shows a high crash rate per 100,000 

residents in its main cities, with 90 percent of all crashes occurring in urb an environments (MTC 2019). 

Lima (642.4), Puerto Maldonado (575.1), and Arequipa and Mollendo (both with 543.8) are the cities 

with the highest crash rates (MTC 2018). 

E.2. 3. COVID -19, new tech, and business models 

The COVID -19 pandemic has increased the d emand for urban freight and parcel delivery, in part due to 

the rise of e -commerce, which tripled sales from 2014 to 2019. New business models such as e -

delivery together with technological transformations in businesses and a rising middle class are 

expect ed to increase usage of delivery vehicles by 36 percent by 2030, resulting in a rise in greenhouse 

gas ( GHG ) emissions (by 32 percent) and congestion levels (by 21 percent) (World Economic Forum 

2020). 

Moreover, the emergence of business models has signif icantly impacted urban parcel and freight 

delivery. Particularly, e -delivery companies offering a variety of products (food, retail, mail, cash) and 

smartphone apps for “ deliver ies in less than 30 minutes ” have had exponential growth. Cities such as 

Lima, Bogota, Sao Paulo, and Buenos Aires have seen streets and public spaces fill up with 

bikes/motorbikes that are refurbished to carry small -to -medium parcels, driven by young informal 

workers —mainly immigrants who earn a small commission for each delivery. R appi, one of the 

“superapps” in the region, reported 300, 000 delivery persons across nine countries in Latin America. 

E-delivery operators in Lima have had a disruptive effect on the urban environment, with about 50,000 

self -employed deliver y persons usin g bikes, e -bikes, and motorcycles. E -delivery sales in Peru increased 

by about 70 percent from 2020 levels, reaching S/. 43 million  (Diario Gestión, 2021) . According to the 

Peruvian Foreign Trade Office (ComexPeru ), mobility and e -delivery platforms increased gross domestic 

product ( GDP ) by 0.25 percent. More importantly, these apps represent 1.4 percent of Lima’s total 

economically active population. 

Moreover, e -delivery services amounted to PEN 2.6 million in Apr il 2021, doubling pre -pandemic levels. 

Although these services are concentrated in Lima (87.5 percent), other cities such as Arequipa (4.5 

percent), Trujillo (3.4 percent), Chiclayo (1.4 percent), and Piura (1.3 percent) are also experiencing an 

emergence in e -delivery (Diario Gestión 2021). 

These factors have put urban freight and parcel delivery at the core of urban and transport planners ’

discussions. Unsurprisingly, the negative externalities of urban logistics are expected to increase 

dramatically if t here are no interventions by local, regional, and national governments. 60   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Although research on the movement of goods (particularly truck traffic in developed countries) captured 

a great deal of attention during the 1960s and 1970s, information remains scarce (Ogden 1992). 

Moreover, freight transport can vary greatly depending on the context and requires a comprehensive 

understanding of the different urban logistics activities (CEPAL 2015). 

Consequently, urban freight has not received the same at tention as passenger transport and is 

practically overlooked in transportation and urban development plans. In Peru, as well as other 

countries in the region, programs at the national and urban level that advocate for active and public 

transport for passen ger trips to increase liveability and mitigate climate change have widely ignored the 

role of urban freight in achieving these goals as well as bikes’ potential to transport goods in urban 

environments. 

E.2. 4. Stakeholders 

Urban logistics is known for bei ng complex because of the diversity of its stakeholders. As mentioned 

earlier, as opposed to passenger transport, urban logistics is extremely fragmented as it depends on 

the diverse economic activities of a city. 

Therefore, urban logistics decisions are likely to affect different interests such as businesses in the 

supply chain, consumers, and urban dwellers. Figure E.1 offers an indicative list of stakeholders in 

Peru, simplifying the complex framework of actors included in each category (for example, sh ippers and 

receivers) .61   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Figure E. 1 Urban logistics s takeholders 

> Source: Original elaboration

Thus, adequate policy design for urban logistics demands continuous consultation with and 

involvement of a wide range of actors in the private, public, and civil sectors to produce consensus -

based solutions. Regardless of the diversity of interests involved, sha red goals and objectives can be 

identified as all actors are affected by urban externalities like congestion and regulatory measures. 

Moreover, a business could be willing to implement progressive measures regarding sustainable 

transportation for diverse r easons like their own commitments to reduce emissions or to make their 

process es more cost efficient.

## E.3. Cyclelogistics: A solution for urban logistics 

Cyclelogistics is an effective solution for achieving carbon neutrality in urban freight and parcel delivery. 

It refers to the tra nsport of goods including, but not limited to, parcels, freight, and tools for service 

delivery, using bikes or tricycles, either human powered or assisted by zero -emission engines, with or 

without adapted compartments or trailers. Sustainable urban mobili ty activists have been asserting for 

over a decade that these vehicles could replace fossil fuel vehicles (LCVs, cars, motorcycles, vans, and 

even small trucks). 

Technical

on  the  means  of  transport  (types  of

to  plan  routes  like  dynamic  routing, 

traffic  controls,  and  real -time 

information  systems  (e .g., information 

and  communication  technology ).

central  areas  of  the  city

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Further, technological improvements to e -cargo bikes have overcome most problems of tradition al 

cargo bikes, such as low battery ranges, lower payloads, and driver fatigue. Today, e -cargo cycles can 

transport up to 50 –250 kg of cargo (with exceptions even up to 500 kg) and have battery ranges of 

50 –80 kilometers (km) (Narayanan and Antoniou 2022) .

Cyclelogistics is not a new phenomenon. Its origins trace back to the 1880s when these types of 

vehicles replaced animal traction to make the transport of goods more cost efficient for mail 

companies, small shops, and businesses, given the high costs of animal maintenance and their 

vulnerability to unforeseen events (Rzewnicki and Cox 2015). Also, as will be described later, 

cyclelogistics ha s been a common feature in Latin American cities for decades.

E.3.1. What it is and what it is not 

There is no clear universal definition of what is and what isn’t cyclelogistics, though many models are 

used for goods transport. The form and complexity of vehicles mainly derive from their purpose .

Nonetheless, the most used vehicles for cyclelogistics share the following common patterns: 

• Conventional bike (with rider bag or panniers), typi cally able to transport up to 25 kg. 

• Conventional bike with trailer, typically able to transport up to 70 kg. 

• Two -wheeled cargo bike, including storage container, generally able to transport up to 80 

kg/0.5 cubic meters (m 3). 

• Three - or four -wheeled cargo t rike or quad, including storage container, typically able to 

transport up to 500 kg 1.5 m 3 (Cairns and Sloman 2019) .

Figure E.3 Types of cargo bike 

> Source: Nurnberg 2019

Cargo bikes are subject to rapid development and innovation, resulting in a growing number of 

electrically assisted bike models, and standardization of containers, security options, track -and -trace 

systems, and battery technologies (Cairns and Sloman 2019). 

Though some consider cyclelogistics as a means of transport exclusively for goods, other approaches 

include workers carrying their own equipment or tools (Laboratorio de Mobilidade Sustentavel -

PROURB/UFRJ 2020). These commutes (known as service trips) represent one of the market segments 

for commercial e -cargo use identified by Rudolph and Gruber (2017) .65   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Further, private trips for shopping and leisure are also included in the Cyclelogistics Project funded by 

the E uropean Union , based on the argument that if these trips were made by a seller or a logistics 

operator, they would be considered freight or parcel delivery (Wrighton and Reiter 2016, 962). 

E.3.2. Why cyclelogistics? 

Cyclelogistics is considered a tool for partially solving the last -mile problem and the most negative 

externalities of urban logistics for several reasons. First, it does not emit pollutant gases and material 

in to the atmosphere, especially direct emissions. 21 According to a study ca rried out in the United 

Kingdom, CO 2 emissions related to e -cargo bikes are 4.5 grams of CO 2/km linked to electricity provision 

and around 280 kg of CO 2 in manufacture (Verlinghieri et al. 2021). Another study concluded that cars 

emit 11 times more polluta nts than bikes to deliver the same food parcels (Allen et al. 2021). 

As regards other pollutants, an earlier study commissioned by Transport for London concluded that 

“each light goods vehicle (LGV) replaced in central London saves over one ton of CO 2 and almost three 

kilograms of NOx per year.”  (Element Energy & WSP, 2017) . Furthermore, trips related to urban logistics 

are responsible for at least 30 –50 percent of NOx and PM emissions (Cairns and Sloman 2019; 

Bundesregierung 2 008), and transport of goods corresponds to a quarter of CO 2 emissions in urban 

areas (Dablanc and Rodriguez 2017), while it represents 72 —82 percent of total transportation 

emissions in developed economies (European Commission 2016; United States Environm ental 

Protection Agency 2019). 

These numbers could be even higher in developing countries due to the advanced age of many vehicles ;

however, in the case of Peru this issue has been targeted by policy as one of the main strategies to 

reduce GHG emissions. Furthermore, in Peru, road transport wa s responsible for 92.97 percent of total 

emissions from the transportation sector (MTC 2014), while transportation represented 33 .5 percent 

of total fuel combustion in 2014 (MINEM 2018). More surprisingly, LCVs (usual ly related to urban 

freight) emit 27.58 percent of total road transport emissions. 

Second, cargo cycles make more efficient use of parking spaces ( cur bside or parking lots) compared to 

motorized vehicles. For example, delivery vans reportedly spend nine mi nutes on average per trip to 

find a parking space and are often forced to block pavements or cycle lanes if they are unable to find 

one (Verlinghieri et al. 2021). 

Third, according to the European Cyclist Federation, cargo bikes have a comparable speed of cargo 

movement with motorized vehicles in city centers (14 .4 kilometer per hour [km/h] compared to 18 

km/h, respectively) (European Cyclists’ Federation 2012 a; Melo and Baptista 2016). Moreover, they 

can be driven in pedestrian -friendly , low -emission area s and can deliver outside the time frames of 

other freight vehicles, as they usually escape the restrictions imposed on motorized vehicles within city 

centers (CEPAL 2015). 

Fourth, cargo vehicles are more affordable than fossil fuel vehicles used for goods delivery, both in 

acquisition and maintenance costs. Table E.1 compares the prices of cargo bikes, electric -assisted 

pedal cycles (EAPC) bikes, and cargo EAPC trikes with small vans in the U nited Kingdom , which shows 

that savings in overall yearly expenses including acquisition can range from 74.8 percent to 92.4 

percent, depending on the model (Department for Transport 2019). Even if, in some cases, fleet 

replacements by e -cargo bikes represent high cost s for businesses, they result in substantial cost 

savings in the long run (Arvidsson and Pazirandeh 2017) .   

> 21 Indirect emissions depend on the city’s energy sources (coal, h ydroelectric, nuclear). 66
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Table E.1 Comparison of cargo vehicles characteristics and related costs 

> Source: UK Department of Transport 2019
> Note: EAPC = electric -assisted ped al cycle; kg = kilogram.

Finally, beyond the public benefits from cycling in terms of physical, mental, and social well -being, the 

cargo bike pilot carried out in Bogota reported that drivers ’ working time s decreased from 8 to 6 hours .

Also , 100 percent o f the participating drivers felt satisfied with the cargo bike experience as opposed 

to that of traditional motorized commercial vehicles, due to the physical activity involved, the time 

flexibility, and the ease of getting around the city (Prato and Reino so 2021). 

E.3.3. What kind of trips could be replaced? 

It should be noted that cyclelogistics cannot replace all vehicles in urban logistics. For example, 

oversized freight and construction materials need to be transported by heavier vehicles. Studies that 

have assessed the potential for switching goods transport to cargo bikes or similar vehicles have 

recognized that the biggest of these vehicles can carry a maximum of 200 –350 kg and 1.5 m 3 of load, 

and their trips do not normally exceed 5 –12 km (Campbell 2022; Figueroa and Polo 2022). Also, 

cyclelogistics cannot replace heavier vehicles for long -distance urban freight due to efficiency and cost 

constraints. 

Moreover, specific regulations state that the continuous rated power of e-cargo bikes should not exc eed 

250 watts (W). According to most legislation (including Peru’s), the maximum speed for bicycles with 

electric support remains below 25 km/h. 

However, research on the economic potential of replacement by cargo bikes and e -bikes has pointed 

to six poten tial opportunities in different sectors ( figure E.4). 67   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Figure E.4 Business cases for cyclelogistics  

> Source: Cairns and Sloman 2019 ,reproducing Maes 2018
> Note: CEP = courier, express, and parcel; B2B = business -to -business; B2C = business -to -consumer.

On the other hand, the replacement potential of LCVs by cargo bikes depends on the network 

configuration. Two network configurations have been identified in the literature on the subject, namely, 

direct scheme s and schemes with intermediate shifting (Narayanan and Antoniou 2022) . In direct 

schemes, g oods are taken directly from point A to point B (which is the final destination ). In the 

intermediate shifting scheme, the parcel or package is transfer red to an (e -) cargo bike from another 

vehicle in a transshipment facility. The mo re the transshipment infrastructure available in a city, the 

higher the potential for cyclelogistics to replace traditional vehicles. 

There are several types of transshipment facilities identified in the literature, like urban consolidation 

centers (UCCs), micro hubs and micro consolidation centers (MCCs), and transit points (TP s). While 

UCCs are specialized urban infrastructures for intermodal exchange or points of consolidation on an 

urban scale, MCCs are much more straightforward infrastructure at the neighborhood or district level 

that can be the size of a container or smaller . Both UCCs and MCCs have storage or consolidation 

purposes, while TPs are only used as a location for transfer ring cargo from a vehicle to a cyclelogistics 

vehicle (Narayana n and Antoniou 2022) . The possibility of shared use by multiple actors is a key 

element in the success of transshipment facilities, as will be discussed later. 

Moreover, several studies have assessed the number of trips that could be transferred to (e)cargo bikes 

and found that 10 –51 percent of motorized trips for goods transport could be replaced by cyclelogistics. 

Cargo bikes are particularly suitable for small, light consignments, which are currently on the rise ,

especially parcel deliveries to pr ivate customers ( business -to -consumer, B2C) (Bogdanski 2017; 

Cyclelogistics Project 2020). 

The EU’s Cyclelogistics Project published a report in 2015 observing that on average 51 percent of all 

motorized trips in urban areas —associated with the transport o f goods —could be shifted from a car to 

the bicycle or cargo bicycle. About one -third of these motorized trips could be attributed to commercial 

transport, whereas private logistics (e.g., transport for shopping or leisure) account ed for two -thirds 

(Wrighto n and Reiter 2016). However, it must be noted that these numbers were achieved considering 

secondary European cities (about 240,000 inhabitants). 

An earlier report by the European Cyclist s’ Federation found that 25 percent of all urban goods could 

be deli vered by cargo bicycles. This figure r ose to 50 percent when talking about light goods (European
