Cyclists’ Federation 2012 b). Moreover, according to research in the United Kingdom, cyclelogistics has 

the potential to replace perhaps 1.5 –7.5 percent of all urban vehicle mileage (Cairns and Sloman 

2019). 

Also, operators argue that the capacity of cyclelogistics to replace motorized trips also depends on the 

training provided to drivers, to help them adapt to the type of cargo they must carry and increase the 

amount of load they can transport safely (Collignon 2021). 

In contrast, more moderate estimations by Baptista and Melo found that cargo bikes can replace up to 

10 percent of conventional vans in areas with maximum linear distances of about 2 km , without 

ch anging the network’s overall efficiency. Furthermore, the introduction of e -cargo bikes in urban 

logistics has positive effects on all vehicle categories and scenarios, reducing external costs by up to 

25 percent. However, Baptista and Melo’s report depart s from the assumption that cyclelogistics 

operations are limited to maximum linear distances of 2 km. This assumption could be contested by 

Peruvian operators and the Cyclelogistics Ahead project that report deliveries in a 7 –12 km range. In 

the case of Bogota’s “BiciCarga” pilot project, the four models of operation (explained further in section 

E. 3.4) had distances rang ing from 2 to 7 km. Further, limitations of the cycle road network could also 

limit the reach of cyclelogistics. 

On the other hand, res earch on the economic sustainability of cargo bikes replacing LCVs in the CEP 

market found that it could lead to a higher average cost per delivery. However, bicycle transport could 

be attractive and competitive with LCV transport in low -value markets. Fur thermore, Maes argues that 

research on cyclelogistics has mainly focused on “technicalities, dissemination or environmental and 

social aspects. The publications on the market demand, i.e., the characteristics of supply and demand 

of bicycle courier deliver y services, delivery costs, external effects (costs and benefits for society) and 

market behavior are rather limited” (Maes 2017). 

The most critical concerns about the replacement of LCV s by bikes/cycles are linked to their efficiency 

and cost -effectivenes s. First, the bikes’ payload weight and volume are less than that of vans, th us 

limiting the carriable loads and potential uses. Simultaneously, the theoretical lower speed of cycles 

makes them unappealing, especially in modelling scenarios that do not tak e into account the actual 

speeds of LCVs in highly congested urban environments or the ability of bikes to use bike lanes. Bikes 

also seem unsuitable to cover long distances and access depots usually located on cities’ outskirts due 

to their lower speeds (Leonardi, Browne, and Allen 2012). 

In conclusion, evidence supports the case for the implementation of cyclelogistics in urban 

environments based on its social and environmental advantages. But research on its economic 

feasibility from the perspective of b usinesses remains scarce, especially in the Latin American region. 

E.3.4. Successful international experiences 

There are many successful international experiences in implementing cargo bikes and e -bikes, 

especially in Europe and Latin America. These exper iences, initiated either in the private or public 

sector, have made a case for implementing cargo bikes in urban logistics in different contexts and 

business models. 

Europe 

There are a wide range of European experiences in using cargo bikes in urban logist ics, especially in 

countries with a rich bike culture like the Netherlands, Denmark, and Germany. While listing all of them 

exceeds the span of this report, it is crucial to review experiences supported by the public sector and 

companies who have implement ed cargo bikes, either as part of their environmental sustainability 69   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

commitments or for cost and operational efficiencies. An example is DHL, which has included 

cyclelogistics as part of its business model in Germany, Austria, Belgium, Greece, Italy, and t he United 

Kingdom. Moreover , about 10 percent of its fleet in the Netherlands is made up of cargo bikes —totaling 

more than 26 ,000 bikes of which 9 ,000 are e -bikes or trikes (DHL 2015). The company has also started 

a pilot project in Mexico City .

The first important experience promoted by the public sector is Berlin’s KoMoDo project. The Senate of 

Berlin implemented electric cargo bikes to optimize urban logistics to achieve environmental, urban, 

and mobility objectives. The project was delivered t hrough the cooperative use of micro depots by the 

courier, express, and parcel industries for the sustainable use of cargo bicycles with a 1.4 m 3 container. 

To this end, the five largest parcel service providers in Germany jointly used a transfer point at the city 

center equipped with micro depots to supply an area with a remarkably high demand for goods delivery 

by cargo bikes. 

The micro depot project aims to develop and test sustainable solutions for delivery traffic in urban 

areas. The focus is on open a nd cooperative provider solutions to optimize the scarce space in the city 

center. The project is a building block to make delivery traffic in Berlin and other municipalities bicycle 

friendly (Department of the Senate for the Environment, Transport and Cli mate Protection 2020). 

As a result of this initiative, the partners saved about 11 tonnes of CO 2 compared to conventional 

delivery vehicles, using up to 11 cargo bikes. In total, more than 38,000 km were covered. 

As part of the model project, around 160,0 00 parcels were delivered in the past year by the five largest 

parcel service providers in Germany through cargo bikes in the vicinity of the micro depots. The 

participating service providers could continuously increase the delivered parcel volumes during the 

project (Smart City Berlin 2019). 

Photo E.3 KoMoDo project  

> Source: © Sina Schuldt/DPA; https://www.rippl.bike/en/rippl -48 -komod o-cooperative -micro -depot -opens -in -berlin/

On the other hand, the city has implemented subsidies for acquiring electric cargo bicycles. In 2018, 

the Senate Department for Environment, Transport, and Climate Protection launched an extensive 

€200,000 finan cing program to purchase cargo bikes. 70   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Another example of national public policies is the project “I replace a car” (originally “Ich ersetze ein 

Auto”) financed by the Ministry of the Environment, Nature Conservation and Nuclear Safety in Germany 

and carri ed out between 2012 and 2014 (photo E.4) . Preliminary data from this project suggest that 

42 percent of deliveries could be made by cargo bikes at 19 percent of the mileage (CEPAL 2015). 

Photo E.4 Ich ersetze ein Auto  

> Source: © Si na Schuldt/DPA; https://www.rippl.bike/en/rippl -48 -komodo -cooperative -micro -depot -opens -in -berlin/

Another exciting experience in Europe is that of San Sebastian , a city in Spain. Since the local 

government -imposed restrictions in 2009 on freight transport from 11:30 a. m. in the Old Town, new 

companies emerged that offered cargo bikes to make deliveries (from documents to 20 parcels at a 

time ) in the area . This service is now used by several traditional freight and parcel transporters. One of 

the companies offering cycle logistics estimates that its operations avoid the entry of 15 trucks into the 

restricted area (Medina 2021). Just like Berlin, the case of San Sebastian is interesting because of the 

engagement of the public sector, which has strategically placed micro dep ots to improve efficiencies in 

last -mile delivery in the Old Town through cargo bikes. 

While no specific information was found about emission s reduction from cyclelogistics , the sustained 

growth of some operators since 2009 is evidence of the efficiency and convenience of cargo bikes in 

dense urban areas. One company that report ed 600 trips per month deliver ed approximately 19 ,700 

kg, showing a 92 percent and 85 percent incr ease in trips and kilograms carried, respectively 

(DACHSER 2021). 

Another critical case is PedalMe in London. The company collected data from its 37 -cargo -bike fleet in 

September 2020 and reported the direct logistical advantages. In summary, it found tha t within a 3 –5

miles radius of the center, bikes are likely to move significantly faster than vans or cars as they can 

move past stationary traffic, are allowed to use bus lanes, and benefit from separat e cycling 

infrastructure. With increasing congestion levels following growing motorization on the one hand, and 

more restricted lanes and increasing investment in cycling infrastructure, the advantages will continue 

to increase. 

Moreover, PedalMe reports shorter trip lengths, as on average, bike trips were 6 percent faster than 

car trips. Interestingly, when looking at trips shorter than 5 km, the journeys were on average 8 percent 

shorter, with 25 percent of trips being at least 10 percent shorter in terms of distance (Collignon 2021). 71   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Further, since the fi rst COVID -19 lockdown, the United Kingdom has reported that 450 independent 

businesses are delivering their products on e -cargo bikes. The rise of these types of deliveries comes 

from industry ’s and consumers ’ perception that this is the “greener” option. One company reports 

having made in total 6,500 deliveries and pickups for local companies, cycling over 10,000 km and 

saving over 2,300 kg of CO 2 (Hill 2022) .

Photo E.5 PedalMe in London  

> Source: PedalMe. https://pedalme.co.uk/

Latin America 

Latin American experiences are also relevant when assessing the potential of cyclelogistics, but their 

differences with European experiences must be noted. Developing economies show a complex urban 

logistics ecosystem where informal commerce (nano business es ) coexists with modern and structured 

business es . The number of nano businesses in Latin America is exceptionally high. In Mexico City, for 

example, Coca -Cola supplies approximate ly 1.2 million points of sale and Unilever delivers ice cream 

to more than 10,000 freezers (Blanco and Fransoo 2013) , whereas Bogotá has around 140,000 nano 

stores (Jallow and Johansson 2015). 

However, nano businesses are far from being a barrier to sustai nable urban logistics. On the contrary, 

small formal and informal businesses have been using a wide range of artisanal cargo bikes for 

decades, making them a common feature of everyday life in the region. 72   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Photo E.6 Cargo trike in Bogota  

> Source: © Claudio Olivares Medina; https://www.flickr.com/photos/quiltro/5187897449

For example, a study on goods delivery using bicycles conducted in Rio de Janeiro in 2011 identified 

732 vehicles (including bikes and tricycles with features adapted to the kind of goods transported) 

making around 23,000 daily trips. Similarly, a study in Sao Paulo in 2013 of a total of 1, 701 shops in 

a commercial district, f ound that 41 percent performed their own account delivery and 16 .3 percent 

used bikes or tricycles. However, few data are available on the patterns, volumes transported, and 

efficiency of the region’s active transport vehicles (Laboratorio de Mobilidade Sustentavel -

PROURB/UFRJ 2020). 

One of the cities with more available data on urban logistics is Bogota. According to a study conducted 

in 2015, the city received about 17,000 delivery tr ucks every day. As a particular feature perhaps 

shared with Lima, the city works as a vast consolidation center for the whole country, regardless of the 

origin or destination of the goods. Moreover, there are more than 140,000 nano businesses and 

100,000 p oints of distribution in Bogota’s metropolitan area (Jallow and Johansson 2015). 

As in the rest of the world, most of these deliveries are done by motorized vehicles operating below 

their capacity, generating high operational and logistical costs. However , a significant amount of 

delivery activity is carried out by motorcycles, rickshaws, private vehicles, and bikes. Even though the 

use of bikes for commercial delivery purposes is a common feature, even among big food 

manufacturers, social perceptions link ing bike s to low -income groups and associating motorized 

transportation as a symbol of status has been acknowledged in part as a barrier to considering the bike 

as an effective, sustainable urban mobility tool (CEPAL 2015). 

However, as negative externaliti es derived from urban logistics have worsened with the emergence of 

new business models and the rise of e -commerce and e -delivery, public authorities have turned their 

attention recently to the potential of cargo bikes. The city of Bogota’s Secretariat of Mobility, together 

with nongovernmental organizations and the support of the World Bank, carried out a pilot program of 

cargo bikes to promote sustainable alternatives to solve problems related to final goods distribution. 

The project involved four compani es from different economic sectors: an e -commerce logistical 

operator, a small and medium enterprise manufactur ing food, a large food manufacturer, and a parcel 

delivery operator. Each of the companies substituted a fossil -fuel -motorized vehicle with a car go bike, 

as shown in table E.2. 73

There is a positive outcome in terms of operational efficiency, with number of deliveries made per hour 

increas ing by up to 67 percent and the effectiveness of deliveries increas ing from 93 percent to 97 

percent. 

Compared to motorcycles, bicycles have an advantage in load capacity and average daily distance 

traveled. It was found that the operation s of a one -ton truck can be replaced by two tricycles under a 

model of collaborative consolidation and distribution operation, eve n with less operation time, which 

means that, for a scalability exercise, two trucks could be replaced by three cargo tricycles. More 

importantly, the project found that 1.97 tons of CO 2 emissions were avoided during the pilot project 

(Prato and Reinoso 20 21). 

While there is growing interest in cyclelogistics in many countries of the region, including renewed offers 

from cargo bikes manufacturers and retailers, the BiciCarga project in Bogota is reportedly the first pilot 

project of its type in Latin Ameri ca.

Another interesting case is the municipality of Sao Paulo, Brazil, which recently passed a municipal law 

for cyclelogistics that promot es , stimulat es , and monitor s sustainable logistics. The regulation orders 

pr ivate and public bike racks to a ccommodate cargo bikes. The law states that private and public 

buildings and public road space s should implement zones for delivery that are adapted for cargo bikes. 

The law also includes some protection for deliver y persons by demanding that bike delivery companies 

provide them with refreshment stations, toilets, phone charging points, and other infrastructure. 

Similarly, it states that companies should train deliver y persons in road safety. More importantly, it 

demands that bike delivery companies share data with the city to help in formulating public policy for 

cyclelogistics (Camara Municipal de Sao Paulo 2020). 

E.3.5. Challenges 

The implementation of cyclelogistics has faced several obstacles such as regulatory issues, 

in frastructure, successful stakeholder engagement, urban planning, data management, vehicle 

security, and labor. We address each of these below. 

Legal and regulatory frameworks 

First, policies that explicitly encourage specific emission reduction targets for urban logistics are 

essential for decisive steps toward carbon neutrality. These plans must consist of a clearly defined 

vision and set goals, and accordingly, a set of actions and implementable measures. The program 

should also offer a target to measure and track the progress to ward achiev ing specific objectives. 

For example, Maes identified that the European midterm policy of reducing the use of conventionally 

fueled cars in urban transport by half by 2030 and the aim of CO 2 free city logistics depicted in a 2011 

white paper was influential at the national, state, and local levels (Maes 2017). 

Even if the policy review carried out in Peru concludes that national and local policy should aim at 

decreasing pollutant emissions fro m the transport sector, these goals have not been appropriately 

articulated in plans for urban logistics, despite its immense impact on urban environments. Considering 

urban logistics in sustainable urban mobility plans, land use plans, and other mid - to long -term plans is 

necessary for decisive actions. 75   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

On the other hand, the legal framework determines the range of tools that local, regional, and national 

governments have to promote sustainable urban logistics. As mentioned before, authorities can take 

var ious measures to create restrictions for fueled motorized vehicles or mitigate their impact on urban 

environments (e.g., zero -emission areas, truck bans, parking and loading areas, emission standards). 

However, these measures must be backed by legal develo pments to enforce them . The implementation 

of restrictive measures affects the costs and operations of traditional logistic operators and therefore 

has a huge political cost, making politicians hesitant to adopt them .

Regulatory interventions could be the most impactful in decarbonizing urban logistics. Even if the 

measures are purely regulatory (time access restrictions, parking regulation s, emission standards) or 

include market -based measures (pricing, taxation, subsidies), they create economic advantage s for 

zero -emission vehicles. For example, establishment of low emission areas and congestion charges have 

resulted in operational and cost benefits for cargo bikes in London and San Sebastian. Further, the 

reviewed literature identified central business d istricts and historical centers with motorized transport 

restrictions as one of the main enablers of cyclelogistics. 

However , the absence of specific regulations for cargo bikes and the lack of categorization among cargo 

vehicles creates a normative vacuu m that prevents many formal operators from using them . For 

example, many actors declined to participate in the BiciCargo pilot program , arguing that it entailed a 

regulatory risk to use vehicles that have not been authorized for cargo (Prato and Reinoso 20 21, 43). 

Further, th is normative vacuum prev ents cyclelogistics operators from insuring their vehicles and 

merchandise and offer ing formal cargo services in accordance with national regulations. 

Infrastructure 

The lack of adequate infrastructure affects the implementation of cyclelogistics. It is a threefold 

problem that involves infrastructure for bikes/cycles , electric charge points, and micro depots. 

The higher the number of interconnected bike lanes a city has, the more it can benefit from 

cyclelogisti cs. In addition, there should be uniformity in bike infrastructure design and maintenance to 

ensure safety and accessibility standards throughout the whole network. The World Bank Bike 

Infrastructure Plan Proposal 23 set standards for Lima and Callao (World Bank 2020) , stating that should 

be :

• Coherent (complete, well connected, signalized with clear messages) .

• Direct (minimizing detours and delays) .

• Safe .

• Comfortable for users ( generating minimal air and noise pollution). 

• Attractive and integrated with urban landmarks and green areas. 

• Generat ing socioeconomic value by proximity to urban landmarks and commercial zones. 

On the other hand, mixed traffic is a barrier due to safety concerns. Physically segregated bike l anes 

are preferable for roads with high volumes of traffic and effective speeds beyond 50 km /h. Likewise, 

traffic pacification measures must be adopted for streets with speed limits below 30 –40 km/h to 

prevent speed ing and hazards to cyclists. The better the cycling infrastructur e, the heavier the bicycles 

that can be used , the higher the load capacit ies, and the greater the possibilities of carry ing out 

deliveries. Specifically, lanes for cargo bikes should be at least 2 meters wide, and ideally 2.25 meters 

for cycle lanes and 2. 4 meters to provide access to all models of cargo bikes.    

> 23 https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/294041589874919754/pdf/Propuesta -de -actualizacion -del -Plan -de -Infraestructura -Cicloviaria -para -
> Lima -y-Callao.pdf 76
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Though cyclelogistics has become popular because of its capacity to escape traffic jams, the 

operational efficiencies of these vehicles decrease the more they deal with congested streets. Another 

fea ture of bike infrastructure that interferes with goods movement is the need for constant stops in 

urban goods distribution. For example, a soft drink company in Colombia report ed having distribution 

routes with more than 100 stops per day (Blanco and Frans oo 2013). Even if bike lanes are available, 

cyclelogistics requires constant stops to deliver goods, interfering with common bike infrastructure. 

Companies using cargo bikes for delivery see the span of their operation s limited by the need to 

recharge vehicles. Whereas the two Peruvian cyclelogistics operators interviewed for this report charge 

their vehicles at their own facilities or their consumers ’, both agree that having fast -charging points 

distributed throughout t he city could expand the ir areas of operation. However, this problem is expected 

to improve as technological advances enhance the autonomy of batteries. 

Moreover, micro depots (MCC s), UCC s, and TP s are critical to the success of cyclelogistics as a last -mile 

solution. Logistics encompasses more than transportation ; it also contemplates the movement of goods 

in space (transport) and in time (storage) . Both actions are closely related, and their organization must 

be programmed in an integrated manner (Barber o, Fiadone, and Millan Placci 2020). Accordingly, the 

EU’s Cylelogistics project argues that “ Cities aiming at promoting cycle logistics models could secure 

space for micro -hubs in policy, planning and zoning strategies, identify and adapt some key public 

spaces for micro -consolidation purposes… as well as testing low -cost mobile micro -hubs and shared 

facilities in cooperation with cycle logistics companies .”  (Chiffi & Reiter, 2015) .

These premises require space for short -term s torage and sorting of goods; freight -cycle entry/exit and 

secure parking; the parking and maneuvering space needed for a larger freight vehicle; loading and 

unloading facilities; and potentially an office and a rest space for riders (Cairns and Sloman 2019 ),

In San Sebastian, Berlin, and Bogotá, micro depots are the point of consolidation for goods that will be 

delivered through cargo bikes. The more strategically located these points are, the more efficient 

cyclelogistics can be. Though c yclelogistics oper ators in Latin America have established these facilities 

by themselves, it would be a great stimulus if local authorities provided this infrastructure, as in the 

case of San Sebastian and Berlin (Campbell 2022). The high cost of land is a barrier to this i ncentive ,

as strategic locations for micro depots are usually in dense residential and commercial areas. 

Stakeholder engagement 

Most policy papers, reports, and academic articles conclude that stakeholder engagement and public -

private collaboration are cr itical for the successful implementation of cyclelogistics and, more generally, 

public policy for urban logistics. For example, shared micro -deposits for various private operators 

located in strategically located areas have made the case for cyclelogistics in the last -mile urban 

logistics in pilot deployments in Berlin and Bogota. 

As highlighted in section E. 2, given the highly fragmented nature of urban logistics, there can be very 

different and even contradictory interests in implementing public policy. Some actors may not be willing 

to cooperate if they do not see a direct benefit (CEPAL 2015). However, empirical e vidence shows that 

cyclelogistics could benefit the public interest, actors in the supply chain, relevant associations, and 

civil society if adequate planning processes are in place (CityChangerCargoBike 2021 ; ICLEI -Local 

Governments for Sustainability 202 1). The cases of San Sebastian and Berlin are excellent examples 

of successful public -private participation .

Even if the operational demands of the fast -growing parcel and freight delivery services are not satisfied 

completely during the early development of cyclelogistics , stakeholders could also be looking to add 77   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

social and environmental value to their services as consumers sometimes consider these aspects in 

their choices (Figueroa and Polo 2022; Campbell 2022). 

Another barrier in Latin America is the lack of organized groups in the logistics sector —due to low levels 

of professionalization and formality ( Departamento Nacional de Planeación de Colombia 2015) —that 

could eventually articulate with public authorities for the formulation of public policy, as well as the fact 

that only very few formal companies are implementing cyclelogistics in their processes. In the case of 

Peru, over 80 percent of the transport sector workers are informal, and transport represents about 36 

percent of total informal busines ses in the country (Comercio 2019). 

Equally important is to promote information exchange among stakeholders. As data about successful 

and unsuccessful instances of cyclelogistics implementation become available, the economic, 

environmental, and social bene fits and downsides of cyclelogistics will become clearer to decision 

makers and actors in the supply chain. 

Urban planning 

Despite the importance of urban logistics and its impact on cities, planning instruments do not address 

goods movement. Even when cit ies have plans for urban freight, these are not articulated in sustainable 

urban mobility plans or land use plans. Furthermore, during the 20th century, planning paradigms like 

zoning and suburbanization have increased the need for motorized transportation due to the long 

distances between origin and destination points. 

Where c ontemporary planning and land use approaches promote transit -oriented development (TOD) 

(through densification, mixed use, and compactness, etc.), the need for considering urban logis tics is 

further increased, as a wide range of needs and modes of transport will share limited space. Curbside 

space, for example, is crucial when considering public road space as it will become indispensable for 

parcel and freight delivery with the rise of e-commerce add ing to the existent demand of businesses 

and consumers. 

The cities of the Global South face additional challenges. Countries like Peru or Colombia have 

concentrated most of their infrastructure investments in road networks for geographic, ec onomic, and 

political reasons. This has consequences for logistics as there is a significant dependency on vehicular 

transport for goods movement and passenger transport. Unsurprisingly, there is a considerable lag in 

intermodal exchange infrastructure and specialized logistics infrastructure (Sanchez and Wilmsmeier 

2006). 

Consequently, the concentration of investments in road infrastructure at the national level is reflected 

in urban environments, which do not have the adequate specialized infrastructure for intermodal 

exchange that could avoid oversized and highly pollutant vehicles in dense urban areas for goods 

movement, as well as satisfactory mobility services for passengers in peripheral regions. 

In simplistic terms, a lack of adequate planning proce sses amid accelerated and uncontrolled 

demographic growth during the 20th century led to the proliferation of informal and formal settlements 

on the geographic peripheries of cities . Consequently, cities were not able to provide specific 

infrastructure suc h as peripherical ring road s to prevent heavy vehicles from entering cities or facilities 

for the consolidation and deconsolidation of cargo on the outskirts of town s (Departamento Nacional 

de Planeación 2020; MTC 2016). 

To mitigate the negative externalities of goods transport and to achieve carbon neutrality, the 

implementation of strategies like cyclelogistics need to be articulated with in planning instruments for 78   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

land use and mobility so as to rationalize distance travel and the use of public space s (CIVITAS 2020; 

ICLEI - Local Governments for Sustainability 2021). 

Data 

The problem of data and cyclelogistics has two sides. First, cities generally do not provide quality data 

regarding cycling infrastructure required for the planning of trips. Data should include not only on the 

availability of bike infrastructure but also its characteristics ( e.g., separate la nes, shared lanes, mixed 

traffic). Companies operating cyclelogistics in Peru have identified the lack of geo -referenced data on 

bike infrastructure as an obstacle to planning the availability of their service s (Campbell 2022; Figueroa 

and Polo 2022). 

On t he other hand, cities do not have enough information about the travel patterns and characterization 

of cyclists to plan for cycling infrastructure and produce public policy to encourage a modal shift to 

active mobility and cyclelogistics. 

As cities come t o understand the value of the data generated through shared mobility systems, 

agreements with operators typically include the sharing of operational information. However, data 

exchanges face significant limitation s due to user privacy and personal data pro tection norms. 

Safety, security, and vehicle quality standards 

Road safety is o ne of the biggest concerns of cyclists and those hesitating to shift to bike s (Melo and 

Baptista 2016). How b ike and road infrastructure is affected by poor weather condition s, sociocultural 

perceptions of car ownership being a privilege, and mixed traffic in busy streets are other concerns .

One of the main obstacles to the implementation of cargo bikes is companies’ concern regarding the 

safety of their workers and cargo (Allen et al. 2021; Leach 2009). Therefore , cyclelogistics companies 

have introduced training for drivers to help them be professional, predictable, and communicative 

actors on the road (Collignon 2021). However, improvements in cycling infrastructure and road s afety 

awareness are still key to incentivizing modal shifts in goods transport. 

Also , perceptions of urban insecurity are a significant concern as s ome actors consider cargo bikes to 

be more vulnerable to criminal activity than are motorized vehicles. However, vehicle standardization 

and technological advances like GPS tracking could help mitigate these concerns in part, as long as 

public policy is specially tailored for cargo bike security. 

Regarding standardization, creating minim um technical norms for cargo bike manufacturing is key to 

mitigating safety and security concerns and adequate biking infrastructure planning. Europe is at the 

vanguard , while few rules have been established in Latin America. In the case of Colombia, for e xample, 

despite the exist ence of informal cargo bike manufacturers for the last 30 years, there is no 

industrialization or professionalization of the production process. On the other hand, the European 

Committee for Standardisation (CEN) has established an EN standard for cargo bikes, with a working 

group that includes important manufacturers of cargo bikes in the region. National industry standards 

for cargo bikes established in Germany (DIN 79010:2020 -02) and France (NF R30 -050 -1) serve as a 

basis for the EN standard (Project 2021). 

For the expansion of cyclelogistics, maintenance and repair capacities must be developed. In Bogota’s 

cargo bike pilot, one of the reported issues was the availability of only one licensed operator to handle 

repairs for the wh ole program. Local governments can become enablers by training the existing informal 

network of mechanics , in order to increase accessibility to maintenance and technical support for e-

cargo bikes. 79   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Moreover, due to the relative novelty of cargo bikes and their added safety and security concerns, 

cyclelogistics companies have difficulties finding insurance for their operations. Some Latin -American 

companies have been forced to look for insurance abroad. 

Labor and gender 

Compliance with labor rights is an a dded operational concern. E -delivery platforms have operated under 

the scheme followed by some ride -hailing companies where delivery persons (often referred to as 

“independent partners”) earn a small commission for each delivery with no further benefits li ke health 

insurance, risk assurance, or pensions. Platforms argue that deliver y persons are independent workers 

in a collaborative economy model. 

However, others argue that these workers do not have other sources of income and devote their 

economic activit y to delivery, asserting that lawful labor schemes should cover them. Furthermore, 

there are no restriction s to their working hours and no protection against arbitrary firing. In Latin 

America, many of these workers are young , undocumented immigrants not c overed by social security 

schemes. 

For platforms to share the costs of health and risk insurance with independent workers has been 

proposed in some countries of the region (Portafolio 2020; Torres Duran 2022) . Two interviewed 

cyclelogistics operators in P eru highlighted that part of their added social value is the fact that their 

drivers are hired as workers with all mandated benefits and receive training and equipment (Campbell 

2022; Figueroa and Polo 2022). 

The phenomenon is not exclusive to Latin Ameri ca, as North America and Europe have witnessed the 

emergence of self -employed individuals paid on a per -delivery basis. This has led to an abundance of 

cycle couriers in many major cities. Deliveroo has over 100,000 couriers working for it, most of which 

are based in the United Kingdom (Business of Apps 2022). 

Concerned voices have emerged, claiming that the “gig economy” is fueled by exploitation and not 

through innovation . S ignificant rulings in Europe and the U nited States have been made against ride -

ha iling and gig economy companies (Temperton 2018). Another relevant challenge is that women are 

underrepresented (5 –7 percent) and are perceived vulnerab le to theft and assault (REVISTA APOLICE 

2022) .

## E.4. The situation of Peru 

E.4.1. Legal, regulatory , and institutional framework 

This section consists of a brief review of existing laws related to urban logistics, followed by a review of 

the relevant public policy documents. The institutional framework of urban logistics will then be 

presented with each actor’s competencies. 

Legal framework 

Probably the law that is most related to the topic of cyclelogistics is Law No. 30936 of 2020 .24 This 

states that the MTC must develop and implement public policies for the design of road infrastructure 

work s and promote urban and rural planning , favoring the use of the bicycle as a sustainable, efficient 

means of transport that contributes to the preservation of the environment, in coordination with public 

entities at the national, regional, and local govern ment levels.     

> 24 Law that Promotes and Regulates the Use of the Bicycle as a Means of Sustainable Transport: https://busquedas.elperuano.pe/normaslegales/ley -
> que -promueve -y-regula -el -uso -de -la -bicicleta -como -medio -ley -n-30936 -1762977 -4/ .80
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Meanwhile , the National Institute of Statistics and Informatics (INEI) must coordinate the produc tion 

basic statistics on the use of bicycle s through censuses, sample surveys, and administrative records. 

The law promotes the progressive adapt ation of existing urban and transport infrastructure to facilitate 

bicycle s as a sustainable and efficient means of transportation, establishing it as a matter of national 

interest and public necessity. 

The law modifie s past norms, prioritizing the means of transport that show more efficiency in the use 

of public road space and the protection of the environment .

Moreover, the law authorizes regional and local governments to use, after technical, economic, and 

legal evaluation, the mechanism ¨Obr as por impuestos ¨ - Work for Taxes 25 , with the purpose of 

adaptation of the existing urban and transport infrastructure and construction of cycle paths to facilitate 

the use of bicycles, following current urban road plans .

The law also addresses intermodal transport, stating that the authorities at the three levels of 

government must implement measures that facilitate intermodal transport for cyclist s to connect their 

journeys with other means of transport. Likewise, it enab les safe spaces and structures for bicycle 

parking at mass transportation stations. Furthermore, the law encourages competent authorities to 

manage the interconnectivity of cycle paths when these involve two or more districts of contiguous local 

government s. 

The law states that public and private entities must adapt their spaces for bicycle parking in 5 percent 

of the area they allocate to motor vehicles. The provincial and district municipalities are responsible for 

executi ng the mandatory application of t his norm in existing and new constructions. 

In addition, the law orders local governments to implement public bicycle systems, consisting of bicycle 

rental or loan, prioritizing interconnection to mass transportation systems to encourage the use of 

bicycl es among citizens. 

The regulation of this law was made through Supreme Decree N o. 012 -2020 -MTC ,26 which states that 

the MTC must develop and implement public policies for the design of road infrastructure work to favor 

the use of bicycles. 

In addition, i t provides the following rights for bike users: 

• Travel along the country’s public roads, with respect and safety, except in those cases where 

the current infrastructure puts their safety at risk. 

• Have preferential circulation routes within the cities, suc h as cycle paths or similar spaces. 

• Have enough space for bicycle parking, both in public and private areas. 

• Access free public bicycle parking. 

• Receive days or hours of preferential circulation in the urban area on specific routes. 

The regulation also implements measures addressing other road actors to protect bike users, like 

forbidding parking in cycle lanes and other bike infrastructure.      

> 25 The Works for Taxes mechanism is an opportunity for the publ ic and private sectors to work hand in hand to reduce the existing infrastructure gap in
> the country. Through this mechanism, private companies advance the payment of their income tax to directly finance and execut e, quickly and efficiently,
> public investm ent projects that subnational governments and national government entities prioritize.

> reglamento -de -la -ley -n-30936 -decreto -supremo -no -012 -2020 -mtc -1867296 -1/ .81
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Addressing transport in general, Law 29370 of Organization and Functions of the Ministry of Transports 

and Communi cations establishes that the MTC collaborates with regional and local governments in 

transportation services of regional and local scope, circulation , and road transport. 

Furthermore, the MTC’s governing functions include formulating, planning, directing, coordinating, 

executing, supervising, and evaluating the national and sectoral polic ies applicable to all levels of 

government, and coordinating with regional and local governments in the implementation of national 

and sectoral policies, and evaluat ing the ir compliance. Likewise, within its shared competencies, the 

MTC provides technical support to regional and local governments. 

On the other hand, Law 27181 , the General Law of Transport and Road Traffic ,27 establishes that state 

action in transportation an d road traffic is oriented toward satisfying the needs of users and 

safeguarding their health and safety conditions, as well as protecting the environment and the 

community as a whole. Moreover, it indicates that the MTC is the governing body at the nation al level 

in matters of transport and land traffic and reaffirms its role as a technical assistance provider for 

municipalities and regions. 

Likewise, National Transport Administration Regulations ( approved by Supreme Decree No. 017 -2009 -

MTC) contain genera l provisions that classify the different modes of transportation services for people 

and goods, as well as the technical suitability requirements like characteristics of the fleet, 

infrastructure of the company, and its organization, as well as the quality and safety conditions of each 

of them. 

Regarding road traffic, the National Traffic Code ( approved by Supreme Decree No. 016 -2009 -MTC), 

establishes relevant definitions for the circulation of vehicles and people, designating the competent 

authorities in traffic matters (MTC, provincial and district municipalities, National Police of Peru, and 

Indecopi) and the scope of attributions. In addition, it establishes requirements for the circulation of 

motor vehicles, rules for the use of public roads for driver s of all types of vehicles and pedestrians, 

traffic violations, and sanctioning procedures, among other matters. 

Regarding the competencies of municipalities, Law No. 27972 , the “Organic Law of Municipalities ,”

states that municipalities, in matters of tra ffic, roads, and public transport, regulat e the public service 

of urban land transport within their jurisdiction, following the national laws and regulations on the 

matter. Moreover, according to the law, municipalities have exclusive or shared powers and specific 

functions regarding roads, traffic, circulation, and public transport. Furthermore, they have powers 

regarding planning, regulation, operation, inspection, and control of urban transport in each city. 

Municipalities are also in charge of the traff ic signaling systems in their jurisdiction, supervision, 

detection of infractions, and their execution. They are also competent in the organization of physical 

space and land use, especially approving territorial conditioning plans and urban development pl ans. 

Regarding regional governments, Law No. 27867 establishes and regulates the structure, organization, 

powers, and functions of regional governments. Regarding transport, regional governments can 

formulate, approve, execute, evaluate, direct, control, and manage the plans and policies regarding 

transport in the region according to national policies and sectoral plans. 

The Law of Bases for Decentralization ( No. 27783) sets the decentralization process ’s purpose, 

principles, objectives, and general criteria. Furthermore, it regulates the formation o f regions and 

municipalities, setting the powers of the three levels of government and determining the assets and     

> 27 General Law of Transportation and Land Traffi c: https://www.gob.pe/institucion/congreso -de -la -republica/normas -legales/9868 -27181 .82
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

resources of regional and local governments, and coordinates government relations at their different 

levels. 

The law also clarifies that the d esign of national and sectoral policies are exclusive competencies of 

the national government. Exclusive competencies of municipalities include planning and promoting 

urban and rural development in their jurisdictions and executing the corresponding plans. Shared 

competencies include collective transport, circulation, and urban transit. 

Another relevant actor is the Ministry of Housing, Construction, and Sanitation (MVCS). The Law of 

Organization and Functions of the Ministry of Housing, Construction and Sa nitation define general 

functions such as to dictate rules and guidelines for the ordering and integration of urban and rural 

population centers at the national level, as well as conurbation processes, urban expansion, and the 

creation and resettlement of populated centers, in a coordinated, articulated , and cooperative manner 

with other agencies of the executive power, and regional and local governments. 

Moreover, it also promotes, develops, contributes, and regulates mobility infrastructure in urban and 

rural areas, as articulated by regional and local governments. Among its shared functions are to 

regulate, approve, execute , and supervise national polici es on urban planning and development, urban 

development, and buildings, and use and occupation of urban and developable land, in accordance 

with the laws and organizations of regional governments and municipalities. 

At the regulatory level, Supreme Decree No. 012 -2019 -MTC approved the National Urban Transport 

Policy (PNTU) , in which the deficiencies of urban public transport have been identified. Among them 

are: 

• A lack of comprehensive planning of urban transport services. 

• The low quality of public transpor t and the prevalence of informality. 

• Weakness in the organization of formal transport service. 

• Inadequate maintenance and renewal of v ehicle fleet s.

• Inadequate and insufficient infrastructure for urban transport including urban roads, bus 

stops, and interc hange terminals; and 

• The institutional weakness of local governments in urban transport management, among 

others. 

This national policy has the general objective of providing cities with safe, reliable transportation 

systems with high -quality standards that are institutionally coordinated and financially, economically, 

and environmentally sustainable. For this, it sets among its prioritized objectives hav ing efficient public 

urban transport systems for the movement of people, improv ing the governance of urba n transport, 

and guarantee ing urban transport services with adequate infrastructure for users. An analysis of the 

specific content of PNTU regarding urban logistics will be done in the next section. 

Following the PNTU, the National Program for Sustainable Urban Transport (PROMOVILIDAD) was 

created as a department within the MTC to implement this national policy. PROMOVILIDAD’s core 

objective is promoting the development of urban transport systems by strengthening the technical and 

institutional capacities of the local governments of Peruvian cities with population s greater than 

100,000 or departmental capitals, except for cities under the Urban Transport Authority for Lima and 

Callao (ATU). 

Regarding the ATU, it is governed according to Law No. 30900 of 201 8. ATU is the specialized technical 

body attached to the MTC , in charge of planning, regulating, managing, supervising, and promoting the 

efficient operation of the Integrated Transport System of Lima and Callao. From a sustainable mobility 83   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

approach, it se eks to implement an integrated network of mass urban land passenger transport 

services of “high quality and wide coverage, technologically modern, environmentally clean, technically 

efficient, and economically sustainable .”

Regarding climate protection, Ge neral Environmental Law (Law No. 28611) establishes the basic 

principles and regulations to ensure the effective exercise of the right to a healthy, balanced , and 

adequate environment for the full development of life, as well as the fulfillment of the duty to contribute 

to effective environmental management and protection , to improve the quality of life of the population ,

and achieve the sustainable development of the country. 

Regarding the integration of environmental policies with other public policies, i t is established that the 

processes of planning, decision, and execution of public policies at all levels of government, including 

sectoral ones, must incorporate the guidelines of the National Environmental Policy. 

Another essential environmental legal de velopment is Law No. 30754, or Law Framework for Climate 

Change. First, the law aims at strengthening institutions for climate change adaptation by appointing 

the national authority in this matter and assigning responsibilities to governmental departments, 

associations, and regional and local governments. Moreover, it incorporates national planning 

instruments against climate change and the monitoring of the Nationally Determined Contribution 

(NDC ).

In this sense, it provides that instruments for climate c hange are binding and mandatory for public 

authorities, so they must be considered in their institutional budgets. In addition, it provides that the 

planning instruments of public entities and investment projects subject to the National Environmental 

Impac t Assessment System must be consistent with the environmental management instruments for 

climate change. 

Regarding the powers and functions of the transportation sector, the MTC is responsible for proposing 

public policies and technical assistance to publi c entities at the three levels of government in matters 

of climate change and the NDC within its functions. Moreover, the law indicates responsibilities for 

regional ( GORE) and local ( GOLO) governments. 

According to the law, local and regional governments are responsible for: 

• Execut ing national public policies on climate change and draw ing up the appropriate strategies 

• Incorporat ing mitigation and adaptation measures for climate change in planning instruments 

and budget programs 

• Report ing to the Ministry of the Environment (MINAM ) the status of execution of the established 

mitigation and adaptation measures 

• Promot ing the informed participation of citizens in the comprehen sive management of climate 

change, aimed at strengthening climate governance and sustainable development in harmony 

with nature 

• Promot ing integrated studies for the identification of vulnerable areas, as well as scientific 

research and technological develo pment for mitigation and adaptation to climate change 

• Building capacity to adapt to the impacts of climate change and reduce vulnerability, prioritizing 

those that most need it. 

Public policy 

PNTU is the national instrument that establishes objectives and prioritizes actions addressed to solve 

the problem of urban mobility. It is also a tool for local governments to align planning and regulatory 84   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

mechanisms toward an adequate public transport system. PNTU aims to reduce time and costs of travel 

and improve road safety and air quality. 

PNTU considers the movement of freight and people as part of its diagnosis for urban transport. 

Moreover, improving the movement of goods and people is identified as a priority. For this, policy 

guidelines are formulated as fol lows: 

• The movement of goods in cities should be carried out based on definitions and proposals from 

the urban passenger transportation system. Freight transport will contribute to improving the 

competitiveness and productivity of the city, optimizing trips . Likewise, it will be guaranteed 

that the cargo vehicle fleet responds to mobilization needs in satisfactory operating conditions 

and consistent with environmental specifications. 

• Within the framework of the mobility policy, cities must consider and struc ture schemes that 

guarantee coherence between passenger a nd goods transport, at least in terms of priority 

roads, traffic schedules, loading and unloading operations, and authorized parking lots. 

Also, regarding transport infrastructure, the document empha sizes increasing demand for 

nonmotorized transportation through infrastructure provision. 

Another essential planning instrument is urban mobility plans, regulated by Supreme Decree No. 005 -

2019 -MTC, which designat e these as the regulatory technical plannin g instrument s that set the norms 

for sustainable displacement within a city. 

It is important to point out that these regulatory provisions are in accordance with P NTU provisions .

In Lima’s metropolitan area, it is worth mentioning that the Sustainable Indi vidual Mobility System, 

approved through Presidential Resolution No. 65 -2020 -ATU/PE , is aimed at the implementation of a 

network of temporary bike lanes defined by the ATU . Its design, planning, development, and monitoring 

are the responsibility of the ATU , while its execution falls to the provincial and district municipalities of 

Lima and Callao. 

Regarding climate change, the country has a National Strategy for Climate Change elaborated by the 

MINAM . The strategy seeks to adapt Peru to the adverse effects of the climate crisis. The document 

lays the foundations for sustainable and low -carbon development, with indicators that include: 

• A GHG emissions growth rate below the GDP growth rate 

• The r eduction of GHG emissions in all sectors, especially those that em it the most GHG 

• Reduced carbon intensity of the economy 

• Increased carbon capture and net reduction of emissions in the forestry sector. 

Institutional framework 

The institutional framework for urban mobility consists of an interaction betwee n the central 

government and local and regional authorities. It is clear from the analysis done in the previous section 

that the MTC and MVCS provide sectorial guidelines and technical assistance to local governments, 

while the latter is in charge of formu lating the planning instruments that adopt national policies and 

execute plans and programs. Table E.4 summarizes competencies at the national and local level 85

E.4.2. The state of cyclelogistics in Peru 

Businesses 

For the elaboration of this report, two cyclelogistics operators 28 were interviewed . These companies 

have less than three years in operation and offer last -mile delivery and B2C and business -to -business 

(B2B ) services in cargo bikes and e-bikes, among other benefits. Fleets are digitally integrated and can 

be tracked through GPS systems. 

Operators also acknowledge that the market opportunity they are trying to seize involves offering an 

ecological solution for increased freight delivery demand in urban environments. In addition, the current 

delivery model ’s inefficiency (25 to 60 percent of all deliveries involve oversized vehicles ) creates 

opportunities for small electric vehicles. 

One operator reports that demand is mainly for food (45 percent of total demand) and retail. Other 

reported clients are postal service operators, couriers, and the public sector. For the food industry and 

other types of businesses, there is increased demand for bikes and e -bikes with refrigerated containers. 

One operator highlights that the supply of these kinds of vehicles in the local market would create 

further opportunities for cyclelogistics. 

These operators include social and environmental value as part of their added value. Hiring deliver y

persons in acc ordance with labor law s and training them for increased efficiency and road safety 

contrasts with fast -growing e -delivery companies whose use of self -employed contractors raises legal 

doubts. Second , their fleets have zero direct emissions. However, both o perators report having difficulty 

attracting new clients , most of whom are concerned exclusively with cost efficiency. 

While one operator reports a travel span of 12 km inside the LMA , the other operator reports operating 

within a 5 km radius of client pr emises. The latter reports dark stores (convenience stores closed to the 

public and placed in strategic locations that deliver with in a 2 –3 km radius) among the main 

implementers of cyclelogistics. One of the operators reports a potential for cyclelogistic s of 1.4 million 

trips, offering 3.8 million kg of CO 2 savings in only last -mile deliveries. 

One of the operators also provides storage services on its premises, located in strategic areas across 

Lima. The same highlights that micro depots are a critical incentive that local authorities could provide 

to enable further efficiencies for cyclelogistics vehicles. 

However, operators report significant barriers to fully deploying their business model. The biggest 

challenges for their companies are bike infrastructure availability, access to loans and insurance, and 

a lack of awareness among potential client s about the ecological and social advantages of 

cyclelogistics. Also, operators point to the high levels of informality in the sector as an additional 

obstacle to competitiveness.

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Infrastructure 

At a national level, the Peruvian government, through PROMOVILIDAD from the MTC, has made 

significant efforts to implement infrastructure that enables active mobility as an alternative to 

motorized transport. Despite the dramatic concentration of cycling i nfrastructure investment in Lima, 89   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

the MTC has announced investments in secondary cities like Trujillo, Cusco, Puno, and Moyomba to 

implement more than 430 k m of bike infrastructure (Ciudades Como Vamos 2021). 

Moreover, the Peruvian government published a t echnical guide for temporary cycle lanes as a 

response to the COVID -19 pandemic, to grant safety and scale up the potential of the bike as a 

sustainable urban mobility solution. However, information about the existing cycling network in 

secondary cities re mains scarce (Despacio 2020). 

At a local level, the Municipality of Lima has been working on expanding its cycling network as part of 

different strategies for sustainable urban mobility like the Master Plan for Cycle Lanes for Lima and 

Callao 2005 –2025 and the Urban Transport Master Plan for Lima and Callao for 2050. A past World 

Bank report reported that by 2019 Lima had 210 .8 km of cycling networks (World Bank 2020). In 

addition, in response to the COVID -19 emergency, the Transport Authority for Lima and Callao 

announced an expansion that will lead to 470 k m of bike infrastructure (Despacio 2020). 

Furthermore, an updated proposal for the Plan for Bike Infrastructure for Lima and Callao foresees a 

total network of 1,383 k m by 2050, composed of 210 .8 km of e xisting network s, 544 km projected in 

previous plans , and 628 .2 km of new network s. But this planned expansion needs to take quality into 

consideration . Previous work by the World Bank pointed to significa nt variations in levels of 

inclusiveness and safety standards (World Bank 2020). 

E.4.3. Scenarios for the implementation of cyclelogistics in 2030 

In Peru, 45 .4 percent of the energy sector’s net CO 2 equivalent emissions are consumed by the 

transport sector (MINEM 2020). The primary energy products used ar e gasoline, diesel, and liquefied 

petroleum gas, with a slight tendency toward biofuels, due to the environmental regulations that have 

been implemented since the creation of Law No. 28 054 ( Law for the Promotion of Biofuel Market). 

However, the transport of goods consumes mainly gasoline and diesel (MINEM 2017). 

In addition, according to the last Official Annual Report on GHG Emissions (RAGEI) for the energy sector, 

road transport accounted for 91.7 percent of total sector emissions, correspondin g to 18,1833.23 

gigagrams of carbon dioxide ( Gg CO 2). Of this, 5,802.4 Gg CO 2 correspond to light commercial trucks 

(LCT s) (27.3 percent), and 3 ,805.5 Gg CO 2 correspond to motorcycles (20.2 percent). It must be noted 

that LCT s and motorcycles account for h alf of total road transport emissions (MTC 2016 ). 

Therefore, to calculate CO 2 emissions avoided through the implementation of cyclelogistics, the case of 

Lima will be used to estimate two scenarios of implementation in 2030 and 2050. The case of Lima is 

in teresting as the region has about a third of the country’s urban population and half of the GDP, having 

a dense logistics network of consumers and businesses. Furthermore, there is a broader range of 

sources and data on urban logistics in Lima than for oth er Peruvian cities. In addition, the two 

cyclelogistics operators interviewed for this report are based in Lima. 

Also, a calculation of NOx emission s reductions will be carried out, departing from data from the 

Transport for London study, which estimated that for each van that is replaced by an e -cargo bike, 3 kg 

of NOx per year are saved (Element Energy and WSP 2017). The calculations will be done according to 

the optimistic and pessimistic scenarios for 2030 and 2050 by using the final number of LCV repl aced 

in both cases. Thus, calculations regarding NOx reductions will only be carried out for LCVs replaced 

and not for motorcycles. 

The sources of information are the RAGEI published by the MINAM; statistics about the total vehicle 

fleet and the fleet reg istered for cargo transport reported by the MTC , including motorcycles (MTC 90   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

2018b); and data from studies on cyclelogistics. Also , baseline emissions for the business -as -usual 

(BAU ) scenario are based on estimations of emissions by the Multisectorial Group of Work for NDC 

implementation (Grupo de Trabajo Multisectorial 2018) for the 2030 baseline scenario and previous 

work by the Inter -American Development Bank (Banco Interamericano de Desarrollo 2021) for 2050. 

First, the official RAGEI calculates the tra nsportation sector ’s emissions . For road transport, the report 

classifies the vehicle fleet in four categories (automobiles —1A3bi -, light service trucks —1A3bii -, heavy 

service trucks and buses —1A3biii -, and motorcycles —1A3biv -) according to the Intergovernmental Panel 

on Climate Change ’s (IPCC ) Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories level 1 methodology. 

In RAGEI, LCVs are included within the LCT category ( as seen in table E.5 ). Therefore, according to 

Peru ’s national vehicle classification, the LCT category comprises pickups, panel trucks, and rural 

trucks (MTC 2021). However, for the estimations in this section, only pickup and panel trucks and 

motorcycles will be used for estimates. As exposed previously, t he potential for cyclelogistics to improv e

urban logistics has to do with replacing LCV s and motorcycles.

Now, CO 2 emissions from Lima are calculated according to the methodology set in RAGEI and official 

information from the MTC about the total vehicle fleet by region . Also, for the BAU scenario in 2030 and 

2050, it is assumed that the distribution by department of total national LCV s remain at current levels 

of 65 percent (LCV s) and 45 percent (motorcycles ) (MTC 2021, 2018b) .91   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Vehicle fleet growth until 2030 and 2050 i s calculated using data from the total circulating vehicle fleet 

and project ed according to average growth in the 2015 –20 period using the information provided by 

the MTC (MTC 2021), which is 3.8 percent for LCVs and 8.8 percent for motorcycles. Furthermor e, it is 

assumed that energy sources from the vehicle fleet and its distribution remain constant in relation to 

the last RAGEI. 

Projections about Lima’s vehicle fleet growth for LCVs and motorcycles were made based on the 

proportion of LCVs and motorcycle s registered in Lima based on official reports from 2015 to 2020. An 

average of 58 percent for pickups, 71 percent for panel trucks , and 28 percent for motorcycles is 

assumed to be constant .

The base for LCVs and motorcycles in Lima is as follows: 

• The tot al pickups and panel trucks registered for cargo transport in Lima .

• 50 percent of total pickup trucks and 30 percent of total panel trucks registered in Lima, 

assuming that these are used either for informal goods transport or for private trips devoted to 

goods movement ( retail ).29 

• 25 percent of total registered motorcycles for Lima, assuming this is the percentage belonging 

to independent workers that work in delivery and services .30

E4.3.1. Pessimistic scenario 

In this scenario, there is no change in the current motorcycle usage for logistics, given that cargo bike 

prices cannot compete with motorcycles and remain unaffordable for most of the po pulation, and only 

10 percent of LCT s are replaced by e -cargo bikes 31 by 2050 because of improved cost and operational 

efficiencies in formal cargo transport companies. It is assumed only 5 percent of potential replacement 

would be achieved by 2030. The es timations presented above are the net emission s reductions, which 

take into account indirect emissions from e -cargo bikes .

Also, no substantial investments from the public sector are made in cycling infrastructure, no 

stakeholder agreement is reached for c yclelogistics implementation (e.g. , no insurance supply for 

cyclelogistics operators), and no subsidies for cargo bike acquisition are introduced to replace 

motorcycles. Table E.7 shows assumptions, savings , and costs. 

Also, fossil fuel savings are calcula ted using the projected growth of the vehicle fleet previously 

explained, together with the average distance travelled per year obtained from MTC (2016a) . It is 

assumed that the fossil fuel distribution according to type of vehicle remains as in RAGEI 2021 . Table 

E.7 summarizes assumptions, replacement values , reductions , and costs of the pessimistic scenario.

Costs 

No costs would be generated from this scenario for the public sector. Costs from replacement are 

assumed exclusively by formal cargo transport actors and are motivated merely by the economic and 

operational efficiencies of e -cargo bikes and cargo bikes. 

Assuming an average e -cargo bike cost of $5,000 ( S/. 18,570) and an average replacement rate from 

LCV s to e -cargo bikes of 1:1.5, 22,880 cycles would be required for 2050 and 11,440 for 2030. 

The estimated costs are $114,440,528 until 2050, assuming prices for e -cargo bikes will remain 

similar to 2022. 

E4. 3.2 Optimistic scenario 

The other scenario is based on estimations by Wrighton and Reiter (2016) from the Cyclelogistics 

Project in Europe. It is assumed that: 

• Cycling infrastructure grows in the years up to 2030 according to predictions of a World Bank 

report (2020), where it was estimated in an optimistic scenario that by 2030 , Lima’s cycling 

infrastructure network would have a total of 600 km. 

• By 2050 , the cycling network has a total of 1 ,384 km implemented according to the optimistic 

scenario described in previous World Bank work. 

• Charging facilities are strategically distributed throughout the metropolitan area .94   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

• Training programs are encourage d by local, regional , and national governments in order to build 

capacities for manufacturing, maintenance , and repair of cyclelogistics vehicles. Also, local e -

cargo bike manufacturing is encouraged, and associations of manufacturers are created. 

• Signific ant agreements between stakeholders in urban logistics are achieved to encourage 

shippers and transport operators to switch traditional commercial vehicles for cargo bikes. 

• Subsidies and tax allowances for cargo bike acquisition are introduced so the price of 

cyclelogistics vehicles can compete with common fossil fuel motorcycles and also private trips 

for goods movement through (cargo) bikes and e -bikes are increased. 

In this scenario, by 2050 , 25 percent of motorcycle trips for independent work are repla ced by e -cargo 

bikes ,32 assuming that independent workers will replace motorcycles with cycles given the reduced 

costs of cyclelogistics vehicles. Also, 50 percent of LCV emissions are saved by replacing 50 percent of 

LCV s with cyclelogistics vehicles base d on Wrighton and Reiter (2016) estimations o f the potential 

replacement of cargo bikes for LCVs. The estimations presented above are the net emission s

reductions, which take into account indirect emissions from e -cargo bikes. 

Accordingly, in 2030 , 50 perc ent of the 2050 estimations are achieved, meaning 12.5 percent of 

motorcycle trips for independent work and 25 percent of LCT trips are replaced by cyclelogistics. 

Despite the relative closeness of 2030, we support this rapid increase with the impact of re gulatory 

measures that create substantial advantages for cyclelogistics. As discussed before, th ese measures 

include but are not limited to low -emission areas, truck bans, and congestion charges, among others.

Value added tax (18 percent) exemption is used as an example regarding tax allowances. Assuming an

E4.4. Indirect emissions of e -cargo bikes 

Narayanan and Antoniou (2022) calculate that, on average, a pedal -assist e -cargo bike consumes 

about 13.5 watt hours per kilometer (from 9 to 18). Moreover, according to the factor of emission of 

the Peruvian electric grid reported by MINAM, indirect emiss ions from e -cargo bikes derived from the 

use of electricity to charge are on average 0.5 tons of CO 2 per bike per year 33 using the average 

kilometers travelled from motorcycles and trucks in urban logistics using data from “Estudio para el 

mejoramiento de la logística de distribución urbana de mercancías en Lima Metropolitana y El 

Callao”(MTC 2016 ) and “ Reporte monitoreo de indicadores Piloto BiciCarga” ( MTC 2021). Using th ese 

data, results are shown for the pessimistic and optimistic scenario, assuming 2:1 and 1:1 replacement 

rates exclusive of e -cargo bikes. Data about indirect emissions are included in the estimations for the 

pessimistic and optimistic scenarios presented in sections E4. 3.1 and E4. 3.2 assuming a 1.5:1 

replacement rate.
