## E.5. Recommendations for implementation 

E.5.1. Legal and regulatory framework 

Although the need for nonmotorized mobility is designated as an objective in national and local 

instruments for sustainability, cyclelogistics has not been acknowledged as a means to decarbonize 

the movement of goods. 

At the national level, the MTC could update the PNTU to include detailed actions to decarbonize goods 

movement that go beyond emission and congestion aspects, including road safety. 

At the local level, policy instruments like urban mobility plans, urban development plans, and 

sustainable ind ividual mobility plans should contain specific guidelines for cyclelogistics. First, these 

plans should include a vision for sustainable urban logistics that is articulated with passenger transport 

and maximizes the engagement of public and private stakeho lders to accommodate the increasing 

demand for goods movement while maintaining operational efficiency within social and environmental 

parameters. Second, p lans should also keep track of progress toward targeted goals. 

More importantly, specific binding i nstruments to decarbonize goods transport at the national and local 

level should be enacted (urban logistics plan s). These plans should set explicit and realistic long -term 

goals for efficient urban logistics with a strong focus on emission s reduction whil e addressing local 

issues like air quality, road safety, traffic congestion, and the economy. An iterative plan with short -,

medium -, and long -term targets and an associated set of measures and periodical evaluations is also 

flexible to adapt to political change and local priorities (ICLEI 2021). 98   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

• For this, the MTC should provide guidelines through PROMOVILIDAD, articulated with public 

entities at the national level involved in economic development and trade. Also, the MTC should 

try to identify national ass ociations and unions that are relevant for urban logistics. Finally, the 

national government should create measurable targets and monitor them periodically. 

• However, local governments must be responsible for engaging local stakeholders and adapting 

nation al guidelines to local contexts. Local governments are also responsible for executing the 

plans and monitoring short -, medium - and long -term targets. 

At the national and local level, regulations should provide specific norms to encourage and protect 

cyclel ogistics. For example, as in the case of  Sao Paulo , regulations should mandate current and future 

bike parking lots and commercial and residential buil dings to allow and provide space for cargo bikes. 

Other stimulating measures could be to provide particular parking lots along public road s or public 

buildings. Regulations should also set a data governance policy that incentivizes potential operators to 

gather and optimize data shared with local authorities. 

• At the national level, the MTC and the MVCS must prepare guidelines for the use of public space 

(including road space) for parking cargo (e -) bikes and the installation of charging points in 

strategic points. Moreover, the MVCS should prepare guidelines for charging facilities in 

residential and commercial buildings. 

• At the local level, municipalities should apply national guidelines to local contexts. Moreover, 

they should implement these guidelines b y adapting land uses and public space regulations. 

Regulatory measures that create competitive advantages for cyclelogistics relative to other means of 

transport include: 

• Regulatory measures like zero -emission zones (geographically determined areas where only 

vehicles with zero -emission standards are allowed), pedestrianized streets (like historic city 

centers), parking regulations ; or 

Photo E. 10 Cargo bike in pedestrianized area of San Sebastian, Spain    

> Source: ©Javier Hernandez (Medina 2021). Further permission required for reuse. 99
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

• Market -based measures like congestion charges (Central London), taxation, tax allowances ,

and subsidies. 

Photo E. 11 Congestion charge in Central London  

> Source: © Anizza https://www.dreamstime.com/editorial -photo -london -congestion -charging -zone -sign -uk -july -charge -introduced -to -reduce -centr al -
> standard -charge -each -day -image76240166 .

To provide legal ground for restrictive measures like zero -emission areas and emission standard 

restrictions for central areas, the MTC should give clear guidelines for the action of local governments .

Subsidies or loans for (cargo) bikes and e -bikes acquisition should be implemented to encourage 

transitions to cyclelogistics from traditional freight vehicles , especially to assume the costs of the initial 

switch . Costs tend to decrease over time since cycles invo lve lower maintenance costs . Moreover, taxes 

for the importation of e -bikes and e -cargo bikes should be lowered to make final prices more 

competitive with fuel -based vehicles. 

Cities should also lead by example. Trips related to activities of public entities that are suitable for 

carrying in cargo bikes should be implemented and made visible. The case of Strasbourg is illustrative, 

as the city has added cargo bikes as part of its fleet for street cleaning and public libraries. A local 

initiative in th e municipality of Jesus Maria in Lima intends to use cargo bikes for recycled material 

transportation .

Photo E.12 Local initiative in Jesus Maria, Lima   

> Source: © Greenbelle Peru, Photographer: Kenneth Campbell 100
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Procurement is also a powerful tool that is often unrealized. Public procurement accounts for 30 

percent of the total GDP in developing countries. Through the tendering process, municipal 

procurement has the potential to both support smaller local cyclelogistics companies and coerce 

international lo gistics companies into adopting electric -assisted cargo bikes (Cyclelogistics -

CityChangerCargoBike 2019). For example, the new public bike system in Bogota operated by Tembici 

includes shared cargo bikes. 

E.5.2. Bike infrastructure 

The network of cyclin g infrastructure should be extended in interconnected ways that provide access 

to cargo bikes. The World Bank recommends that the bike network of Lima and Callao be :

• Coherent (complete, well connected, and signalized with clear messages) 

• Direct (minimizing detours and delays) 

• Safe 

• Comfortable for users ( emitting the least noise and air pollution possible )

• Attractive and integrated with urban landmarks and green areas 

• Generate socioeconomical value by proximity to urban landmarks and commercial zo nes. 

In addition, for roads with high demand and effective speed s beyond 50 km /hour, physically segregated 

bike lines are required. Likewise, for streets with speed limits below 30 –40 km/h our , traffic pacification 

measures must be adopted to prevent speed excess and reduce hazards for cyclists. Cargo bikes 

should be taken into account. Studies by the Cyclelogistics project in Europe state that bike 

infrastructure adapted to cargo bikes should be at least 2 meters (m) and ideally 2.25 m for cycle lanes 

and 2 .4 m for cycle paths to provide access to all models of cargo bikes (CityChangerCargoBike 2021) .

This is to protect cyclelogistics vehicles and other actors of active mobility like pedestrians , scooters ,

and bike riders. 

Parking 

Mandating current and futu re parking lots and public buildings to provide space for cargo bikes can 

encourage cargo bike use. Also, providing space for parking in public areas like parks, plazas , or 

sidewalks (whenever there is enough space for pedestrians) can incentivize transpor t operators and 

shippers to use cargo bikes as they usually struggle to find parking space in dense urban areas. 

Specialized infrastructure for frequent stops 

Designating loading zones in dense urban areas will encourage cargo bike use and improve the ove rall 

efficiency of urban logistics. 

Also, bike lanes should be adapted with frequent “exits” for cargo bikes to do deliveries. Frequent stops 

in cycling infrastructure can affect traffic flows .

Charging points 

Charging points that are geographically distributed and adequately connected with bike infrastructure 

can maximize the operational advantages of cargo bikes. Transport authorities should work with energy 

companies to set agreements for charging point implementation. Also, new residential and commercial 

buildings should provide charging points to encourage electrification. 

Microdeposits 

Microdeposits or transshipment hubs have proven essential for unleashing cargo bike potential for first -

and last -mile deliveries. The administration of these hubs is also essential for considering succes s, as 

collaborative platforms can benefit several actors while contributing to overall logistical efficiency. 101   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Cities must identify and adapt critical public spaces for microconsolidation purposes and include them 

in urban development plans and urban mobil ity plans. These premises require space for short -term 

storage and sorting of goods; freight -cycle entry/exit and secure parking; the parking and maneuvering 

space needed for a larger freight vehicle; loading and unloading facilities; and potentially , offi ce and 

rest space for riders. 

A useful tool is to partner with private space administrators like parking companies and offer them 

incentives for the implementation of microdeposit facilities for cyclelogistics. 

E.5.3. Stakeholder engagement 

All stakehold ers participating in urban logistics (shippers, transport operators, relevant associations, 

civil society, receivers, etc.) should be identified for further integration into the planning process. 

Spaces for stakeholder participation should be kept permanen tly for consultation and evaluation of 

regulatory measures. 

Pilot projects that involve real actors from urban logistics like KoMoDo in Berlin and BiciCarga in Bogota 

can make a case for cyclelogistics and promote the use of cargo bikes for B2B, B2C, and first - and last -

mile deliveries. For example, the high costs of renting spaces for microdeposits and cyclelogistics hubs 

can be assumed by several actors through public -private partnerships and therefore shared by multiple 

operators. 

E.5.4. Urban planning 

Urban development plans should be integrated with urban mobility plans and sustainable individual 

mobility plans, focusing on transit -oriented development. 

Urban development plans should acknowledge goods transport and consider actors and goods 

transport dynamics (first - and last -mile trips) to provide adequate land use and infrastructure (bike 

lanes, microdeposits) to mitigate the impact of delivery/consolidation trips. 

Urban planning instruments should be aligned with national and local logistics plans in order to avoid 

heavy service vehicles entering dense urban areas and leveraging the potential of cyclelogistics instead. 

E.5.6. Data 

Georeferenced information about bike infrastructure availability and characteristics should be made 

available for cycle logistics operators to improve coverage, accessibility, and efficiency. Moreover, it will 

also favor private trips through cargo bikes. 

A data governance policy must be established for public authorities to access data mainly from shippers 

and transport op erators to optimize public policy for cyclelogistics. However, this policy should be 

constructed through consultation processes with stakeholders, incentivizing the recollection and 

processing of data. 

E.5.7. Labor 

Adequate infrastructure for hydration, sa nitation, and rest must be provided for drivers. 

Local governments should provide such training whenever companies do not provide road safety 

training. Moreover, cities should create awareness campaigns aimed at prioritizing active and 

nonmotorized actors. 102   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Specialized regulations for deliverers from e -delivery companies must be enacted to guarantee 

minimum working conditions according to Peruvian law. 

E.5.8. Security, safety, and quality standards in vehicles 

Law enforcement officers (national police) shoul d be brought in to the planning process as well as during 

stakeholder engagement phases , as many actors report security as one of their concerns for adopting 

cyclelogistics. The establishment of safe routes or corridors could be helpful. 

The creation of qua lity standards for cyclelogistics vehicles will improve vehicle safety. As these 

standards become available, regulators should control and monitor safety requirements in such 

vehicles. European developments in vehicle standardization should be used as an i nspiration. 

The MTC should coordinate with relevant actors from the insurance sectors to make insurance available 

to cyclelogistics operators. To begin, state -backed insurance could be offered for cyclelogistics 

operators. 

Local governments must ensure a dequate training for cyclelogistics drivers to ensure they are 

predictable and communicative and take precautions on the road. 

## E.6. Conclusions 

The literature review carried out for this report highlights the potential of cyclelogistics in urban logistics, 

especially regarding the replacement of LCVs in first - and last -mile trips, private shopping and leisure 

trips, service trips , as well as B2B a nd B2C deliveries. Whereas societal and environmental benefits are 

clearly demonstrated, further research needs to be done regarding the economic feasibility of 

cyclelogistics businesses and the implementation of cargo (e -) bikes in broader supply chains, 

especially in the Latin America and the Caribbean region. 

Most of the successful experiences reviewed are from European cities. However, there is growing 

evidence of the adoption of cyclelogistics in Latin America by private operators and public entities 

through pilot projects as in the case of Bogota. Moreover, the use of bike -inspired vehicles for urban 

logistics has historical roots in urban environments in the region, even if it is more linked to nano -

commerce and informal transport of goods. While the scale of possibilities for cyclelogistics 

implementation in urban logistics in the region is not yet clear , existing evidence makes a case for 

further research. 

Cyclelogistics is not a solution for all urban logistics problems in modern cities. It could b e part of the 

solution, as long as it is implemented along with the aforementioned elements regarding infrastructure, 

policy, regulation, safety, and so on .

The emergence of new business models and the rise of e -commerce create additional opportunities fo r

cyclelogistics to be implemented. 

The review of international experiences shows several measures that local authorities can take to 

promote cyclelogistics. In general, restrictive measures toward fuel -motorized vehicles have 

demonstrated great potential in creating competitive advantages for cyclelogistics. Moreover, pilot 

projects and public -private partnerships are ideal instruments to begin with. 

Specific policy or regulations for sustainable urban goods movement at the national and local level are 

sc arce. Most planning instruments like urban development plans and urban mobility plans focus on 

passenger transport with only vague references to urban logistics. However, there is a clear guideline 

in th ese policy instruments toward the promotion of the bi ke and the decarbonization of the motorized 

fleet. 103   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Rough estimations carried out for this report show a significant potential for emission s reduction in 

urban environments based on previous studies about LCV replacement by cyclelogistics. However, 

these s tudies were carried out in European contexts and need further adaptation to the Latin American 

context. This does not mean that the potential for implementation is more moderate, but instead, that 

context -specific actors and commercial dynamics must be con sidered.

## F.1. International climate negotiations in the past decades 

In his speech at the 2018 United Nations General Assembly, Secretary -General António Gutierres drew 

the international community’s atte ntion to the latest data from the World Meteorological Organization, 

which revealed that 18 of the 20 hottest years (recorded since 1850) have been in the past two 

decades (IPCC 2019). He also pointed out that increases in temperature confirm that global w arming 

has been —unfortunately —directly induced by human activity (IPCC 2013). 

From its first report published in the early 1990s, the United Nations’ Intergovernmental Panel on 

Climate Change (IPCC) 34 has clearly shown the consequences of climate change a nd how they will affect 

the global ecological balance. The increase in the Earth’s temperature due to the higher concentration 

of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions directly affects the frequency of global droughts, the rise in sea 

levels, and the change of ra infall patterns, among other natural events. However, the biggest problem 

of all is the direct impact on the quality of life of the world’s most vulnerable populations. This often 

leads to other problems, such as forced migrations, increased poverty in the affected countries, and 

reduced food security. 

In its fifth report (2013), the IPCC concludes that it is vital to rapidly and drastically reduce human activities 

generating the most GHGs. Otherwise, we could jeopardize the earth’s balance by reaching the climate’s 

tipping points or a point of no return. 35 

Faced with the evidence of the effects of GHGs and the lack of efforts made by the international 

community, in 2015, at the COP21 36 in Paris, 195 countries agreed to decrease their GHG emissions 

immediately to achieve a climate -neutral planet by the middle of the century. The immediate objective 

was to limit global warming to below 2°C (ideally 1.5°C), compared to preindustrial levels (UNFCCC n.d.). 

The Paris Agreement thus represented a significant milestone in the history of climate change at the 

international level, since it was the first time that a multilateral process, in which all countries got together 

for a common cause, reach ed a binding agreement. They also made a commitment to carry out concrete 

and measurable efforts to reduce GHG emissions, to control the increase of the earth’s temperature, and 

to adapt to the effects of climate change .        

> 34 Th e IPCC’s First Assessment Report was published in 1990, and it proved through scientific assessments the concerns on climate change effects. After
> its publication, the United Nations General Assembly decided to prepare a Framework Convention on Climate Cha nge (UNFCCC), which entered into force
> in March 1994.
> 35 “Tipping points or a point of no return,” a phrase coined by the IPCC, referring to reach a point of irreversible change. In other words, from this point
> onward, the earth’s climate system would behav e in a completely different manner than before, seeking a new balance from the one that preceded it.
> According to the IPCC these tipping points are the: (1) arctic ice melting, (2) Greenland ice sheet melting, (3) retreat of b oreal forests, (4) melting of
> permafrost, (5) alteration of North Atlantic currents, (6) loss of Amazon rainforests, (7) degradation of warm water corals, (8) collapse of the West Antarctic
> Ocean ice sheets, and (9) loss of ice in Antarctica.
> 36 UNFCCC ’s Twenty -First Conference of the Parties. 110

## F.2. Integrated climate change management and resilience in Peru 

Since the publication of the first IPCC report on the effects of climate change and GHGs in the early 

1990s, Peru, as an active member of the United Nations (UN), has been committed to strengthening 

its institutional structure and reducing its GHG emissions. Above all, it is committed to developing 

management mechanisms to strengthen its risk management system in the event of natural disasters .

Given its diverse geography, its different climatic conditions, 37 and its location within the Pacific Ring of 

Fire, Peru is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world as it is subject to constant earthquakes, 

floods, or extreme climatic events such as el friaje (a “cold spell”) and the El Niño phenomenon, among 

others. The country registers the following seven of nine characteristics of a country vulnerable to 

climate change, as recognized by the UNFCCC :

• Low coastal zones 

• Arid and semiarid zones 

• Areas exposed to floods, droughts, and desertification 

• Fragile mountain ecosystems 

• Disaster -prone areas 

• Urban areas with high levels of air pollution 

• Local economies heavily dependent on incomes generated by the production and use of fossil 

fuels 

Peru h as a special and vulnerable ecosystem, bu lack resilient infrastructure relative to low -income 

countries such as Mali and Zambia . It is also important to strengthen the country’s public policy to 

assist and protect the most vulnerable people throughout the territory, as well as to follow the 

international climate agenda to make good on international commitments to reduce GHG emissions .    

> 37 Peru is one of the 10 most diverse countries on the planet, as it has 84 of the 117 life zones, and 28 of the 32 different cl imates on Earth. The country
> encompasses a large part of the world’s biological diver sity (MINAM 2016) .111
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Peru’s economic growth in recent decades is notable: its gross domestic product (GDP) grew at an 

average annual rate of 6.4 percent and its GDP per capita increased from S/. 6,961 in 1990 to S/. 

16,939 in 2018. Seventy -nine percent of the population lives in urban areas and 21 percent, who 

mostly depend on primary production and extractive activities, lives in rural areas. Additionally, 51 

percent of the rural, economically active population is involved in the agricultural sector and more than 

80 percent of the rural, economically active population lives in poverty. Rural populations work mostly 

in agriculture, fishing, and mining .

Meanwhile, the national transportation infrastructure has not developed as expected. According to 

estimations by the Association for the Promotion of the National Infrastructure, the national 

transportation infrastructure gap in 2016 exceeded US$50 billion. This explains the country’s 

regression in the 2019 Global Competitiveness Report, which ranked Peru 97 th out of 114 countries 

in terms of transportation infrastructure .

> Source: INDECI

The increase in the frequency of the country’s natural disasters has prompted the government to 

enhance its policy and disaster response system with tools and instruments to promote environmental 

management and to adapt to the climate change agenda. For th is reason, after publication of the first 

IPCC report alerting us to the consequences of climate change, Peru created the National Commission 

on Climate Change (CNCC) in 1993 with public and private representation. The objective of this 

commission was to c oordinate, promote, and propose measures to improve the management of climate 

change risks, as well as to reduce GHG emissions. Although initially the presidency was in charge of the 

Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the National Environmental Council (CONAM ), in 1996, it was finally given 

to the Ministry of Environment that led —and still leads —the CNCC. 112   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Figure F.1 Timeline of Peru’s main climate change milestones 

> Source: MINAM

In 2003, the CNCC generated the first National Strategy on Climate Change (ENCC), 38 a guide that 

establishes national, regional, and sectoral development strategies, plans, and projects including 

adaptation and mitigation measures to manage the current climate change scenario 39 (MINAM 2015). 

In this sense, the ENCC represents the first organized step in the country’s plan to reach its goals, by 

carrying out specific and coordinated actions across the government’s three levels and as per the 

national agenda .

In 2014, regulations were approved for development of the first management platform to measure GHG 

emissions more precisely at the national level. This led to the creation of the national GHG inventory, 

INFOCARBONO, 40 which included a relevant regulatory and institutional fra mework for the collection, 

evaluation, and systematization of GHG emissions data. Additionally, sectoral authorities had to issue 

periodic reports on the management of GHG emissions in their corresponding sectors. 41 

As mentioned before, in 2015 and within the COP21 framework, 195 nations unanimously agreed to 

fight climate change and promote measures to keep the global temperature well below an increase of 

2°C relative to preindustrial levels. 42 For these purposes, Peru undertook regulatory and institutiona l

efforts in the national management of GHG emissions. A technical report outlining the country’s 

Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDCs) 43 was released, and systems were created to 

monitor compliance with the endorsed commitments .

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

The most important management tool implemented was the Law on Climate Change (LMCC, Law No. 

30764), approved on April 18, 2018, as well as its regulation in 2019. 44 The LMCC aims to establish 

general norms and to organize and spread public policies using an integrated and territorial 

management vision. Through participatory dialogue and transparent communication, public and private 

entities are chosen to define the scope of adaptation and mitigation measures that will enable the 

country to comply with its international commitments under the NDCs as per the established times and 

terms .

The following are the law’s most important norms :

• Peru’s Ministry of Environment (MINAM) is the national authority on climate policy, and will 

establish, guide, direct, facilitate, monitor, and promote compliance with climate change 

management. 

• A multisectoral competency framework will be established, where public entities at the 

natio nal, regional, and local levels define, prioritize, and report their concrete adaptation and 

mitigation actions. 

• Planning at the three government levels (management, development planning, and sectoral 

investment instruments) should include adaptation and m itigation actions for climate change. 

• A permanent commission under the framework of the current regulations will be created to 

update the NDCs .

Finally, in 2020, the CNCC released its latest 2021 –30 Technical Study on Nationally Determined 

Contributions, w hich readjusts national net emissions commitments for 2030: from 208.8 metric tons 

of carbon dioxide equivalent (MtCO 2eq) to 179.0 MtCO 2eq .

F.2.1. Analysis of Peru’s institutional framework for climate change 

Over the past two decades, the government has been adapting its institutional model to face the 

challenges of promoting the climate agenda in the country, according to the international agreements 

adopted at the COPs. Although the CNCC was created in 1993 under the leadership of the Min istry of 

Foreign Affairs, it was assigned in 1996 to the National Environmental Council (CONAM) under the 

leadership of the Presidency of the Council of Ministers .

In 2008, MINAM was created through Legislative Decree 1013, and established as the national 

environmental authority and central entity to manage climate change issues. This was an effort to gather 

together all management tools outlined by public policies, using a cohesive , territory -wide approach .

MINAM determines the main objectives, guideline s, and national standards for the management of 

emissions and adaptation to climate change through the National Environmental Policy . MINAM is made 

up of the Vice -Ministry of Environmental Management and the Vice -Ministry of Natural Resources’ 

Strategic De velopment, which is responsible for the General Directorate of Climate Change, 

Desertification and Water Resources (DGCCDR ). 

The DGCCDR is responsible for guiding the country’s climate change management, and promoting and 

aiding the different public entit ies to include climate change aspects in their planning and action    

> 44 D.S. No. 013 -2019 -MINAM. 114
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

processes. The directorate is in charge of the Technical Secretariat of the CNCC (which is chaired by 

MINAM) and the Multisectoral Commission that is responsible for managing Peru’s NDCs .

Figure F.2 Institutional framework for managing climate change in Peru 

> Source: MINAM

As regards the adaptation of the transportation sector to climate change, the Ministry of Transportation 

and Communications (MTC) is the governing entity responsible for transportation infrastructure and 

implementing transportation systems. It is in charge of the construction, improvement, and 

rehabilitation of roads and railways, as well as air, maritime, and river transportation. It plans, regulates, 

supervises, and operates the integrated network of public land transportation systems for mass use. 

In 20 19, the MTC started adjusting its organizational structure to respond more efficiently to the needs 

of the sector. The reorganization was completed with the approval of the Integrated Text of the 

Regulation of Organization and Functions (ROF), which stated that the MTC’s General Directorate of 

Environmental Affairs (DGAAM) would be the focal point for climate change and responsible for 

monitoring implementation of the sector’s NDCs. 115

F.2.2. Progress on the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) 

Despite the fact that Peruvian emissions represent less than 0.4 percent of total global GHG emissions, 

in 2014 the country began the process of formulating its INDCs. 45 The process started with the 

establishment of the Multisectoral Commission, which was appointed 46 to prepar e the first technical 

report that would include the Peruvian INDC proposal to be presented to the UNFCCC .

In 2015, the commission completed the technical report, which determined that there would be a 30 

percent reduction in Peru’s projected contributions for the year 2030 in relation to previous projected 

emissions —all this as part of a business -as -usual 47 (BAU) scenario. Thus , the INDCs would result in the 

reduction of GHG emissions in the order of 89.4 MtCO 2eq by 2030, in comparison to 2015.  

> 45

In 2015 the Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDC) were to be ratified by the government at a later date.

Communications (MTC); the Ministry of Production; the Ministry of Housing, Construction and Sanit ation; the Ministry of Foreign Affairs; the Ministry of 

Education; the Ministry of Justice and Human Rights; the Ministry of Health; and the Ministry of Culture.

Likewise, to meet the proposed commitments, the Peruvian state planned the implementation of 75 

mitigation activities divided across six sectors (energy, transport, industrial processes, agriculture, 

LULUCF, 48 and waste), and an additional measure that included the reduction of emissions across all 

sectors .

Subsequently, in 2016, the Peruvian government ratified the Paris Agreement 49 and the country’s 

INDCs finally became binding before the international community —that is, the INDCs officially became 

NDCs.

Group (GTM -NDC) to approve guidelines, methodologies, guides, and procedures for formulating or 

updating the country’s NDCs. The climate change adaptation and mitigation actions listed by the 

Multisectoral Co mmission were focused on five thematic areas as shown in table F.3.

Throughout the participatory process of preparing the Climate Change Adaptation Plan (NAP), many 

stakeholders have suggested the inclusion of the transportation sector as a new potential thematic 

area. However, since this inclusion is still subject to the analysis established in the LMCC regulation, 

the transport sector has not yet presented any adaptation measures 52 for approval to the GTM -NDC. 

In 2020, the GTM -NDC presented an update to the NAP, containing the following proposal: 

“The Peruvian State commits to not exceed its net greenhouse gas emissions over 208.8 

MtCO2eq in 2030 (not conditioned goal). Additionally, the Peruvian S tate considers that 

greenhouse gas emissions could reach a level of 179.0 MtCO2eq57 depending on the 

international external financing and the existence of favorable conditions (conditioned 

goal).” 

Climate Change Adaptation Plan 2021 –2030, page 59 

According to the NAP, targets need to be updated because the ones set in 2015 are considered relative 

targets, 53 meaning that the magnitude of the necessary reductions will depend directly on the projected 

emis sions levels in the BAU scenario. Therefore, in order to avoid ambiguities, this update establishes 

a peak amount of 208.8 MtCO 2eq of GHG emissions in 2030 in a BAU scenario and 179.0 MtCO 2eq as 

the new target.

F.2.3. National inventory and sectoral reporting of greenhouse gases (RAGEI) 

In 2016, after regulations for determining the national GHG inventories were approved and sectoral 

authorities were given the direct responsibility for releasing periodic reports on GHG management in 

their corresponding sectors, MINAM developed the INFOCARBONO platform. This national platform is 

transparent and allows competent entities and the general public to access available and updated 

information on the National Greenhouse Gas Inventories (INGEI), the Sectoral Greenhouse Gas Reports 

(RAGEI), the methodologies applied, and the results obtained. 

INFOCARBONO has assigned responsibilities to e ach one of the state’s entities involved in the 

preparation of INGEI. Currently, six ministries —the Ministry of Energy and Mines (MINEM); Ministry of 

Agriculture (MIDAGRI); MTC; Ministry of Housing, Construction and Sanitation (MVCS); Ministry of 

Productio n (PRODUCE); and MINAM —are in charge of drawing up annual GHG reports for activities 

under their jurisdiction. MINAM is responsible for quality control and consolidation of these reports, and 

for the final writing of the INGEI report. 

INGEI is part of the System for the Monitoring of Adaptation and Mitigation Measures in Peru, and 

transparency in the provision of its information complies with the obligations of the Paris Agreement 

and the UNFCCC. 

The last national inventory was presented in 2021 to the nati onal and international community and the 

UNFCCC. It was calculated based on the 2016 exhaust gas ignition emissions. According to INGEI, as 

of 2016, the country’s total net emissions reached a total of 205.2 MtCO 2eq. 

The agriculture, forestry, and other l and use sectors were the main sources of national GHG emissions, 

amounting to 134.901 MtCO 2eq (65.71 percent of the total) in 2016 . Next was energy, with a total of 

58.132 MtCO 2eq (28.32 percent of the total). Within this sector, emissions generated in the “energy —

mobile combustion” subcategory stand out at 21.047 MtCO 2eq, equivalent to 10 percent of total GHG 

emissions at the country level . The agriculture and transportation sectors alone represented around 

94.03 percent of Peru’s total GHG emissions in 2016 . Land transport emitted a total of 19.294 

MtCO 2eq, making up the largest share (91.70 percent) of the transportation sector’s emissions. 

Figure F.7 Share of GHG emissions among the INGEI sectors , 2016    

> Source: INFOCARBONO —MINAM 121
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

As mentioned in table F.4, the link between the transportation sector and GHG emissions at the national 

level is directly related to the category of mobile combustion (generated by the burning of fuel in 

different transportation modes). 

The evolution of GHG emissions was rapid: emissions in 2016 increased by approximately 20.88 

percent compared to 2014, and 122.77 percent compared to 2000 (figure F.8). The sector’s emissions 

keep increasing, probably due to the increased use of the road fl eet for transportation: as reported by 

the MTC, in 2000 there were 1,179,158 units running and by 2016 these had increased by 317 percent 

to 4,913,893 units. 

Figure F.8 GHG emissions by transport mode , 2016  

> Source: INFOCARBONO RAGEI -MINAM

The rise in mobile combustion emissions is mainly explained by land transportation (91.7 percent) and 

civil aviation (5.1 percent), which together account for 96.8 percent of the total GHGs produced 

annually by Peru’s transport sector. It is estimated that in 2019, mo bile combustion emissions reached 

23.3 MtCO 2eq, which represent an increase of 11 percent of the total recorded in 2016. 54 

Additionally, it is relevant that the RAGEI as of 2016 also categorized ground transport emissions 

according to type of vehicles, suc h as “light duty trucks,” responsible for 27.58 percent (5.8 MtCO 2) of 

GHG emissions; “heavy duty buses,” for 25.16 percent (5.3 MtCO 2); automobiles, 20.88 percent (4.4 

MtCO 2); and finally motorcycles, 18.1 percent (3.8 MtCO 2). 

Through the RAGEI reports (f or the years 2000, 2005, 2010, 2012, 2014, 2016, and one prepared by 

a consultant in 2019), we observe that the participation ratio of the different modes of transport in the 

sector’s GHG emissions remains the same throughout the years, (i.e., land transpo rt and civil aviation 

account for around 97 percent of emissions). These ratios are therefore not expected to vary 

significantly in 2030 (the target year of Peru’s NDCs).    

> 54 The recently available data for b oth the vehicle fleet and fuel consumption in 2019 allow the sector’s emissions for that year to be estimated. 122
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

In this way, considering Peru’s modified NDCs, it is estimated that the emissions tar get for 2030 for 

the transport sector should be around 17.9 MtCO 2eq. Additionally, land transport should reach 16.4 

MtCO 2eq compared to the 19.2 MtCO 2eq recorded in 2016.

currently there is an emissions gap of 49.9 MtCO 2.123

by at least 12.23 MtCO 2 by 2030, whereas to date the 14 approved measures represent only 54

Following is an explanation of the scope of the 14 mitigation measures committed and 

approved by the transportation sector in an initial effort to reduce GHG emissions by 2030. 

Implementation of the complementary corridor s of Lima’s integrated transportation system 

The complementary corridors are high -capacity trunk -feeder route systems that operate on segregated 

or mixed routes in the Lima Metropolitan Area under the administration of the MTC. These are part of 

the integrated transportation system and are administered by the Metropolitan Institute of 

Protransporte de Lima (Protransporte) of the Metropolitan Municipality of Lima. 

The measure proposes to reduce the oversupply of urban transportation ser vices in the Lima 

Metropolitan Area through the implementation of structured urban transportation services throughout 

the city, on the main metropolitan routes, promoting their physical, modal, and fare integration. 

GHG emissions will decrease due to the e ffects of replacing the fuel in public transport units with fuels 

that have lower emissions (e.g., natural gas) and optimizing hydrocarbon consumption (mainly DB5 )

through mass transit. Likewise, there will be positive effects on the reduction of aging public 

transportation fleets and the optimization of transportation routes in the Lima Metropolitan Area. 

Service provision in the complementary corridors began in 2014 with the Tacna -Garcilaso -Arequipa 

Corridor in July 2014, followed by the Javier P rado –La Marina Corridor in December of the same year 125   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

and the San Juan de Lurigancho Corridor in January 2017. To date, five corridors have been approved, 

which involved the bidding of 49 route packages for a total of 5,200 fleet units bidded. 

Main benefit s

• Environmental: (1) reduction of local pollutants (as buses are fitted with eco -friendly 

technology —EURO IV as of 2018 —and buses running on natural gas have entered the local 

market); and (2) reduction of noise pollution. 

• Social: (1) positive impacts on the population: s avings in travel times, fewer traffic accidents, 

among others; and (2) improvement in road education and increase in safety and health. 

• Economic: (1) lower transportation costs, savings due to fewer pollution -related illnesses, and 

increase in employment; and (2) increase in productive activities and commercialization, and 

promotion of employment. 

Progress made to date 

• The Urban Transportation Authority (ATU) carries out inspections, seeking to improve and 

protect the integrity of users, as well as to free the roads from informal traffic in coordination 

with the National Police of Peru. 

• The ATU’s Regulations for the Organization of Functions contemplate specific functions for 

formulating the Road Regulatory Plan and the Annual Inspection Plan. 

• The ATU plan s to propose a strategy for monitoring environmental requirements to be followed 

by each corridor concessionaire. 

Current operation of the Metropolitano (bus service) and extensions 

This mitigation measure, under the administration of the MTC, contemplates the replacement of 

conventional diesel buses with new ones operated on natural gas. The High -Capacity Segregated 

Corridor (Corredor Segregado de Alta Capacidad, COSAC I), the mitigation measure, known currently as 

the Metropolitano, consists of an integra ted public transportation system for Lima, which has high -

capacity articulated buses that circulate along exclusive corridors, under the bus rapid transit scheme. 

This transportation system currently has 35 intermediate stations, 1 central subway station, and 2 

transfer terminals. The trunk routes are integrated with the feeder routes and connect the north and 

south of the city, as well as 18 districts. 

The Metropolitano is the first system in the world to operate natural gas vehicles (NGVs), currently 

carr ying around 720,000 passengers (each working day), and is expected to reduce CO 2 emissions by 

68,000 tons annually. The project is executed by the Metropolitan Municipality of Lima, through the 

Metropolitan Protransporte Institute of Lima (Protransporte). It currently has 300 articulated vehicles 

(18 meters long with a capacity of 160 passengers) for trunk routes and 222 (between 8.5 and 12 

meters long with a capacity of 40 and 80 passengers, respectively) for feeder routes. 

The Metropolitano road corrido r is made up of 35 intermediate stations distributed along the 26 -

kilometer (km) trunk route, 1 subway central station located under the so -called Paseo de los Héroes 

in downtown Lima, and 2 terminals located in the Matellini (Chorrillos district) and El N aranjal 

(Independencia district) urbanizations.  The system connects 18 districts: Independencia, San Martín 

de Porres, Puente Piedra, Carabayllo, Comas, Rímac, Breña, Cercado de Lima, La Victoria, Lince, San 

Isidro, Surquillo, Miraflores, Surco, Jesús Marí a, Barranco, Chorrillos, and Villa El Salvador. 126

Main benefits

Implementation of Lines 1 and 2 of the Lima and Callao Metro 

This mitigation measu re, under the administration of the MTC, consists of the basic network of the Lima 

Metro, which was approved on December 24, 2010, by Supreme Decree No. 059 -2010 -MTC, and was 

initially planned to be made up of six lines: 

Line 1: Avenida Separadora Industrial, Avenida Pachacútec, Avenida Tomás Marsano, Avenida Aviación, 

Avenida Grau, Jirón Locumba, Avenida 9 de Octubre, Avenida Próceres de la Independencia, and

Line 2: Avenida Guardia Chalaca, Avenida Venez uela, Avenida Arica, Avenida 9 de Diciembre, Avenida 

Paseo de la República, Avenida 28 de Julio, Avenida Nicolás Ayllón, and Avenida Víctor Raúl Haya de la 

Torre (Carretera Central). 

Line 3: Avenida Alfredo Benavides, Avenida Larco, Avenida Arequipa, Aveni da Garcilaso de la Vega, 

Avenida Tacna, Avenida Pizarro, Avenida Túpac Amaru, Avenida Rosa de América, and Avenida 

Universitaria. 

Line 4: Avenida Elmer Faucett, Avenida La Marina, Avenida Sánchez Carrión, Avenida Salaverry, 

Avenida Canevaro, Avenida José P ardo de Zela, Avenida Canadá, Avenida Circunvalación, and Avenida 

Javier Prado. 

Line 5: Avenida Huaylas, Avenida Paseo de la República, Avenida República de Panamá, and Avenida 

Miguel. 

Line 6: Av. Túpac Amaru, Avenida Los Alisos, Avenida Universitaria, Ave nida Bertolotto, Avenida Pérez 

Aranibar (former Avenida del Ejército), Avenida Angamos, and Avenida Primavera. 

This mitigation measure aims to solve the mobility problems faced by Lima and Callao. The high level 

of congestion on roads used for public trans port implies a great loss of man -hours, high levels of 

pollution, and persistent road insecurity, among others. Implementation of this mass transport system 

will allow greater fluidity in terms of mobility for the population and will have a significant pos itive impact 

in environmental terms as the metro line is integrated in the electric energy matrix. 

The measure has one operational line, Line 1, which runs mostly through the metropolitan area on an 

elevated viaduct extending to 35 km and having 26 statio ns. It transports approximately 320,000 

passengers per day, which exceeds the projected demand. 55 On the other hand, Line 2 (27 km) is under 

construction and will cross the metropolitan area from east to west, and the future Line 4 (8 km) that 

will run und er the Elmer Faucett Avenue.

Training in efficient driving for professional drivers 

This mitigation measure, under the administration of the MTC, consists of the application of a series of 

driving techniques a nd maintenance procedures to achieve greater fuel efficiency, which would result 

in reduced GHG emissions. 

In principle, the measure is proposed to be implemented through the national driver’s license system 

and aimed at drivers who opt for or hold the dri ver license types A -IIa, A -IIb, A -IIIa, A -IIIb, and A -IIIc, which 

entitle them to drive cargo or goods transport vehicles within Peruvian territory (category N1 to N3 

vehicles), as indicated in Directive No. 002 -2006 -MTC/15, “Vehicle Classification and Sta ndardization 

of Registrable Vehicle Characteristics.” 

This measure seeks to develop a culture of energy efficiency that will allow drivers and logistics 

companies to identify the benefits in fuel savings and operational costs for transportation, mainly of 

cargo, on national and interdepartmental roads. This will not only reduce GHG emissions and local 

pollutant emissions that are harmful to health but will also result in improved road safety, reduced 

variable costs (repairs, maintenance, tires, and fuel), and increased effectiveness of transport units 

(less downtime for repair and maintenance work). 

The measure will be implemented through the National Regulation of the Driver’s License Issuance 

System, which incorporates the evaluation of knowledge of drivi ng techniques for optimization and 

rationalization of fuel during vehicle activity. Its application is national in character and its 

implementation will be progressive. Drivers will be evaluated on the criteria of “‘efficient driving’” in the 

case of licen se renewals, this being a requirement to issue or deny the driving license or brevete. 

Main benefits 

• Environmental: Reduction of pollutant levels such as nitrogen oxides (NOx), sulfur oxides (SOx), 

particulate matter (PM2.5), and methane (CH 4). 

• Social: (1) Promotion of knowledge among the population on environmental impacts, (2) 

increase in drivers’ technical skills, and (3) enhancement of drivers’ professional careers. 

• Economic: (1) Increase in business competitiveness, and savings in fuel consumption an d

maintenance costs of units. 

Progress made to date 128   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

• On September 15, 2020, the Efficient Driving Guide was published as an instruction manual 

for driving school teachers and instructors, as well as for evaluators at centers issuing driver’s 

licenses, so a s to implement a standardized evaluation mechanism at the national level. 

National program for sustainable urban transportation 

This mitigation measure, under the administration of the MTC, consists of the creation and start -up of 

the National Program for Sustainable Urban Transportation (PROMOVILIDAD), whose general objective 

is to (1) improve the mobility conditions of the population in intermediate cities through the 

implementation of high -quality urban transportation systems that are efficient, reliabl e, respectful of 

users, sustainable, and institutionally coordinated; (2) improve the population’s (especially lower -

income citizens’) access to employment and service areas; and (3) reduce travel times, accidents, and 

environmental pollution, thus contrib uting to an improved quality of life for inhabitants. 

This program considers the sustainable mobility and transportation approach, that is, the 

implementation of a transportation system that is technically feasible and sustainable in economic, 

social, and environmental terms and meets the population’s travel needs in an adequate, efficient, and 

safe manner. 

Main benefits 

• Environmental: Reduction of pollutant levels (NOx, SOx, PM2.5, and CH 4). 

• Social: (1) Improved quality of life; and (2) reduction of traf fic accidents. 

• Economic: Improvement in the: (1) sector’s productivity; (2) efficiency of use of institutional 

resources; and (3) speed of execution of institutional goals. 

Progress made to date 

• Publishing of PROMOVILIDAD and start of the program’s operations within the framework of 

Supreme Decree No. 027 -2019 -MTC. 

• Subscription of framework agreements for interinstitutional cooperation in the urban 

transportation sector between the MTC and the provincial municipalities of Arequipa, Trujillo, 

Cusco, Huamanga, and Piura. 

Na tional program for vehicle scrapping and renewal 

This mitigation measure, under the administration of the MTC, consists of implementing a national 

program for scrapping and renewal of old vehicles so they are no longer in circulation in the market, 

through economic incentives that encourage owners of these vehicles to voluntarily accept the total 

disintegration of their units, eliminating their operability and the possibility of their use in other 

economic activities. 

The measure is proposed in the National Vehicle Regulations, approved by Supreme Decree No. 058 -

2003 -MTC, a general regulatory framework that applies to all categories of vehicles —passenger as well 

as cargo —that, due to their age, are inefficient in terms of GHG emissions (measured in gCO 2eq pe r

person/km and ton/km, respectively). 

This initiative thus contemplates the implementation of a National Program of Vehicle Scrap and 

Renewal, supported by a legal framework that is enabled by the MTC as the governing body, with each 

city articulating an d implementing its own program on vehicle renewal needs. For a first stage of 

implementation and calculation of the potential of the measure, all those units that make up the cargo 

transport will be considered. 

Main benefits 129   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

• Environmental: Reduction of p ollutant levels (NOx, SOx, PM2.5, and CH 4) by withdrawing aged 

vehicles from the market, as they are one of the main sources of pollution in the sector. 

• Social: (1) Improvement of the quality of life (as harmful emissions impact the health of the 

populatio n and air quality); and (2) reduction of traffic accidents. 

• Economic: Improvement in the: (1) sector’s productivity and competitiveness and also the 

country’s GDP; (2) efficiency of use of institutional resources; and (3) speed of execution of 

institution al goals. 

Progress made to date 

• Publication of Emergency Decree No. 029 -2019 that establishes incentives for the promotion 

of scrap metal. 

• In 2020, the draft Supreme Decree that approved the Regulations of Emergency Decree No. 

029 -2019, National Regulation s for the Promotion of Scrap Metal, was published by Ministerial 

Resolution No. 0325 - 2020 -MTC/01.02. 

• The publication of this measure enabled its sharing with public and private sector actors, and 

their comments and suggestions helped improved it. 

“Constru ction of the Trans -Andean Tunnel” Project 

This mitigation measure, under the administration of the MTC, consists of optimizing the current and 

projected transportation of cargo and passengers on the railroad line known as the Ferrocarril Central 

del Perú ( Central Railroad of Peru), which comprises the Callao –La Oroya –Huancayo/La Oroya –Cerro 

de Pasco sections. It was granted in September 1999 to the consortium Ferrovías Central Andino S.A., 

for a period of 40 years, for the operation and maintenance of rail infrastructure, rolling stock, 

workshops, and other reversible assets. 

It is worth mentioning that the Central Highway (Carretera Central) is 310 km long between Lima and 

Huancayo and has only two lanes along its entire length (the urban sections have four ), which are 

insufficient for urban, interprovincial, and interregional traffic. Topographical limitations of the area also 

limit investments to open more two -way lanes to allow vehicles to pass one another, and to improve 

the radii of the curves to avoid congestion and reduce the accident rate. 

The Central Railroad has only one railroad track, linking the port of Callao –Lima –La Oroya –Huancayo 

with a main branch line (La Oroya –Pasco) and a secondary branch line (Huancayo -Huancavelica). There 

is also an old route that cannot be used efficiently since switchbacks are in places to gain altitude. 

In this sense, both the Central Highway and the Central Railroad must complement each other to form 

an intermodal system that will provide a solution to the transport ation problem through construction of 

the Trans -Andean Tunnel (Túnel Trasandino). 

Main benefits 

• Environmental: Reduction of GHG emissions due to the decrease in the projected vehicle fleet 

(cargo trucks and passenger buses). 

• Social: (1) Decongestion of traffic on the Central Highway (Carretera Central); (2) reduction of 

traffic accidents; and (3) reduction of tra vel time. 

• Economic: (1) Increase in employment; (2) reduction in travel costs; and (3) diesel fuel savings 

generated by the construction of tunnels that will save time and distance for the transportation 

of cargo and passengers. 

Progress made to date 

• Execu tion of a pre -investment feasibility study for the “Construction of the Trans -Andean 

Tunnel” project. 130   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Improvement of the railroad transportation service in the Tacna -Arica section 

The Tacna -Arica railroad is a Peruvian state -owned railroad that provides transportation services 

between the city of Tacna (Peru) and Arica (Chile), with a length of 62 km and a gauge of 1,435 

millimeters. It is currently managed by the regional government of Tacna and is partially operational. 

The project will be responsible for improving the current quality and safety standards in relation to track 

infrastructure, rolling stock, and other systems, with modern trains that reach a higher commercial 

speed and have more capacity (more seats for passengers) . In addition, it will reduce the negative 

externalities of land transportation, decrease local pollution, and increase employment in its area of 

influence. 

Cargo transportation has not been considered in this measure —it is focused on improving rail 

transpo rtation, to reduce the number of passengers that travel using other modes of transport (bus, 

shared taxi, private vehicle). 

This measure arises from the existence of a supply -demand gap in the area of influence —that is, there 

is a deficit in the supply o f railroad transportation (not all rail cars are operational) while there is an 

increase in passenger demand for this mode of transportation (interurban and urban services). This 

deficit is also seen in cargo transportation services. 

Main benefits 

• Environ mental: Reduction of air pollution. 

• Social: (1) Shorter trips for rail and road passengers; (2) greater safety in rail transportation 

and reduction in the number of traffic accidents; and (3) greater cultural exchanges. 

• Economic: (1) Shorter cargo trips an d savings in road infrastructure maintenance due to traffic 

reduction; (2) economic benefits generated by tourists; and (3) savings due to loss prevention. 

Integral rehabilitation of the Huancayo -Huancavelica Railroad 

The Huancayo -Huancavelica railroad is located in the central highlands of Peru and provides 

transportation services between the capital cities of the Junin and Huancavelica regions. This mitigation 

measure —which consists of renewing the existing tracks, refurbishing stations and stops, acquir ing 

rolling stock, and establishing the operating model —aims to provide a safe, efficient, and comfortable 

rail transportation service for users. 

Main benefits 

• Environmental: Decrease in air pollution. 

• Social: (1) Shorter travel times for passengers captured from the road mode (intercity buses 

and light vehicles); (2) greater safety in rail transport and a reduction in the number of traffic 

accidents; and (3) greater cultural exchanges. 

• Economic: (1) Time savin gs in transporting cargo via railroad (instead of the current mode of 

cargo trucks); (2) savings in road infrastructure maintenance and vehicle operating costs due 

to traffic reduction; (3) economic benefits generated by tourists; and (4) savings due to th e

prevention of losses caused by natural phenomena. 

Promotion of natural gas vehicles (NGVs) for light vehicles 

This mitigation measure, under the administration of MINEM, proposes the conversion of gasoline -

powered automobiles to NGVs through the Natural Gas Financing and Conversion Program. The 

replacement of gasoline, which emits large amounts of CO 2, with a more environmentally friendly, 

sulfur -free fuel will reduce GHG emissions. 131   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

It will be implemented through a natural gas massification project focuse d on vehicle use, and it is 

expected that by 2030 there will be 202,444 NGVs. 

Main benefits 

• Environmental: Lower concentration of sulfur pollutants and particulate matter. 

• Social: Creation of jobs. 

• Economic: Higher NGV performance (approximately 13 percen t more than gasoline) and 

significant savings of 53 percent in relation to the price of gasoline. 

Progress made to date

the programs aimed at expanding universal access to energy supply and rural energization.

creates the Natural Gas Manager.” 

Promotion of the use of cleaner fuels 

This mitigation measure is administered under MINEM an d aims to reduce sulfur levels in fuels (diesel, 

gasoline, and high -octane gasohol), improving the quality of life of the population and safeguarding air 

quality and public health. The application of this measure optimizes the efficiency of the combustion 

engine, since lower fuel consumption results in lower GHG emissions. 

Two mechanisms of action are proposed for this purpose: 

• Prohibit the trade and use of B5 diesel with a sulfur content of more than 50 parts per million 

(ppm); and 

• Regulate sulfur conten t in 95, 97, and 98 octane gasoline and gasohol (to not exceed 50 ppm). 

Main benefits 

• Environmental: Optimizes air quality due to the reduction of local pollutants (NOx and sulfur 

dioxide). 

• Social: Improves the quality of life of the population. 

• Economic: Extends the useful life of vehicle engines through the use of a quality fuel. 

Progress made to dat e

• Resolution of the Board of Directors of the Supervisory Agency for Investment in Energy and 

Mining (OSINERGMIN) No. 051 -2020 -OSC/CD, “Approval of a transitory measure of exception 

to the obligation to modify the data of the Hydrocarbons Registry to acqui re and store or 

commercialize diesel B5 S -50 in replacement of diesel B5, for the Holders of Establishments 

selling liquid fuels to the public and direct consumers, located in the departments of Piura, 

Tumbes, Amazonas and San Martin.” 

Promotion of electr ic vehicles at the national level 

This mitigation measure, under the administration of MINEM, proposes replacing the energy matrix 

used in transportation (gasoline, diesel, liquefied petroleum gas, and natural gas vehicles) with electric 

vehicles to promot e the use of clean technologies and minimize the environmental impact. 

The goal, in the first stage, is to introduce electric buses and light electric cars, through a package of 

proposals including regulatory changes and incentives, nationwide disseminati on programs, 

development of electric infrastructure for vehicle charging, and access to financing mechanisms (for 132

Promotion of liquefied natural ga s (LNG) for cargo transportation within the natural gas massification

Energy efficiency labeling for light vehicles 

This mitigation measure, under the administration of MINEM, proposes the implementation of an 

energy efficiency labeling system for all light vehicles weighing less than 2,700 kilograms. This measure 

will ensure the delivery of strategic information (energy consumption, performance, and emissions 

generated) to hel p users in their decision -making, thus reducing fuel consumption and the associated 

GHG emissions. 

The mitigation measure proposes four main lines of action for its implementation: 

• Strengthen technical capacities in the energy labeling of light vehicles. 

• Improve the availability and quality of information on energy efficiency in light vehicles. 

• Develop means for implementation of homologation tests. 133

Emission reduction gap in the transportation sector 

According to the official results o f the RAGEI 2016, of the 21.04 MtCO2 emissions issued by the 

transportation sector, 92 percent are produced by land transportation. In order to relate emissions to 

the type of service performed (e.g., private, public, passenger, or cargo transport), the IP CC 

methodology classifies vehicle units according to their passenger or cargo capacity (detailed in table 

F.6).

The results by show that light -duty trucks contribute 30 percent of the emissions from land 

transportation, while heavy -duty truc ks and buses contribute 27.4 percent. Heavy -duty trucks are 

further divided into two subcategories: (1) cargo transportation vehicles (bus and truck) and (2) 

passenger transportation vehicles (bus). The participation of both subcategories indicates that wh ile 

freight transport accounts for 20 percent of land transport emissions, buses (public and passenger 

transport) contribute 7 percent. 

It is interesting to note that emissions from automobiles (private cars and taxis) and motorcycles 

represent 43 percent of total land transportation emissions, which indicates that mitigation policies to 

reduce private transportation, such as incentives for electromobility, would help reduce emissions.

F.2.4. Resilience and emergency response system in the event of natural disasters 

As mentioned above, in addition to the climate change policies implemented at the national level, the 

government is also developing a platform for m anagement instruments to organize and react quickly 

in the event of a natural disaster (that are recurring every year throughout the national territory ). In 

2011, the government enacted Law No. 29664 (Government of Peru 2011), creating the National 

Disaste r Risk Management System (SINAGERD) —an interinstitutional, synergic, decentralized, cross -

cutting, and participatory system that would identify, reduce, and prevent risks, as well as develop 

measures to be prepared to respond to them. 

However, the main sec toral and territorial planning instrument is the National Plan for Disaster Risk 

Management (PLANAGERD) 2014 –21. 56 This is SINAGERD’s strategic planning document for 

implementing the National Policy for Disaster Risk Management, which aims to establish str ategic 

actions for disaster risk management (DRM) and cross -cutting coordination at all governmental levels 

in accordance with the country’s decentralization process. The implementation of PLANAGERD thus 

reduces the vulnerability of the population and thei r livelihoods in the case of disasters. 57 

The regulation of the law establishing SINAGERD states that public entities at three governmental levels 

must form a Working Group for Disaster Risk Management (GTGRD), which should conduct all DRM 

processes within the scope of its competency. The GTGRD would thus be the space where norms and 

plans are formulated, and DRM processes are evaluated and organized (Government of Peru 2011).      

> 56 The PLANAGERD (approved by S.D. No. 034 -2014 -PCM of May 13, 2014) is a joint work between the Secretariat for Disaster Risk Management (SGRD,
> from the Presidency of the Council of Ministers); the National Center for Estimation, Prevention and Reduction of Disaster Ri sks (CENEPRED); the National
> Institute of Civil Defense (INDECI); the M inistry of Economy and Finance (MEF); the National Center for Strategic Planning (CEPLAN); other SINAGERD
> members; as well as other stakeholders involved in participatory workshops.
> 57 Six main objectives have been established: to (1) develop risk knowledg e; (2) avoid and reduce the risk conditions of the population’s livelihoods using
> a territorial approach; (3) develop responsiveness in case of emergencies and disasters; (4) strengthen the capacity for phys ical, economic, and social
> recovery; (5) strength en institutional capacities for the development of disaster risk management; and (6) strengthen the participation of the popu lation
> and society for the development of a prevention culture. 136
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

At the transportation level, the MTC’s DRM Working Group has 24 members includin g general directors, 

vice ministers, and the minister, who ultimately presides over the group. They are supported by a 

technical team (made up of representatives of group members) for operational activities. Its roles 

regarding general management resolutio n (RDG ) processes can be divided into: (1) the formulation of 

standards and plans; (2) planning, implementation, and execution; and (3) reporting on progress. 58 

The GTGRD -MTC has written out its responsibilities and competencies in the Sectoral Plan for Di saster 

Risk Management (PSGRD 2018 –21) and other management instruments. The PSGRD determines 

and establishes the sectoral objective, strategic objectives, specific objectives, and strategic actions, 

with regard to PLANAGERD 2014 –21 (MTC 2020a) . These mana gement instruments have been 

approved by the ministry only since 2017 (three years after PLANAGERD’s approval). This delay is partly 

because the MTC started to reorganize its organizational structure in March 2019, when the Integrated 

Text of its Organizat ion and Functions Regulation (ROF) was finally approved. Consequently, the 

management documents approved until that date had to be updated, which further delayed its 

implementation.

In terms of reactive and corrective risk management, the MTC has made significant progress in the 

development of plans and functions that allow the sector to quickly respond to emergencies. Within the 

framework of the GTGRD, several management instruments have been approved to develop effective 

responses to emergency or disaster situations such as the Operational Continuity Plan PCO -MTC (MTC 

2020c) and the Sectoral Emergency Operations Plan PSOE -MTC (MTC 2020b). The PCO -MTC 

establishes operating procedures for the entity’s main activities and services in the event of a major 

disaster affecting the operations of the Head Office. The PSOE -MTC assigns roles and responsibilities 

to the GTGRD -MTC members in the event of an emergency, a disaster, or im minent danger affecting or 

destroying the road and/or communications infrastructure.    

> 58 GTGRD -MTC’s Minutes of Meeting No. 003 -2019, which approved the I nternal Operating Regulations of the Working Group for Disaster Risk
> Management of the Ministry of Transport and Communications. R.M. No. 150 -2019 -MTC/01, amending Art. 01 of R.M. No. 678 -2017 -MTC/01. 137
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Equally, contingency plans complement the PSOE -MTC’s roles by establishing sectoral guidelines for 

specific events, for example, MTC’s Sectoral Contingency Plan for Inte nse Rainfall (MTC 2019b) 59 and 

Sectoral Contingency Plan for a Major Earthquake followed by a tsunami off Peru’s central coast (MTC 

2019c). In summary, table F.7 presents the management instruments defined and approved by the 

MTC within the SINAGERD framew ork. 

Despite the efforts already implemented at the sectoral level, it is important to strengthen preventive 

and territorial approaches in DRM and develop an implementation strategy for the PSGRD. Progress in 

preventive and corrective management is still i ncipient, even though the MTC has made significant 

progress in aspects such as development of reactive management plans, and the DRM working groups 

have made credible efforts to incorporate a preventive approach nationally. This supports enforcement 

of reg ulations in the post -investment phase . A starting point is the ongoing preparation of the Disaster 

Risk Prevention and Reduction Plan (PPRRD) and the vulnerability maps, with technical assistance from 

the National Center for Estimation, Prevention and Redu ction of Disaster Risks (CENEPRED). However, 

progress is subject to budgetary and agenda issues. Besides, although MTC has protocols for reactive 

management, it considers territorial actors purely as information agents, and lacks the approach of an 

interin stitutional and intergovernmental organization. Therefore, MTC has been working on a new 

coordination model through establishment of territorial decentralized centers (CDTs), which are 

considered the coordination and management units linking regional and l ocal governments. However, 

this is a new process that cannot be evaluated as yet. Finally, it is necessary to develop timely 

monitoring and follow -up mechanisms to evaluate the progress of actions —all this as part of a PSGRD 

implementation strategy. 

DRM wi ll thus be strengthened and enhanced by operational actions, the definition of a baseline, and 

the development of instruments. These instruments will help to improve the plan for monitoring, follow -

up, and evaluation of risks. Currently, although the PSGRD details MTC’s responsibilities and 

competencies, there are still lags because the ministry went through an adjustment of its organizational 

structure that was completed only in March 2019. 

Finally, it is important to include the PPRRD strategy in the Mu ltiannual Investment Programming 

through measurable and monitorable indicators in order to prioritize resilient infrastructure projects. 

Entities’ multiannual programming offices, as well as executing units, should include DRM as a tool for 

prioritizing in vestment projects. Data on recurring natural and climatological phenomena show that 

disasters are becoming more frequent; therefore, it is important to standardize disaster risk 

assessments. Also, public investment must be increased so that the country is not stuck in a 

reconstruction loop. 

## F.3. Electromobility in the Peruvian transportation sector 

The nine mitigation measures adopted by the Ministry of Transport and Communication (MTC) would 

reduce the sector’s GHG emissions by only 1.08 MtCO 2 by 2030. This represents only 9 percent of the 

total reduction in emissions (12.2 MtCO 2) that the transport sector should reach under the new 

emissions target of 179 MtCO 2. 60      

> 59 According to the Sector Contingency Plan, the hydrome teorological phenomena with the greatest impact on the sector in recent decades are: (1) the El
> Niño phenomenon during 1982 –83, which affected the country’s northern part with rains and droughts; (2) the El Niño phenomenon of 1997 –98, which
> was considered a mega event and affected the country’s northern, central, and southern parts; (3) floods in the country’s highlands and jung les in 2004
> and 2010 in Puno, in 2006 in San Martin, in 2007 in Junín, in 2010 in Cusco, in 2011 in Ucayali, and in 2012 in Ayacuch o and Loreto; and (4) the 2017
> coastal El Niño phenomenon, which affected the departments of Tumbes, Lambayeque, Piura, La Libertad, Ancash (Casma and Huarm ey were severely
> affected), Lima (Metropolitan Lima: Chaclacayo, San Juan de Lurigancho, Cieneguilla , Punta Hermosa, Huachipa, Pucusana, and Rimac; and Lima
> Provinces: Canta, Huaral, Huarochirí, and Yauyos), Ica, Arequipa, and regions located far from the coast such as Cajamarca, H uánuco, and Junín .
> 60 The transport sector’s share of total emissions is e xpected to remain steady at around 10 percent of the Greenhouse Gas Inventory by 2030. 138
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Considering the estimated emissions gap by 2030 of the transportation sector (1 1.1 MtCO 2), 61 it is 

necessary that the MTC promote policies and complementary measures that help to reduce 

consumption of fossil fuel quickly, since this is the main cause of GHG emissions. While the latter 

section of this report discusses different initia tives concerning road cargo transportation (section F. 4), 

the MTC must accelerate and promote the electrification of its public and private passenger transport 

fleet. 

Electromobility refers to the use of driving or traction systems that use electric energ y. This can apply 

to different means of transportation. On one hand, internal combustion vehicles operate by a chemical 

process that combines fuel and air, and releases energy afterwards. On the other hand, battery electric 

vehicles are driven by a motor p ropulsion system that uses electricity as their energy source. 

Thus, electric vehicles release lower emissions since they do not burn fuel internally. According to 

several studies by the International Energy Agency, an electric vehicle with a medium -sized battery 

emits 60 percent less CO 2eq per kilometer traveled compared to a gasoline -powered vehicle. 

Because of electric mobility’s great benefits, as well as the major technological advancement and digital 

transformation of construction processes, large v ehicle manufacturers have started to switch their 

production lines to electrified vehicles. Thus, internal combustion engine vehicles will gradually be 

replaced by electric vehicles in the coming years. 

However, the manufacture and production costs of ele ctric propulsion systems are higher, making the 

final price of electric vehicles higher than that of internal combustion vehicles. According to estimations 

by Bloomberg New Energy Finance’s Electric Vehicle Outlook Report, prices and performance of both 

ty pes of vehicles will be the same only by 2050. 

Currently, national policies are addressing the global transition toward electromobility, and important 

progress has been made in countries of the region such as Chile, Brazil, and Colombia . However, in 

Peru, the development of electromobility is still nascent, with most of Peru’s vehicle fleet composed of 

internal combustion vehicles that consume gasoline, diesel, liquefied petroleum gas, and natural gas.
