This section presents the main policies implemented in the six countries that have been promoting 

electric mobility. It also describes MTC’s efforts in promoting the electrification of Peru’s passenger 

transportation fleet. 

F.3.1. Experiences of promoting electromobility in the world and in the region 

The following aspects are considered as the benchmark experience in electromobility ( OSINERGMIN 

2019) :

• Norway was selected as the leading country in the adoption of electric mobility. As of 2020, 50 

percent of all sold vehicles were electric. 

• Among the European Union countries, Spain was selected as the country with the regulatory 

model closest to Peru. 

• From the Latin American region, the following countries were selected: Mexico, Chile, Colombia, 

and Ecuador. 

Norway 

Policies to promote electric mobility began in 1990, when electric vehicles were exempted from paying 

registration tax (also known as impor t tax). This was a key factor impacting the purchase cost and 

reducing the purchase price of electric vehicles in comparison to other technologies. 

Between 1990 and 2008, several policies and incentive programs were implemented. For example, 

the circulati on tax for electric vehicles was reduced by 75 percent of the original rate, the tax for private 

vehicle use by 50 percent, and the value added tax (VAT) by 25 percent. During the same period, 

operating costs incentives were implemented, such as reduction in parking fees and exemption from 

road tolls for electric vehicles since 1997. 

The combination of policies encouraging people to acquire and operate this type of vehicle contributed 

to the steady sales growth of electric vehicles. In 2020, 50 percent of all sold vehicles were electric 

vehicles. 

Spain 

Policies for electric mobility in Spain began in 2008, when all electric vehicles were exempted from 

paying registration tax. Subsequently, in 2009, Law 19/2009 was passed, which established measures 

to prom ote and speed up the installation of charging points for “plug -in” vehicles in private buildings’ 

parking lots. In 2011, load manager activities for energy charging services were regulated. The new 

regulation strengthened the promotion policy for the devel opment of charging stations. 

In 2017 the Movalt Plan was launched —the first specific plan for the promotion of electric and plug -in 

hybrid vehicles. Of the plan’s €35 million allocation, €20 million was earmarked for the purchase of 

“alternative” vehicles , and €15 million for the installation of charging infrastructure. Subsidies for 

alternative vehicles, ranging from €500 to €18,000, amounted to €20 million. 

In 2018, traffic restriction policies were implemented. For example, the Madrid Central Program 

restricted vehicles that did not carry the zero -emission environmental badge from circulating in 

regulated parking service zones. 

These policies for promoting electromobility allowed Spain to experience a steady growth in the sales 

of electric vehicles and plug -in hybrids, from 2018 onward. 140   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

This growth was supported by the implementation of Plan Moves in 2018, whose main objective was 

to provide incentives for the purchase of alternative vehicles. Plan Moves had a budget of €45 million 

for the purchase of such vehicles and €10 million for the construction of charging stations. In 2020, 

Plan Moves was renewed —this time with a budget of €100 million. 

Mexico 

Electric mobility policies and incentives in Mexico began in 2014 with the approval of the Special Tax 

Law on Production and Services. According to this law, different fossil fuels were assigned different tax 

rates according to the amount of CO 2 they emitted. In the same year, the government also approved 

exemptions on taxes for new electric vehicles and on annual property taxes for electric vehicles. 

Besides the reduction in acquisition costs, in 2014 electric and hybrid vehicles were exempted from 

vehicle restrictions 62 through the “Hoy no Circula” program. In 2016, the government approved an 

income tax d eduction for purchase (up to Mex$250,000) or lease of electric or hybrid vehicles, and a 

30 p ercent tax credit on investments in charging station equipment. 

Operating costs decreased, with a 20 percent reduction in toll payments for electric and hybrid ve hicles 

on certain highways in Mexico City in 2017. Finally, in 2020, new imported electric vehicles were given 

temporary tariff exemptions. By the end of 2020, sales of electrified vehicles had increased by 8 

percent. 

Colombia 

Electromobility policies st arted in Colombia in 2012 with the reduction of VAT from 16 percent to 5 

percent for cabs, and electric and hybrid buses. In 2013, the Technological Ascent Plan was released, 

which aimed to replace fossil fuel vehicles and promote electric energy vehicles for public transport 

services, through the implementation of zero -emission technologies in the Transmilenio fleet and the 

Integrated Public Transport System. 

Subsequently, Law 1819 was approved in 2016, which retained the reduction in VAT (5 percent inste ad 

of 16 percent) and proposed a carbon tax. This tax assigned a different rate to each type of fuel 

according to the amount of CO 2 released during combustion. 

In 2017, the customs tariff was modified, with a 0 percent rate for imports of electric vehicle s and a 5 

percent rate for hybrid vehicles. In 2019, different incentives were approved for promoting electric 

mobility, such as (1) discounts for vehicle technical inspections, (2) exemptions from vehicle 

restrictions, (3) tax rates of motor vehicles and the related annual payments not to exceed 1 percent 

of their commercial value. 

Finally, the Bogota Marshall Plan was published in 2020, which proposed a tax discount of up to 70 

percent on the motor vehicle tax for new electric and hybrid vehicles. 

Chile 

For Chile, the implementation of electromobility policies began in 2014 with the publication of Law 

20.708. This law introduces the green tax for all new light and medium vehicles, except electric and 

hybrid vehicles.     

> 62 The “Hoy no Circula” program is a measure that every day prohibits the circulation of vehicles ending with a certain license plate number. Electric
> vehicles are exempted from this measure and can circulate every day. 141
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

In 2016, a vehicle labeling program was planned to give users information on the type of vehicle and 

its technology, and the fuel and energy efficiency of each vehicle on sale. 

In 2017, the National Electromobility Strategy was published, which aimed at replacing conventional 

vehicles. It also launched the “Renueva tu colectivo” program, which gave away a scrap voucher for the 

renewal of collective cabs. The number of parking spaces with appropriate charging infrastructure. 

In 2018, incentive vouchers were distributed for purchase of electric and hybrid buses in public 

transport bus concessions. Guidelines were also published for the installation and commi ssioning of 

charging infrastructure services. These guidelines were promoted through public -private partnerships 

in 2019. 

Finally, in 2020, incentive policies were released exempting electric vehicles from traffic restrictions. 

Ecuador 

In Ecuador, incen tive policies for electromobility started in 2017 with the publication of the Technical 

Regulation PRTE -INEN -162 for charging infrastructure specifications. The regulation stated procedures 

and technical requirements for the correct installation of chargin g services. 

In 2018, the organic law for Productive Promotion, Investment Attraction, Employment Generation, 

Fiscal Stability, and Balance was published. This law levied a 0 percent rate of VAT and selective 

consumption tax (ISC) on all electric vehicles for private use, as well as for passenger and cargo 

transportation. 

Additionally, a new incentive rule for promoting purchase of electric vehicles was implemented and the 

import tariff was reduced to 0 percent. A new tariff scheme was approved, which appl ied discounts 

according to the voltage schedule. In addition, the Organic Law of Energy Efficiency mandated that all 

vehicles incorporated into the transportation systems must be electric and will be given preferential 

energy rates, as of 2025. 

Finally, i n 2020, the National Transit Agency (ANT) established the homologation process for electric 

passenger and cargo vehicles. 142

F.3.2. Progress of and opportunities for electromobility in Peru 

Compared to several countries in the region, Peru has not made sufficient progress in the development 

and implementation of incentives and policies to promote electromobility in the transportation sector. 

However, in recent years some actions approved by t he state have been carried out. Additionally, pilot 

project initiatives by the private sector intend to identify other policies or possibilities that could be 

implemented on a larger scale through different programs. 

In Peru, incentive policies for electro mobility started in 2018, when tax rates for fuel and vehicles were 

modified. Through Supreme Decree No. 094 -2018 -EF, the MEF, in cooperation with MINAM, imposed taxes 

on fuels depending on their degree of harmfulness. So, though 97 octane gasohol used to pay more ISCs 

than diesel earlier, the tax rates were revised because the latter was more harmful. Considering that the 

current vehicle fleet runs mostly on fossil fuels, this measure seeks to redirect consumers toward less -

polluting options to reduce CO 2 emissions. 

Additionally, through the publication of Supreme Decree No. 095 -2018 -EF, and because new electric, gas, 

and hybrid vehicles were not mentioned , the ISC was practically suppressed . A rate of 10 percent is 

applicable on new gasoline vehicles and 2 0 percent on new diesel or semi -diesel vehicles, so users acquire 

fewer polluting vehicles. 

At the end of 2018, the Peruvian government modified the National Vehicle Regulation (RNV) through 

Supreme Decree No. 019 -2018 -MTC, aiming to integrate vehicles ma nufactured with modern technology 

and driven by clean energies (such as electric cars and pedal -assist bicycles) into the vehicle fleet. Likewise, 

a National Vehicle Homologation System was adopted, supported by the National Vehicle Homologation 

Registry. The regulation indicates that this registry would be a crucial tool for the state to verify whether 

new -model vehicles (whether imported, manufactured, or assembled in the country) comply with 

environmental, energy efficiency, and road safety standards. 

Am ong the public -private actions, an important one was the establishment of the electric public transport 

bus lane in San Isidro in 2018. In cooperation with the electric company Engie Peru, the Pilot Plan of the 

MiBus Project was implemented. Besides, in 20 19, Engie Peru introduced the first 100 percent electric bus 

in the district of Hualgayoc (Cajamarca), which transported personnel of the Cerro Corona mine (belonging 

to Gold Fields). 144   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Figure F.1 3 Actions for promotion of electromobility in Peru

private and public passenger transportation, the ministry proposes to introduce 6,700 buses and 171,300 

light electric vehicles (5 percent of the total vehicle fleet). 

Though the MTC and MINEM has implemented improveme nts in the regulatory framework over the last two 

years, it is still important to come up with greater incentives not only for the acquisition of vehicles, but also 

for their operation and circulation. 

Emissions by vehicle category show that both automobi les and motorcycles represent approximately 43 

percent of total land transport emissions at the national level. Since both vehicle categories mostly 

represent trips made privately, mitigation policies aimed at promoting electromobility will have a substant ial 

impact on reducing the gap in GHG emissions related to transportation. 145

## F.4. Freight transportation: opportunities for GHG emissions reduction 

As mentioned in section F. 2, the mitigation measures approved for the transportation sector are mainly 

focused on the reduction of mobile combustion emissions from private and public passenger 

transportation. Thus, this section focuses mainly on mitigation policies aimed at reducin g emissions 

from freight and goods transportation by road. 

Subsection F. 4.1 focuses on the characterization of land transportation and its share in national GHG 

emissions; subsection F. 4.2 details the situation of transportation infrastructure as well as the flow of 

cargo and passenger movement by mode of transportation; and, finally, subsection F. 4.3 focuses on 

mitigation proposals for the reduction of emissions in cargo and goods transportation. 

F.4.1. C haracterization of the transportation sector 

Acco rding to the IPCC calculation methodology, GHG emissions from the transportation sector are 

caused by the consumption of mobile combustion energy. This energy comes from the burning of fossil 

fuels by the vehicle fleet during transportation of cargo and pa ssengers at the national level. Thus, GHG 

emissions are intrinsically related to the use of transportation, and to the economic growth experienced 

by the country. 

According to INGEI estimations, GHG emissions from the transportation sector reached 21.04 Mt CO 2eq 

in 2016, representing 10 percent of all Peru’s emissions . Furthermore, 92 percent of emissions came 

from land transport, 5 percent from civil aviation, 2 percent from sea transport, 1 percent from rail 

transport, and 0.1 from others. 63 

Passenger tran sportation refers to the regular transportation of people: private and public, national 

(between regions and/or provinces), and provincial (urban transportation). According to the MTC, in the 

period 2011 –19, passenger transportation grew at an average rate of 4 percent per year, reaching 

115.41 million passengers in 2019. However, in 2020, due to the COVID -19 emergency, passenger 

transportation experienced a 60 percent reduction (only 45.8 million passengers) compared to the 

previous year. Of the total numb er of passengers, 82 percent were transported by land, 17 percent by 

air, and 1 percent by rail . 

> 63 The “Others” category relates to GHG emissions from all -terrain transportation, including mobile vehicles used in the seaport of Callao and the
> Peruvian airports.

Among precious minerals, gold is transported mainly from the departments of Cajamarca, La Libertad, 

Madre de Dios, and Arequipa and silver from Pasco, Ancash, Junín, Junín, Arequipa, and Ayacucho. In 

both cases, cargo is moved by road and then leaves the c ountry mainly via the ports of Callao (57 

percent) and Matarani (41 percent), and to a lesser extent through Callao airport (2 percent). 

Figure F.20 Precious minerals logistics chain , 2011   

> Source: MTC’s Transportation Logistics Services Development Plan 152
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Exports in the agriculture sector have experienced remarkable growth, reaching a total of US$7,949 in 

2021, which represents an increase of 35 percent in comparison to 2018. Among the relevant exports 

are fruits and vegetables (13 percent), grapes (16 per cent), avocados (16 percent), and blueberries 

(15 percent), which as a whole represent 57 percent of Peru’s total agricultural exports. 

Driven by the changes in consumption preferences (such as a demand for healthier food), avocado 

exports (mostly of the Hass avocado) have grown by 45 percent in the last four years, with European 

countries as their main destination. Also, according to the MTC’s logistic chains identified in 2011, 

avocado is transported mainly from coastal departments such as Lima, La Liber tad, Junín, and Ica by 

road to the ports of Paita (22 percent) and Callao (77 percent). 

Figure F.21 Avocado logistics chain, 2011 

> Source: MTC’s Transportation Logistics Services Development Plan

Similarly, and given the better climatic conditions that allow grape production throughout the year in 

comparison to other producing countries, the exports of table grapes (especially seedless) have also 

experienced a growth of 53 percent in the last four years, reaching an export value of US$1,256 million 

in 2021. Similar to avocados, this product is transported from the coastal departments of Ica, Lima, La 

Libertad, and Piura by road to the ports of Paita (30 percent) and Callao (65 percent), from where i t is 

exported abroad, mainly to North America and Europe. 153   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

> Source: MTC’s Transportation Logistics Services Development Plan

F.4.2. Cargo and passenger mobilization by mode of transportation 

Air transportation 

Per u currently has 144 operational airport facilities, of which 71 are publicly owned and 73 are privately 

owned. Of the 68 publicly owned airfields, 18 operate through concessionaires. From the remaining 

other 53 airfields, 30 are managed by CORPAC S.A. and 23 are under the administration of subnational 

governments, native communities, the National Police, and the Territorial Air Region of the Peruvian Air 

Force (MTC 2020 d)

Air transportation of passengers 

For years, air passenger transportation has been growing steadily, both domestically and 

internationally —through the period 2016 –19 itself, the number of mobilized passengers increased by 

26 percent. In 2019, it reached a peak of 13.8 million passengers domestically and 12.4 million 

internationally. However, in 2020, air transport registered a 70 percent contraction compared to the 

previous year because of the COVID -19 pandemic, with the number of mobilized passengers decreasing 

from 26.2 million to 7.8 million. 154

Air cargo transportation 

Among air cargo services, 89.7 percent is carried out internationally (in and out of Peru); while only 

10.3 percent is domestic. In recent years, domestic and international air cargo has been decreasing. 

However, there was a 32 percent decrease in 2020 due to the quarantine and closure of airports in 

Peru, resulting from a government measure to prevent the spread of COVID -19.

Cabotage refers to the transportation of goods and containers through Peruvian ports by Peruvian 

vessels. Usually, navigation is carried out near the coast, bordering the territory and without reaching 

the high sea routes. 

Compared to traditional land tra nsportation, cabotage presents great benefits such as a greater 

capacity to move cargo, a reduction in logistics costs, and a potential predictability that helps schedule 

cargo operations . However, despite its great benefits, 89 percent of domestic cargo i s still moved by 

road and less than 1 percent by cabotage. Peruvian people pay a lot more than in regional average 

because of the logistics costs included in the value of the products —approximately 34 percent. 69 This 

situation makes the country one of the most expensive in Latin America (average logistics costs 

included in value of products —24 percent), and well above that of countries of the Organisation for 

Economic Co -operation and Development (with only 8 perc ent) .

Legislative Decrees No. 644 and No. 683 were approved in 1991, and they liberalized the cabotage 

sector in Peru by declaring total freedom of routes and eliminating all legal and administrative 

restrictions that prevented its adequate and efficient o peration. However, they also mandated that            

> 69 Approved by Ministerial Resolution 060/2 019 of January 31, 2019 —found in the MTC's Transportation Logistics Services Development Plan (Lima
> 2019) .
> Ownership Total
> Public property 49
> Not under concession 30
> Given in assignment 8
> Given in concession 8
> ENAPU S.A. 3
> Private property 47
> Total 96 156
> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

cabotage services could be carried out by Peruvian or foreign -flag vessels, only if they are chartered 

(without time limit) or operated by national shipping companies. 

In 2005, Law No. 28583 was passed in order t o promote and reactivate the national merchant marine 

and give greater flexibility to merchant vessels. The law states that commercial water transportation is 

reserved exclusively for Peruvian flagged merchant vessels. However, this regulatory framework di d not 

provide the conditions needed for the growing national fleet, and the shortage of national and local 

shipping supply persisted. According to the MTC, between 2001 and 2017, only 21 domestic vessels 

were authorized and registered. Of these, 19 transpo rted liquid bulk, 1 transported container vessels, 

and 1 transported passengers .

Though Law No. 28583 allowed for a better transportation of hydrocarbons, there is still a lack of national 

maritime transit services for general cargo and containers. This si tuation does not allow for adequate 

connectivity between the main port of the country (Port of Callao) and the secondary ports of the Peruvian 

coast. 

Therefore, in 2018, the MTC approved Legislative Decree No. 1413 as the new regulatory framework 

to fulfi ll the needs of national shipping companies and promote a competitive alternative for cargo 

transportation on the Peruvian coast .

Since enactment of this decree, the MTC has made several efforts to promote development of the 

cabotage market, such as provid ing cargo carriers the opportunity to carry out a modal exchange of 

goods: from road to ship. The ministry has brought together shipping companies and cargo owners for 

development of cabotage operations. They have stated that cabotage services should be ma naged by 

logistics operators, since they provide door -to -door operational facilities. These logistics operators are 

also in charge of managing the entire chain of payments to the different operators involved in the 

cabotage logistics chain. 

Besides, the MT C has proposed an institutional strategy encouraging new companies to develop 

maritime cabotage, as a competitive alternative for cargo transportation alternative on the Peruvian 

coast. To this end, three subobjectives have been determined (table F.14 ). 157   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Table F.14 MTC’s objectives to promote maritime cabotage 

Objectives  Actions 

1. Modify the regulatory framework  Through Legislative Decree No. 1413 from 2018, the regulation of Law No. 28583 was 

amended to promote and ease maritime transportation in cabotage traffic. This would 

eventually generate a competitive alternative for cargo transportation on the Peruvian 

coast. 

2. Simplify processes and establish clear rules 

for vessel certification and cabotage 

operations 

With Supreme Decree No. 029 -2019, cabotage was regulated and subsequently assigned 

to different entities so they could improve it: 

• Operational Technical Standard for the Treatment of Domestic Cabotage Cargo in 

Peruvian ports (APN). 

• Customs procedures were improved to include maritime cabotage of cargo calling 

at international ports (SUNAT —Customs). 

• Customs procedure for internal maritime transit was updated. 

• Certificates of transport safety conditions were issued, to authorize shipping 

companies to carry o ut cabotage operations. 

3. Promote the benefits of this transportation 

alternative to the target public (stakeholders 

involved in merchant shipping, entrepreneurs, 

micro and medium -sized enterprises, and 

citizens) 

A strategy was implemented to promote the regulations and benefits of maritime 

cabotage to the target audience and the entire country. This strategy included: 

• Dissemination workshops through digital platforms 

• Media interviews in the regions 

• Meetings with companies 

Source: MTC 

These actions have stimulated economic growth of the four ports through seven regular coastal 

shipping frequencies, operating with four vessels of three companies, as of 2021. Also, 20 other 

companies have benefited from maritime cabotage as they have mana ged to mobilize 419 containers 

equivalent to 11,313 tons of cargo.

land to sea transport in the short term. It is important to keep the flow of operations growing between 

ports, so transportation companies can better predict growth. Increasing the flow of operations will also 

simplify cargo loading/unloading processes. 

Rail transportation 

Peru’s railroad network is 1,939.7 km long. Its eight operational railroad lines are under the 

management of different public (88 percent) and private (12 percent) companies. Additionally, 89 

percent of the state -owned railway network is under concession, while the remaining 11 percent is 

managed by the MTC (Huancayo -Huancavelica Railway) and the Regional Government of Tacna (Tacna -

Arica Railway). According to the MTC, the country’s operational rail network has remained steady since 

2014 (MTC 2020).

infrastructure. Besides, as of 2016, freight traffic increased significantly in the Southern Section 

(Matarani -Cusco) of the Southeast Railroad due to the start of commercial opera tions of the “Las

The Southern Railway transports 51 per cent of its volume through rail infrastructure given in

National plan for railway development 

R.M. No. 396 -2016 -MTC/01.01 of 2016 approved the National Railway Development Plan for railway 

projects under development and construction. This plan establishes policies and strategy guidelines to 

optimize the logistics costs of cargo and passenger transportation in Peru. 

Following are the railway projects being developed by MTC’s General Directorate of Transportation 

Programs and Projects: 

The Huancayo -Huancavelica train (estimated investment US$284 million) is currently being managed 

by the MTC, but is under a bidding process for awarding of the concession. Its length is 128.7 km, and 

it is one of the highest -altitude railroads in the world. According to the contract pro posal, the 

concessionaire will be responsible for the design, financing, construction, and operation of the system. 

The train will benefit more than 250,000 people, transporting them to their workplaces, educational 

institutions, and health centers. 

The L ima -Ica Commuter Train System (estimated investment US$ 3,263.9 million for 324 km of train 

line) is expected to benefit 9.32 million people ( MTC 2017) . In August 2019, the MTC called for a public 

bid for preparation of preinvestment studies of the Lima -Ica Railroad. The resulting contract was 

awarded in November 2019, and was signed in December 2019 with the Lima -Ica Train Consortium 

formed by the the co mpanies: Fynsa Ingenieros SAC, Vitrubio Ingenieria SAC, CREEC Peru SAC, and the 

China Railway Eryuan Engineering Group Ltd. 

The San Juan de Marcona –Andahuaylas train (estimated investment US$9.2 billion) is a 920 km long 

railroad: 577 km (main track) and 343 km (branch lines). This is an important railroad as it enables the 

modal interchange of cargo from road to rails, while going through the country’s prominent agricultural 

and mining areas . According to the schedule shown in its prefeasibility study, th e technical file on this 

project is expected to be completed by end 2023 and construction is to begin in early 2030. 

The passenger and cargo demand analysis for this project indicates that the railroad can transport 900 

passengers per day (mainly on the P isco -Ayacucho route) as well as around 66 million Mt of cargo per year. 

The majority of the cargo would comprise mining products because of the route’s proximity to various 

mining projects in the department of Apurimac such as Antillas, Anubia, Cotabambas, Trapiche, and the 

expansion of Marcona. Finally, it is estimated that the train could benefit from US$14,000. 162   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

Figure F. 30 Railway rout e of the San Juan de Marcona –Andahuaylas Railway Project  

> Source: Directorate of Management of Infrastructure and Transport Services —MTC

Land transport 

As of 2021, the length of Peru’s existing road network was 175,589 km, of which 15 percent (27,046 

km) corresponds to national highways, the responsibility of the MTC; 15 percent (27,951 km) to 

depa rtmental highways, the responsibility of regional governments; and the remaining 66 percent 

(120,593 km) to local roads, the responsibility of local governments. 

According to the MTC’s National Highway System (SINAC), the National Road Network (NRN) is ma de 

up of 3 large longitudinal axes (coast, highland, and jungle) and 20 transverse axes, which connect the 

coast with the highland and jungle, interconnecting the Longitudinal National Road Network (MTC 

2016). The Departmental or Regional Road Network (DRN ) includes the most important regional routes 

that connect and link the capitals of the departments with the main cities in the region. Finally, the 

Local or Rural Road Network (NRV) connects the district capitals and populated centers with the 

provincial capital.

As regards the age of vehicles in t he passenger transport fleet, of a total of 17,346 vehicles operating 

nationally and internationally that were registered at the end of 2020, 46.8 percent were 1 –5 years 

old, 34.1 percent were 6 –10 years old, 15.5 percent were 11 –15 years old, 3.0 percent were 16 –21 

years old, and 0.6 percent were over 22 years. 

It is important to highlight that the number of vehicles between 1 and 10 years old increased from 75.4 

percent in 2011 to 80.9 percent in 2020. This increase was due to the implementation of Supre me 

Decree No. 015 -2017 -MTC of 2017 that modified the national regulation of transport administration, 

establishing 15 years as the maximum age for public transport vehicles at the national, regional, and 

provincial levels. 

Likewise, the greater costs assoc iated with an aging vehicle fleet are a result of greater fuel 

consumption, greater environmental pollution, less security , and longer travel times. To resolve these 

issues and to complement Supreme Decree No. 015 -2017 -MTC, Emergency Decree No. 029 -2019 wa s

published in 2019, approving the guidelines encouraging promotion of scrap. The regulatory framework 

promoted the definitive removal or renewal of vehicles from the fleet, in order to reduce GHG emissions 

and local pollutants that affect public health, t o reduce the accident rate on public roads, and to 

improve the land transportation system. 165   

> Country Climate and Development Report: Peru .Background note on transport

> Source: MTC

The strategic node of Lima -Callao is particular important , since it organizes the main distribution activity 

toward the south, north, and the central regions of Peru. The flows of goods —and the consolidated 

logistic relations —of the country are structured radially from the main ports, land borders, and the main 

centers of consumption. 

Between 2011 and 2020, the land cargo transport vehicle fleet registered an increase of 93.5 percent, 

with a total of 304,732 motor and nonmotor vehicles registered in 2020. However, it is important to 

highlight that, although ther e was a sustained increase from 2011 to 2018, reaching a maximum of 

315,670 vehicles, in the period 2018 to 2020 a drop of 10,938 vehicles was recorded. According to 

the MTC, the reduction was the result of better supervision by the Superintendency of Land Transport 

of People, Cargo and Goods (SUTRAN) that allowed an update of the register of both national and 

international cargo carriers, in compliance with D.S. No. 017 -2009 -MTC (Article 59) and Supreme 

Decree No. 006 -2010 -MTC, contributing to the formaliz ation of the service .168

Meanwhile, land cargo transport companies have been increasing progressively, reaching a total of 

114,913 companies by 2020, of which 98.9 percent were national companies (113,605) and 1.1 

percent international companies (1,308).

Of the 137,568 vehicles registered at the end of 2020, 27.9 percent were 1 –5 years old, 32.9 percent 

were 6 –10 years old, 13.9 percent were 11 –15 years old, 16.4 p ercent were 16 –30 years old, and 8.9 

percent were over 30 years. 

Comparing these figures with the percentage distribution in 2011 and 2016, we observe that the 

number of vehicles aged between 1 and 10 years increased in proportion, from 48.2 percent in 201 1

to 60.8 percent in 2020. But the proportion of vehicles older than 15 years decreased from 43.2 

percent in 2011 to 25.2 percent in 2020. It should be noted that 8.83 percent of vehicles in circulation 

were over 30 years old.

F.4.3. Mitigation measures in the freight transportation service 

The remarkable economic growth that Peru has experienced in recent decades has boosted the 

demand for transport in the country. According to our estimates, between 2011 and 2018, the total 169  

> Country Climate and Development Report: Xxxxx

tons of cargo transported increased from 72 million to 109 million Mt, a growth of 50 percent in 7 

years. This growth is directly reflected in the level of national exports that grew by 50 percent during 

the same period. The MTC, as the entity responsible for promoting transport infrastructure in the 

country, has strength ened it by prioritizing the transport of cargo according to the “product -market -

client” model .

The 2011 Transport Logistics Services Development Plan and the determination of the 57 most 

important logistics chains in Peru helped to focus public investments and highway concessions on their 

construction and maintenance. The MTC’s sectoral strategy has focused on responding to the needs of 

land transport through the modernization of its national and departmental road network to facilitate 

the transport of good s from the interior (mainly agricultural goods and minerals) to the ports located 

along the coast for exports. 

This priority of developing land transport can be justified by the cost -benefit analysis of land projects in 

comparison with other means of tran sport. For example, while investment in one kilometer of highway 

could cost US$1.5 million, a kilometer of railways could cost US$12.8 million , or the simple 

construction/modernization of a port (to promote cabotage) could mean an investment of US$155 

mill ion. Although land transport infrastructure was prioritized for years, the MTC is currently promoting 

its portfolio of projects according to the National Railway Plan and the concessions granted to ports in 

the country. 

Assuming that the development of lan d transport infrastructure is the main axis of cargo movement 

has driven the growth of the country’s vehicle fleet. According to MTC data, the number of transport 

vehicles increased from 157,000 to 304,000 vehicles (from 2011 to 2020), a growth of 93 perce nt. 

This increase in the cargo fleet and the kilometers traveled by these vehicles represent a greater 

consumption of fossil fuels, which is ultimately reflected in the GHG emissions from the energy sector 

in Peru. 

It should be noted that “energy” emissio ns include those from stationary combustion (power 

generation, from the combustion of fuels by extraction and use in manufacturing and construction 

industries) and mobile combustion (from the burning and evaporation of fuel for all transport activities). 

According to the INGEI 2016, stationary and mobile emissions have different calculation 

methodologies, so mobile combustion emissions almost exclusively represent emissions from the 

transport sector in the country. It is important to highlight that the ener gy sector, according to the INGEI 

2016, was the sector with the second -highest GHG emissions, registering a total of 58.13 MtCO 2eq .

On the other hand, mobile combustion emissions reached a total of 21.047 MtCO 2eq, which 

represented 10 percent of total GHG emissions at the country level. Additionally, land transport has the 

highest emissions (a total of 19.29 MtCO 2eq) among the different modes of transport, representing 

91.70 percent of emissions in the transport sector. These emissions results are closely r elated to what 

has been stated about the growth of the vehicle fleet and the tons transported by the land transport 

sector .170  

> Country Climate and Development Report: Xxxxx

Figure F.3 8 Evolution of vehicle fleet, land cargo metric tons, and emissions  

> Source: Original compilation based on MINAM and MTC data

Although Peru has committed to reaching a maximum level of 179.0 MtCO 2eq by 2030 in its NDCs, by 

following the emissions contribution structure of the last Sectoral Greenhouse Gas Report (RAGEI), 71 

we conclude that the transport sector w ould achieve savings of no more than 17.9 MtCO 2 in 2030. 

As described in section F. 2.3, the contributions proposed for 2030 by the MTC and MINEM include 14 

mitigation measures that cumulatively reach a total of 6.9 MtCO 2eq. The s aid commitment would 

repres ent 54 percent of emissions from the transport sector by 2030. 

In order to complement the government’s mitigation measures, this report includes the evaluation of 

three public policies that can help the MTC reduce emissions in freight transportation. 

It i s important to highlight that the estimates obtained in the present public policy analysis were made 

in accordance with the IPCC guidelines, which consider two levels of calculation: the first for carbon 

dioxide (CO 2) emissions and the second for methane (CH 4) and nitrous oxide (N 2O). Additionally, the 

calculation of CO 2 emissions (level 1) is obtained by multiplying the amount of fuel burned, according 

to sales reports, by the default CO 2 factor. 

The application of the methodology for calculating carbon d ioxide emissions is as follows 72 :

Thus, the input variable for calculating Level 1 emissions according to the IPCC is the amount of fuel 

sold and the emissions factor, which is d erived from the relationship between fuel and the number of 

registered and circulating vehicles. 

One of the main limitations when calculating emissions is that, although the MTC does have information 

on the vehicle fleet by type of vehicle and age of carg o vehicles, the Supervisory Agency for Investment 

in Energy and Mining (OSINERGMIN) only reports the total fuel consumption and does not relate it to 

the type of vehicle. 

Thus, in order to relate both variables (vehicles and fuel) specifically to cargo tr ansport, it was essential 

to identify the relationship s illustrated in figure F.3 9.

The three mitigation measures proposed in this document estimate emissions on the basis of the 

relationship between the cargo fleet and its annual fuel consumption capacity. However, in order to 

obtain more robust results, it is important that the MTC and the relevant regulatory entities request 

detailed data from the transportation companies on the fuel consumed per vehicle unit used. 

Regarding Component 1, the MTC records the total number of registered and authorized cargo transport 

vehicles, as well as the age of the vehicle fleet. However, and given that trucks and trailers represent 

70 percent of the total vehicle fleet, the analysis of the different policy scenarios was limited to the 

increase or reduction of this fleet. 73

freight transportation in Peru, interviews were conducted 75 with the main unions and associations of 

cargo carriers to estimate the average kilometers traveled, as well as the fuel consumption by age of 

the vehicle. The interviews showed that a truck a nd/or trailer can travel between 3,000 and 5,000 km 

per month. In this way, and in a conservative scenario, it was considered that the kilometers traveled 

by cargo transport in Peru were around 35,000 km per year.

measures, mobile fuel consumption was estimated based on the expected GHG emissions in a

Climate change mitigation measure recommend ation 1: Promote the intermodal exchange from land 

cargo to railway 

Due to its greater transport capacity, the railway is an efficient mode for the mass transport of goods 

over long distances, and represents lower transp ort costs (lower energy consumption) compared to 

land transport. Additionally, the railway can transport a load equivalent to 50 trucks, which consume 

3.5 times more fuel. According to various studies, with a liter of fuel, a ton of cargo can be transporte d

for 67 km by land, while by rail it would reach 127 km (MTC 2022) .

Currently, the railway network in Peru has a length of 1,940 km, of which 77 percent is under 

concession, 13 percent is private (mineral transfer), and the remaining 10 percent is under the 

responsibility of the MTC (Huancayo -Huancavelica Train). 77 

The railway infrastructure in Peru is inferior compared to countries in the region such as Chile, Bolivia, 

and Colombia, among others. However, the National Railway Plan , approved in 201 6, includes the 

promotion of new railway lines such as the construction of the local train system or the San Juan de 

Marcona –Andahuaylas train. 174  

> Country Climate and Development Report: Xxxxx

Currently, the San Juan de Marcona –Andahuaylas train takes on special relevance given that the 

prefeasibility s tudy has been approved and the construction schedule indicates that it could start 

operations in early 2030 . In addition, the train route runs through a mining and agricultural area which, 

according to the demand study of the prefeasibility study, estimate s that the train could transport 

around 180,000 tons per day, which would be equivalent to 5,000 truck trips along the route.

Climate change mitigation measure recommend ation 2: Promote efficient gas consumption through 

development of cargo truck centers 

In the past decade, the MTC has prioritized the development of the road infrastructure of the national 

and subnational ne twork through the construction and maintenance of key logistics chains. Although 

progress to date has been important (83 percent of the national road network is paved, at the 

departmental and neighborhood levels), the paved surface only constitutes 15 perc ent and 2 percent, 

respectively. In addition, the high logistics costs of cargo transport are not only limited to guaranteeing 

the good condition of roads, but also how the cargo can be distributed efficiently across strategic points. 

In this regard, the i nstallation of service centers or “truck centers” would help the distribution of goods 

and , therefore, transport companies could reduce their kilometers traveled per vehicle unit .

Climate change mitigation measure recommend ation 3: Promote the intermodal exchange from land 

cargo to cabotage 

Given the greater cargo capacity of container ships, 83 the cabotage of goods, due to Peru’s geographical 

conditions (more than 2,400 km bet ween its extremes Tacna and Tumbes), is considered an efficient 

alternative for cargo transportation compared to ground transportation by road . The economies of scale 

that cabotage offer allow for greater economic savings in logistics, mainly the use of fu el, which 

ultimately contributes = to the amount of GHGs that the transportation sector produces annually.

transport of goods by container , widely used for the transport of dry cargo , especially in maritime trade ); b ulk cargo vessels or bulk carriers designed for 

the transport of solid bulk cargoes, such as minerals, all types of grains, and construction materials, such as cement, among others ; r oll on –roll off , used 

for transporting all types of goods on wheels, such as cars, vans, and indus trial vans, loaded trucks for long -distance routes, etc ; o il tankers (l arge 

tankers transport ing crude oil and petroleum products that moor at high sea due to their large dimensions); r eefer ships (v essels for transporting goods

Although different studies indicate that cabotage is more cost efficient than ground transportation, 84 

perhaps one of the greatest benefits of mariti me cargo transportation is that it is a viable alternative to 

quickly connect the different regions of the country when road infrastructure services (national or 

departmental) are interrupted due to events such as natural disasters and blockades, among oth ers. 

For example, in 2017, the ravages of the El Niño Costero phenomenon caused landslides of mud and 

rock, floods, and collapses that ultimately severely affected public infrastructure, harming thousands 

of people, and putting both their lives and health at risk. The most affected departments were La 

Libertad, Piura, and Lambayeque, where the movement of people and goods was interrupted for several 

days, limiting the timely delivery of aid, and generating a shortage of basic goods due to the impossibility 

of ground transportation to reach the affected areas. 

Table 12 Damage to road infrastructure during the El Niño Costero phenomenon              

> Department Roads Destroyed
> (km)
> Roads Affected
> (km)
> Bridges Destroyed
> La Libertad 1,038 701 38
> Piura 345 1,032 27
> Lambayeque 98 122 63
> Source: MTC -INDECI

For this reason, the promotion of maritime cabotage is of vital importance for the economic 

development and commercial security of the country. Thus, to boost the domestic cabotage market, in 

recent years the MTC, as the governing body of the sector, has been modifying regulations to make the 

requirements requested to shipping companies and cargo operators more flexible and to make this 

means of transportation more competitive . Among such efforts, it is worth mentioning that in 2018, 

Legislative Decree 1413 was approved by which national and foreign flag vessels are now allowed to 

perform cabotage service. 85 However, despite a more competitive regulatory framework, the results 

have no t yet been as expected. During the period between 2019 and 2021, cabotage barely registered 

the mobilization of 423 containers and 11,400 MT, representing less than 0.05 percent of the total 

national cargo mobilized through the road network along the Pan -American Highway in those years. 

There are several factors associated with the lack of increased demand for domestic cargo maritime 

transportation :

• Port reception, storage, and shipping costs are high. Vessels that are allowed to engage in 

cabotage do not h ave “preferential attention” or differentiated rates compared to import and 

export cargo. In addition, ground cargo must go through a deconsolidation process, 86 which 

increases loading and unloading costs. 

• The time it takes to move cargo by cabotage could be up to five times longer than for ground 

transportation (157 hours vs. 29 hours). 

• Lack of regular frequencies of cabotage shipping companies. 

• Lack of a greater number of cargo logistics operators.       

> 84 According to a master’s thesis conducted at the Universidad del Pacífico, “Análisis del Cabotaje Marítimo como alternativa de transporte de carga
> multimodal en la c adena de suministro del Perú” (Analysis of Maritime Cabotage as a multimodal cargo transportation alternative in the Peruvian supply
> chain), it is estimated that cabotage in Peru is profitable for port -to -port cargo movement distances greater than 500 km a nd that in terms of cost, cabotage
> is 25% cheaper on average than truck transportation.
> 85 Until the enactment of Legislative Decree No. 1413 in September 2018, the only possibility to move goods (general cargo and c ontainers) by sea from
> one domestic port to another domestic port was through authorized domestic vessels.
> 86 It consists of the sa fe removal of the packages from the interior of an import container, separating the cargo for direct delivery by truck or the ir entry
> into the warehouses for later dispatch. 180
> Country Climate and Development Report: Xxxxx

Despite the great potential benefits, cabotage in Peru has not yet consolidated a captive market, largely 

due to the factors mentioned above. For example, if we compare the transportation of a 25 -ton 

container of “wire coils” between the cities of Ilo and Callao (Semino, Berrospi, and Akimoto 2020), 

cabotage registers savings of up to 25 percent compared to moving the cargo by truck (US$1,121 vs. 

US$1,500). However, the transportation time is five times longer than the ground transportation time 

(157 vs. 29 hours). In addition, if we look at the cost structure of cabotage (see figure F. 41 ), 35 percent 

of the cost is explained by the costs related to unloading, storage, and deconsolidation of cargo. 

Figure F. 41 Cost structure of Callao -Ilo cabotage  

> Source: Original compilation

However, our estimates indicate that comparing only the cost of port -to -port transfer, without 

considering the costs associated with cargo service, single -container cabotage is still more efficient by 

27 percent compared to transporta tion by truck 87 (US$460 vs. US$633).

mobilized. In the case of mining cargo, more than 90 percent of this cargo is moved in bulk or “ore 

concentrate” form by means of bulk carriers that depart from and arrive at regional ports on their way

abroad. Thus, by 2030, nonmineralized cargo transported by road is estimated at 153 million MT, which

To evaluate the potential for cargo transport by maritime cabotag e, it was evaluated to promote the 

development of the logistic chains of the main regional ports that are at least 500 km from Callao and 

that currently already have significant cargo movements of different nonmineral products. Therefore, 

the ports with gr eater conditions to promote cabotage would be 88 : (1) Port of Paita (Piura), (2) Port of 

Salaverry (La Libertad), (3) Port of Matarani (Arequipa), and (4) Port of Ilo (Moquegua). 

Likewise, the main routes used by ground cargo operators to the main cities in northern and southern 

Peru 89 and their proximity to the selected ports were identified. Thus, the list of ports and main cities 

through which cargo could be moved is as follows: 

Port of Paita: Tumbes, Talara, Paita, Sullana, Piura, Sechura

Port of Ilo: Ilo, Moquegua, Tacna, Desaguadero 

According to the origin -destination matrix of the MTC’s Cargo and Passenger Demand Study, it is 

expected that by 2030 the total amount of ground cargo moving along the North and South Pan -

American Highway will amount to 21 million MT, representing about 14 percent of the total national 

cargo of nonmineral products.

Of the total nonmineral national cargo, it is estimated that 6 percent of it travels north of Lima , and 

between 600 and 1,000 km depending on the destination city, while 8 percent of the cargo going south 

travels at least between 900 a nd 100 km. Thus, the potential demand that can be promoted by 

cabotage through the mentioned ports could reach a maximum of 21.9 million MT in 2030.     

> 88 The importance and relevance of cabotage for these ports was validated through interviews with shipping operators, transporta tion unions, experts, etc. .
> 89 Interviews with transportation unions. 182
> Country Climate and Development Report: Xxxxx

Despite efforts to make the regulatory framework more flexible, cabotage cargo continues to register 

almos t zero movements compared to the potential of ground cargo that can be captured along the 

Peruvian coastal ports. Likewise, and although today the regulation allows not only national flag 

shipping companies but also foreign ones to carry out cabotage servi ces, the results obtained suggest 

that the limitations are not necessarily due to the supply of the fleet but are mainly based on the 

demand of transport companies and logistics operators. 

However, it is important to determine the capacity of the domestic and foreign fleet in a scenario where 

the costs and times of cabotage are very competitive in comparison to ground transportation, and 

where there is an intermodal migration from truck to ship. 

For the following analysis and given that mineral ca rgo is currently being handled by bulk carriers as 

well as the hydrocarbon sector by oil tankers in the selected regional ports, the potential for the transfer 

of goods by containers has been considered as cabotage. Likewise, it is important to highlight t hat the 

potential fleet for cabotage includes the national flag vessels that are currently transporting goods, but 

also includes those vessels that arrive both in the north and south of the country and that have the 

draft and length capacity to load and un load in the selected regional ports, but that are currently served 

in the Port of Callao.

According to information provided by the National Port Authority ( Autoridad Portuaria Nacional, APN), 

and considering only vessels with a capacity of up to 8,000 TEU, in 2021 a total of 947 vessels were 

registered, of which 67 percent performed operations according to origin -destination to the north of 

Lima and the remai ning to the south of Lima. For example, that in 2021 there were 308 vessels coming 

from Chile (or heading to) that could potentially bring cargo from Ilo and Matarani to Lima or vice versa. 

However, interviews to different shipping operators and logistics agents indicated that on “average” 

container ships coming from the north could have between 10 percent to 15 percent of capacity for 

cabotage, while those coming from Chile could have only 5 to 10 percent. 183

In consequence, our fleet analysis indicates that with the national and international fleet in 2021, as 

well as the frequency of container ships entering and leaving Peru through the port of Callao, it is 

possible to provide cabotage service to the maximum potential cargo that would be transported by 

2030 along the Pan -American Highway , both north and south. 

Regarding programmed investments in the four selecte d ports, three of them (Matarani, Paita, and 

Salaverry) are under concession and have approved investment plans, and many of the expansions or 

complementary works are currently underway (table F.33 ). Only the port of Ilo is still under government 

administr ation , and there are plans for modernization, including a new multipurpose terminal. The 

expansion of the port infrastructure will allow a greater speed of service to vessels that could undertake 

cabotage, which added to the coordination of the MTC with re spect to simplification of procedures can 

promote a greater competitiveness of cabotage over ground transportation.

In addition to the improvement of port infrastructure, it is essential to rapidly improve procurement 

related to cargo services. Likewise, as the MTC has stated the need for truck centers to improve ground 

transportation, the development of outer harbors wo uld help to order and make the entry of trucks to 

regional ports more efficient, reduce the use of fuel by trucks , and provide parking spaces for carriers. 

The outer harbors would be integrated with the port terminal management systems and would provide 

co mplementary services to carriers and cargo vehicles. 

Among the benefits for carriers are the following: 

• Reduction of cost overruns due to delays that affect vehicle turnover and productivity 

• Availability of complementary services such as restoration, washing and basic maintenance of 

vehicles, gasoline, banking services, rest and toilet facilities, primary health care, and so on 

• Cargo security 184  

> Country Climate and Development Report: Xxxxx

For logistics operators: 

• Opportunity to have a parking area for recurring customers and those req uiring long -term 

parking, allowing for more space to organize the operations of the company’s own fleets. 

• Orderly traffic and lower cost overruns due to higher fleet turnover. 

• Better and more reliable service planning.

As can be seen in table F.36, the promotion of cabotage services would reduce land freight transport 

emissions by 2030 by between 0.069 MtCO 2 (optimistic) and 0.023 MtCO 2 (pessimistic). These 

emissions savings would represent an advance of between 3 percent and 1 percent of the target to be 

achieved by the land freight transport sector according to the gap to be filled by 2030 of 2.32 MtCO 2.

Compared to Proposed Mitigation Measures 1 and 2, cabotage does not have a major impact on 

reducing emissions, given that according to the origin -destination matrix developed by the MTC in 2011, 

the cargo moving along the Pan -American Highway and the ports selected in this analysis represent 

barely 14 percent of the total cargo of nonmineral products transported annually at the national level. 186  

> Country Climate and Development Report: Xxxxx

Box F.1 Cleaner and More Efficient Fleet through Fleet Renewal 

Although the MTC has already identified a measure to mitigate vehicle scrapping, the measure does not yet 

have an implementation plan or incentive policies for owners. 

As an exercise of this document, a proposal for the renovation of the cargo fleet was d eveloped, in which 

through the reduction of import tariffs, but above all through financing for the purchase of new cargo 

vehicles, the age distribution of the fleet is reduced in percentages of 60 percent, 40 percent, and 20 

percent. 

Fleet renewal with n ewer trucks/trailers has the potential to reduce CO2 emissions because performance 

improves as the units are newer. The lower the fuel consumption per kilometer, the lower the CO2 emissions. 

As a result of the renewal of the fleet and the better fuel cons umption of the newer vehicles, the fuel 

consumption by 2030 would be reduced in relation to the BAU between a minimum value of 0.09 MtCO2 

and a maximum of 0.27 MtCO2.

## F.5. Proposed government actions 

As described in section F. 2.3 of this document, the transportation sector emissions gap between the 

BAU and the NDCs would indicate that by 2030 the transportation sector should have the capacity to 

reduce its emissions by at least 12.23 MtCO 2 in order to meet its emission reduction commitments. 

Likewise, it is important to highlight that the 14 mitigation measures identified by the MT C and MINEM 

(see section F. 2.3) represent an advance of 6.9 MtCO 2 of reduction of this gap, with other mitigation 

measures still to be identified to help the sector reduce the 5.3 MtCO 2 that are still pending. 

This document focuses on the evaluation of mit igation measures in land freight transportation, which 

according to the RAGEI as of 2016 represents 19 percent of the total emissions produced by the 

transportation sector. The three mitigation measures evaluated present the following emissions 

reduction c apacity by 2030: 

• Marcona –Andahuaylas Train Construction: Between 0.366 and 0.220 MtCO2 

• Truck centers’ implementation: Between 0.567 and 0.113 MtCO 2

• Promotion of cabotage: Between 0.069 and 0.023 MtCO 2

## F.6. Conclusions and key recommendations 

Since the early 1990s, when the IPCC presented its first report on the effects of climate change on the 

earth’s temperature, the Peruvian government has actively participated to include an adequate 

institutional framework to develop a goo d natural disaster response system and include climate change 

adaptation and mitigation policies on the national agenda. 

Regarding natural disaster risk response, it is important to continue strengthening the preventive and 

territorial approach to disaste r risk management and the development of a PSGRD implementation 

strategy, which are the main pending tasks. Although the MTC has made significant progress in 

developing reactive management plans, progress in preventive and corrective management is still 

in complete. Likewise, although there are protocols for reactive management of the MTC dependencies, 

the agents of the territory are only considered as information agents, lacking interinstitutional and 

intergovernmental articulation. In this regard, the MTC has been developing a new model : decentralized 

offices for coordination and management among regional and local governments . Monitoring and 

evaluation of the PSGRD implementation strategy are recommended to strengthen DRM. 

In relation to Peru’s institution al organization regarding matters of climate change, although MINAM is 

central in the management of policies and guidelines, the implementation of proposals depends on 

sectoral agenda s. In 2018 , with the publication of Law No. 30764 —the Climate Change Law —a

multisectoral competences framework was created where by each public entity has the obligation to 

report its specific adaptation and mitigation actions . Yet little progress has been made regarding 

adaptation within the ministries to strengthen the areas in charge of complying with the national climate 192  

> Country Climate and Development Report: Xxxxx

change policy. According to the new integrated text of the MTC’s Regulation of Organization and 

Functions (ROF) , approved in 2019 , the duty falls to the General Directorate of Environmental Affairs 

(DGAAM) . But that entity’s capacity to dictate guidelines, processes, or protocols related to climate 

change is low. Likewise, and given that the MTC already has a Disaster Risk Management Working 

Group (GTGRD), with the participation of all the line dire ctorates of the ministry, it is recommended that 

it be the same group that includes the management of climate change adaptation and mitigation 

measures, with the DGAAM as coordinator. This would allow the MTC to better manage climate actions 

and DRM from t he institutional side , across the nation. 

In the High -Level Commission on Climate Change (CNCC), subnational government stakeholders raised 

concerns about including the MTC in promoting both mitigation and adaptation measures . Although to 

date these measur es have not been adopted , it is highly possible that in the coming years the MTC will 

have to include adaptation policies in its transportation infrastructure. 

The 14 mitigation measures committed to by the MTC and MINEM represent a reduction of 6.9 MtCO 2,

or 54 percent of Peru’s total reduction goals by 2030 (119.3 MtCO 2) compared to the BAU (298.3 

MtCO 2). The present study indicates that the mitigation measures of the MTC are insufficient in the 

effort to meet the GHG target emissions. Both the INGE I and RAGEI 2016 indicate that the transport 

sector contributes 10 percent of total emissions at the country level. Just 8 years from 2030, there are 

no significant investment projects to achieve 0 percent emissions. Therefore, it is recommended that 

by 20 30, the transport sector should reduce its emissions by at least 12.23 MtCO 2.

Despite the great benefits that electromobility presents for the reduction of GHG emissions, the 

promotion of initiatives and policies for the electrification of the automotive fleet has been very poor 

compared to what is being advanced by other countries in the region. While countries such as Chile, 

Colombia, and Mexico, among others, began to adapt their regulatory framework to implement 

incentives for the acquisition, circulat ion, and operation of electric and hybrid vehicles more than a 

decade ago, in Peru, only since 2018 have tax incentives sought to increase the purchase and sale of 

cleaner energy vehicles. There were only 39 battery -powered electric vehicles in Peru as of 2020. The 

MTC aims for 6,700 buses and 171 ,300 light electric vehicles to be circulating in 2030, which would 

represent 5 percent of the vehicle fleet at the national level. Thus, it is essentia l that in the coming 

years the MTC be able to promote a more competitive regulatory framework with clear incentives for 

the acquisition and operation of electric vehicles in the country. It is also important to coordinate with 

MINEM and MINAM in the develo pment of electric charging infrastructure. 

Ninety percent of emissions from the transport sector in Peru are due to land transport of cargo and 

passengers. In recent decades, the focus has been on strengthening the construction and maintenance 

of the natio nal and departmental road network throughout the country. But land transport uses internal 

combustion vehicles that, when burning fuel, produce more than 90 percent of the GHG emissions in 

the sector. Although the MTC is working on the implementation of ma ssive urban transport projects 

such as train lines (Lines 3 and 4) and a more efficient bus corridor system, it is essential to focus 

investment on cargo transport as well. 

Maritime cabotage promises great benefits such as increased capacity to transport cargo, a reduction 

in logistics costs, and more predictable scheduling of operations. For years, commercial water transport 

was reserved exclusively for Peruvian -flagged merchant ships . This limited the growth of the national 

shipping fleet. Although since 2018 the MTC has made various efforts to promote the development of the 

cabotage market , results are not promising . It is important to expand the cargo -carrying capacity of the 

national fleet and related cargo agencies , which promise savings relative to l and transport. 193  

> Country Climate and Development Report: Xxxxx

Accelerating the construction of the San Juan de Marcona –Andahuaylas train, the implementation of 

truck center s, as well as promoting maritime cabotage could help reduce GHG emissions in the sector 

by up to 0.36 MtCO 2 by 2030. Given that car go transport exclusively uses internal combustion vehicles 

that use diesel as their energy source, policies that help reduce the kilometers traveled and improve 

the fuel efficiency of cargo vehicles would have a rapid impact on emissions. The implementation and 

commissioning of the San Juan de Marcona –Andahuaylas train in the coming years (2029 –30) could 

contribute to a reduction between 0.366 and 0.22 MtCO 2. The construction of truck centers will allow 

cargo to be transported more efficiently from distribution centers, which will reduce the kilometers 

traveled by cargo trucks, and reduce GHG emissions between 0.56 and 0.11 MtCO 2. Finally, the 

promotion of maritime cabotage could also contribute up to 0.06 –0.02 MtCO 2 of emissions reductions .

In the past decade, thanks to several free trade agreements, Peru has experienced sustained growth 

in exports of nontraditional goods, mainly agricultural products such as avocado s, grapes, and 

blueberries. This greater commercial openness has boosted prod uction, strengthening and promoting 

new regional crop areas as well as distribution centers along the country’s coast. In order to identify 

development poles and their logistic s chains, it is important that the MTC updates the cargo and 

passenger demand st udies carried out in 2011. 91 

Projects promoting electromobility or the use of clean fuels promise the greatest impact on CO 2

emissions. Therefore, it is important that both the MTC and MINEM prioritize their efforts to promote 

an adequate regulatory framew ork as well as infrastructure projects that allow a change in the energy 

matrix of the national vehicle fleet.
